By incorporating Views within the ArchiCode workspace and meticulously associating them with relevant Viewpoints and Layers, you harness a powerful tool for effectively communicating architectural insights. As we continue our exploration of ArchiCode, you’ll delve further into the mechanics of Views and their pivotal role in conveying the depth and breadth of your architectural design.

Views

ArchiCode places a strong emphasis on the dynamic generation of views, fostering a dynamic and adaptable approach to architectural visualization. Here’s how ArchiCode leverages this dynamic view generation:

  • Pluggable Viewpoint Service: ArchiCode harnesses a pluggable Viewpoint Service architecture, enabling the dynamic generation of views that seamlessly align with the elements of both the Application and Technology layers. This extensible framework empowers architects to tailor viewpoints according to their architectural needs.

  • Diverse Architectural Elements: With ArchiCode, the dynamic view generation process taps into the rich spectrum of architectural elements within the Application and Technology layers. This ensures that views are not only comprehensive but also finely tuned to convey the intricacies of your design.

  • Ad-Hoc View Definition: ArchiCode takes flexibility a step further by allowing architects to define ad-hoc views. This empowers you to create specialized viewpoints that cater to specific architectural inquiries or scenarios, providing an extra layer of customization.

The workspace defined a View representing an overview of the TerminalSession node
technology:
  elements:
    - kind: "environment"
      id: "Reference"
      elements:
        - kind: "node"
          id: "Laptop"
          elements:
            - kind: "node"
              id: "OperatingSystem"
              elements:
                - kind: "node"
                  id: "TerminalSession"
views:
  - kind: "view"
    id: "terminal-session"
    viewpoint: "overview"
    layer: "technology"
    properties:
      element: "Reference.Laptop.OperatingSystem.TerminalSession"

A view that has an id is a named view. Without --view, archicode views generate draws the views it finds from the elements of the model (a named view is drawn when it is asked for); archicode views generate --view terminal-session (or -i) draws the views of that id alone, no other view with them, into views/plantuml/technology/terminal-session.puml. A view can be a manifest of its own, in the folders of the manifests (see Stories); it belongs to no plateau, see [plateau-untied].

The map. A view of the map viewpoint draws a whole layer instead of the surroundings of one element: layer: application (the default) or layer: technology, folded, so that only the top of the layer shows and a link joins the boxes its two ends land on. Three lists of references, in properties, shape it: pinned keeps only what touches these elements, hidden leaves these elements, what is inside them and their relationships out, and opened opens these boxes, so that their children show. A map of the technology layer has two more properties, booleans, that are true when they are absent: applications: false leaves out the applications deployed on the nodes, and relationships: false leaves out the links (see below). The layer of a view (and of a step that carries its own view) is read in any case by both tools: layer: Technology and layer: TECHNOLOGY are the technology layer, and anything else is the application layer; application and technology in lower case remain the form to write. The editor draws the same view on its page Applications (the application layer) and its page Environments (the technology layer, see Applications and Environments: the Map), and both tools draw the same boxes (the example below has the boxes of each view pinned by a test). The command line draws a map when it is asked for it by its id, as for any named view:

The content of .archicode.yaml of the example domain_technology_map: three maps of a technology layer
settings:
  name: "Datacentre"
views:
  - kind: "view"
    id: "datacentre-map"
    name: "The datacentre, folded"
    viewpoint: "map"
    layer: "technology"
  - kind: "view"
    id: "datacentre-map-open"
    name: "The datacentre, opened down to the racks"
    viewpoint: "map"
    layer: "technology"
    properties:
      opened:
        - "datacentre"
        - "datacentre.edge"
        - "datacentre.rack"
  - kind: "view"
    id: "datacentre-map-database"
    name: "What touches the database, without the edge"
    viewpoint: "map"
    layer: "technology"
    properties:
      pinned:
        - "datacentre.rack.db"
      hidden:
        - "datacentre.edge"
archicode views generate --view datacentre-map-open

writes views/plantuml/technology/datacentre-map-open.puml. The command line does not read what the editor keeps in a map besides the three lists, the two booleans and links (facet, hide-unlinked and the texts of the diagram; see [map-links] for links). A list that names an element that does not exist is one error: line that names the view (error: the view datacentre-map-database: dangling reference: datacentre.rack.dbx); in the views of a facet, where the facet may have removed the element, the entry is dropped. A comparison of two plateaus gives a map the states of its boxes, as it does for the other viewpoints.

Choosing the relationships a map draws. A map of the application layer can name the relationships it draws, with a fourth property, links. Each entry is a mapping with a source, a destination and, optionally, a label:

properties:
  links:
    - source: customer
      destination: shop.web
      label: uses
    - source: shop.web
      destination: shop.catalog

The map then draws these relationships, the boxes of their two ends and what encloses them, and nothing else of the layer. pinned, hidden and opened keep their meaning: hidden still leaves out a relationship of or towards a hidden element, pinned still keeps the relationships that touch a pinned element (the pinned elements are boxes too), and an end inside a closed box lands on that box. A links that is absent, empty or has nothing after it draws the whole map, as before.

An entry names a relationship by what the source element declares: the destination and the label must be equal to the ones declared (the references are exact, an entry does not select what is inside the source). An entry with no label names the relationships that have no label; it is not a wildcard. Relationships that have the same source, destination and label and differ only by their qualifiers or tags cannot be told apart by an entry, so one entry draws them all. An entry of another shape (not a mapping, no text source or destination, a label that is not text, another key) is one error: line that says where it is, and so is an entry that names no relationship, with nothing written for the view:

error: the view orders_map: `links[1]` names a relationship from `shop.web` to `shop.catalog` with the label `reads`, which does not exist

(with no label when the entry has none; an unknown source or destination is dangling reference as for pinned.) The map of the technology layer reads no links and refuses them. The command line and the editor read links: the editor lists the relationships of the layer under the tree of its page Applications and draws the ticked ones (see [editor-map-links]).

In a workspace of plateaus, the entry is left out when the plateau drawn does not have the relationship, with warning: the view `m is drawn without the link a → b, which the plateau p does not have`, and the view is skipped, with warning: skip `m: none of its links is in the plateau p`, when none of its entries is there (see [plateau-views-lacking]). A typo in an entry is therefore only a warning in a workspace of plateaus, where it is an error in a workspace without. A relationship that only the plateau compared with has can be named in a comparison: it is drawn as removed.

Highlighting a relationship. A map of the application layer can also point at relationships among the ones it draws, with a fifth property, highlight. It is a list of the same entries as links (a source, a destination and, optionally, a label), matched the same way (exact references, a missing label names only the relationships that have none):

properties:
  links:
    - source: customer
      destination: shop.web
    - source: shop.web
      destination: shop.catalog
      label: reads
  highlight:
    - source: shop.web
      destination: shop.catalog
      label: reads

The highlight changes how links are drawn and nothing else: it adds no box and no link, and highlight without links points at links of the whole map. In PlantUML the highlighted link is the heaviest line of the view, in a vivid orange, with a black triangle before its label and the label in bold, so that the colour is never the only sign. As soon as one link is highlighted, the other links are drawn calm: thin, light grey, with a grey label. A link that carries a state of a comparison (new, changed, removed) keeps its colour and its dash and only takes the weight and the triangle when it is highlighted, and it is not made calm when it is not. A link that stands for several relationships (a grouped link, or one that lands on a closed box) is highlighted when one of them is. A view with no highlighted link is drawn as it always was, whatever the shape of its highlight: an entry that names a relationship the map leaves out (hidden, inside one closed box, or left out by only changes) marks nothing and is not an error.

The shape of an entry and the errors are the ones of links, with highlight[n] in the sentence, and a missing entry is error: the view orders_map: `highlight[1] names a relationship from shop.web to shop.catalog with the label reads, which does not exist`. The map of the technology layer refuses highlight, and the other viewpoints (overview, detailed, deep) ignore it. In a workspace of plateaus, an entry the plateau drawn does not have is left out with warning: the view `m is drawn without the highlight a → b, which the plateau p does not have`; unlike links, the view is never skipped for it, since a highlight is not what the view is about. In a comparison an entry may name a relationship that only the plateau compared with had. The command line reads highlight (in views generate and stories generate), and so does the editor: it draws the same heavy link and calm links on its Applications page, in a saved view and in a story step, and plays them in the story player (see [editor-map-links], Stories: stories and views and [editor-story-player]). It writes highlight sorted, without a duplicate and after links, and follows the references in it when an element is renamed or moved.

What a map of the technology layer draws. Besides the nodes and the environments, it draws the applications deployed on the nodes and the relationships:

  • The applications are the applications that the nodes list, one box each, inside its node. Only an open node shows them (a node is open when it is in opened, or when it encloses a pinned element), and a node that is hidden shows none; when the map is pinned, they are drawn only inside what it is pinned on. A node that hosts applications can be opened, so it is no longer drawn as a closed box with nothing in it; an application box has nothing to open. A pinned, hidden or opened entry names a technology element: an application is not pinned, hidden or opened on its own (hide its node, or leave the applications out with applications: false).

  • The relationships are the ones the elements of the layer declare, and the dependencies between environments that the deployed applications give: an application of one environment that depends on an application the environment does not host, found in an environment or a node that the first uses, is a link between the two application boxes when both are drawn, and between the boxes of the nodes that host them otherwise. These are the dependencies that the overview, detailed and deep viewpoints draw; the dependencies inside one environment stay out, as in those viewpoints. uses is a scope and no link of its own. The dependency is read from the relationships of the application that is deployed and from the ones of everything inside it (a relationship of a component of a deployed container counts); a relationship that ends inside the same application gives nothing. The destination is read the same way: a relationship that ends on a component of a deployed container is a dependency towards the node that hosts the container, and the destination must be a deployed application or inside one (see Using other environments).

A saved map of the technology layer that says neither property draws both, so that a saved map of the technology layer draws more than it did in 0.14.0, where it drew neither the applications nor the dependencies between the environments. The properties are applications and relationships, false to leave one out; any other value is no choice, in both tools. They mean nothing in a map of the application layer. A comparison of two plateaus gives the states to the applications as it gives them to the other boxes.

A view in a workspace of plateaus. A view is read against one plateau at a time. A name that the plateau drawn does not have, and another plateau has, is not a mistake of the view, and the two tools say the same: an entry of pinned, opened or hidden is left out of the map (warning: the view `m is drawn without x, which the plateau p does not have`), and a view that cannot be drawn without the name (the element of an overview, a map none of whose pinned elements is there, or none of whose links is there: see [map-links]) cannot be drawn in that plateau. archicode views generate --view <id> skips such a view with a warning, and does not fail, since asking for a view in every plateau of a loop is legitimate:

warning: skip `x`: it names `shop.mailer`, which the plateau `to-be` does not have

The editor says it in the view (The element “x” is not in the plateau “p”.). A name that exists in no plateau is the error of [plateau-untied].

A shipped example. The layered Brightcart of the repository (src/test/resources/brightcart/layered, a fixture of the tests, not an example of this manual) keeps seven views and three stories in a folder, shared/, that no plateau owns, so that these cases are checked on it. shop_overview is an overview of shop, which the plateau marketplace renames storefront: archicode views generate --plateau marketplace -i shop_overview skips it with the warning above, and the three other plateaus draw it. marketplace_overview is drawn in one plateau only, and applications_map is drawn everywhere, without shop in marketplace, with a warning. A view is drawn by views generate only when it is named with -i; the plain command draws the built-in views alone.

A view whose element a facet removes. A view you ask for by its id (views generate --view <id>) that is about an element that a facet removes cannot be drawn in that facet. The command does not skip it, since you asked for it by name: it stops with one line that names the view, the element and the facet, exits with the code 1 and writes nothing, not even the folders of the facets before it. The check is made when --view is given, in the facets of the plateau and in those of its comparison, before the first view is drawn:

error: the view `saas_probe` cannot be drawn: it names `commerce-saas`, which the facet `no-externals` removes

The line is the one of archicode views generate --plateau saas -i saas_probe on a copy of the layered Brightcart with a view saas_probe, an overview of commerce-saas, which the facet no-externals of that workspace removes (the first facet that does, in the order of the file; the facets of the plateau come before those of its comparison). Name the view in a workspace where the facet keeps its element, or leave it out of the command. The same holds in a workspace that has no plateau. Without --view nothing is checked, since the views drawn are made from the elements the facet still has; a map whose lists name an element that a facet removes draws it without that element and says nothing.

By embracing dynamic view generation, leveraging pluggable Viewpoint Services, and facilitating ad-hoc view definitions, ArchiCode equips architects with a toolkit for capturing, communicating, and exploring architectural insights in a dynamic and tailored manner. As we navigate deeper into ArchiCode’s capabilities, you’ll uncover the rendering of views and how they enhance your architectural workflow.

Stories

A story puts views in order, with a sentence for each, to tell how a part of the architecture works. It does not draw anything on its own: each step shows a view, and the editor draws that view live from the model, so a story stays true as the architecture moves.

Stories and views are manifests, like the elements: one file each, in the manifests folders, with the kinds archicode.morin.io/story and archicode.morin.io/view. The editor writes them for you (see Stories: stories and views), but they are plain YAML:

manifests/story.order_flow.yaml
header:
  kind: archicode.morin.io/story
  version: "1"
content:
  id: order_flow
  name: How an order travels
  description: From the shop to the payment provider.
  steps:
    - view: shop_overview          # a view that has a name: see below
      text: The shop receives the order.
    - viewpoint: map               # or a view written right in the step
      layer: application
      properties:
        pinned: [shop.payments]
        hidden: [mailer]
        opened: [shop]
        hide-unlinked: true
      text: It hands the payment over.
manifests/view.shop_overview.yaml
header:
  kind: archicode.morin.io/view
  version: "1"
content:
  id: shop_overview
  name: Shop — Overview
  viewpoint: overview     # overview, detailed or deep: drawn by the command line as well
  layer: application
  properties:
    element: shop
    facet: flow           # optional: the editor draws the view through that facet
    descriptions: true    # the texts of the diagram, when they differ from its default (see the editor)
    wrap: true

A step either names a view by its id, or carries one: a viewpoint, a layer and properties, as a view has. A named view can be used by several stories and generated by the command line (its id is what --view takes); a view written in a step is only for that step, and has no file of its own. The text is plain text. A step on a view that is not declared is reported as a warning by the command line, and shown as missing by the editor. The command line does not draw the stories (see below): it reads them and checks them, and it generates the views of the workspace as it always did, the views of the map viewpoint included (see [map-viewpoint]).

A story belongs to no plateau, like a view (see [plateau-untied]): it is the same story whatever plateau is drawn, and it is declared once in the whole workspace. A plateau: in its header is accepted and means nothing, as it does in a view (see [plateau-untied]).

A step told at a plateau. A step may say which plateau it is told at and which plateau it is compared with, with two optional keys, at and against, each the id of a plateau. They are not the plateau: of a header, which says that a manifest belongs to a plateau: here the story belongs to none and a step says where to look.

  • Without at, the step is told at the plateau the reader has drawn. Without against, nothing is compared. A step that names neither is drawn as the reader had it, the plateau and the comparison.

  • against without at is allowed: the step is told at the plateau drawn and compared with against (what changes from baseline to where I am). When against is the plateau drawn, nothing is compared, which is no mistake.

  • A key with nothing after it, or an empty text (at: ""), names nothing.

plateaus/story.migration.yaml of the example domain_plateaus
# A story belongs to no plateau: it is the same story whatever plateau is drawn. A step may say where it is told (`at`) and what it is
# compared with (`against`), with the id of a plateau; a step that says neither is drawn in the plateau the reader has drawn.
header:
  kind: "archicode.morin.io/story"
  version: "1"
content:
  id: "migration"
  name: "From the shop to the new shop"
  description: "What the new shop changes, one look at a time."
  steps:
    - viewpoint: "overview"
      layer: "application"
      properties:
        element: "shop"
      at: "baseline"
      text: "Today the shop is one system: orders, invoices and receipts, and an inventory of its own."
    - viewpoint: "overview"
      layer: "application"
      properties:
        element: "shop"
      at: "to-be"
      against: "baseline"
      text: "The same system next year, with what changed marked: orders are split in two, invoices and receipts are merged."
    - viewpoint: "detailed"
      layer: "application"
      properties:
        element: "warehouse"
      at: "to-be"
      text: "The inventory moved to the warehouse."
    - viewpoint: "overview"
      layer: "application"
      properties:
        element: "shop.web"
      against: "baseline"
      text: "The web site in the plateau that is drawn, compared with where we are today."

Both tools check the keys when the manifests are read, one sentence for each, with the story, the file and the number of the step (from 1), after the layers and before the names the step carries:

error: the story `migration` (`plateaus/story.migration.yaml`) step 1 says `at: tobe`, which is not a plateau of this workspace (the plateaus are `baseline`, `to-be`)
error: the story `migration` (`plateaus/story.migration.yaml`) step 2 says `at: to-be` and `against: to-be`: a plateau is never compared with itself
error: the story `s` (`manifests/story.s.yaml`) step 1 says `at: to-be` but the workspace declares no plateau (a `plateau` key in a header, or a manifest of kind `archicode.morin.io/plateau`)

The last one is told for a workspace that has no plateau too. The command line checks the steps when it loads the workspace, and draws them with archicode stories generate (see Draw the stories). The fork of the layered Brightcart ships a story that tells one (fork_tour: its steps are told at baseline, at saas and at inhouse, two plateaus that override baseline and do not stand on each other, and compared with baseline, with each other and with the plateaus marketplace stands on), next to order_journey, whose steps name no plateau, and seller_journey, whose steps are all told at marketplace. A name that the plateau of a step lacks is no mistake either: stories generate skips the step with a warning, and the editor, which plays the steps and draws their plateaus, tells it (see Stories: stories and views).

Workspaces written before stories became manifests keep their views in views: of .archicode.yaml, and may have a stories: there too: both are still read. The editor offers to move them to manifests, which keeps .archicode.yaml small and free of the rewrites an editor save makes to it.

A story of a flow. A story can show how something travels, one relationship more at each step, so that the reader sees where the flow has got to. Each step is a Map of the application layer with its own recipe: properties.links holds the relationships drawn so far, and the step’s highlight key names the newest one only, so that one link is heavy and the others are calm. A step adds one entry to links and moves highlight to it. The step’s key is the one place of its highlight: it replaces the highlight of a view, as above.

The layered Brightcart ships one, order_flow, with three steps at baseline: the path of an order from the customer to the payment provider, each step opening shop and shop.app so that the last one draws its link.

shared/story.order_flow.yaml of the layered Brightcart
header:
  kind: "archicode.morin.io/story"
  version: "1"
content:
  id: "order_flow"
  name: "An order, link by link"
  description: "The path of an order through the shop, one relationship more at each step, the newest one highlighted."
  steps:
    - viewpoint: "map"
      layer: "application"
      properties:
        opened: ["shop", "shop.app"]
        links:
          - source: "customer"
            destination: "shop.web"
            label: "browses and buys with"
      text: "The shopper browses and buys with the web storefront."
      at: "baseline"
      highlight:
        - source: "customer"
          destination: "shop.web"
          label: "browses and buys with"
    - viewpoint: "map"
      layer: "application"
      properties:
        opened: ["shop", "shop.app"]
        links:
          - source: "customer"
            destination: "shop.web"
            label: "browses and buys with"
          - source: "shop.web"
            destination: "shop.app"
            label: "calls"
      text: "The storefront calls the application."
      at: "baseline"
      highlight:
        - source: "shop.web"
          destination: "shop.app"
          label: "calls"
    - viewpoint: "map"
      layer: "application"
      properties:
        opened: ["shop", "shop.app"]
        links:
          - source: "customer"
            destination: "shop.web"
            label: "browses and buys with"
          - source: "shop.web"
            destination: "shop.app"
            label: "calls"
          - source: "shop.app.payments"
            destination: "psp"
            label: "authorises payments with"
      text: "And the payments component authorises the payment with the provider."
      at: "baseline"
      highlight:
        - source: "shop.app.payments"
          destination: "psp"
          label: "authorises payments with"

The editor writes the same form. On the Applications page, tick the relationships so far, highlight the newest one and choose Add to story…​: the new step carries the ticked relationships in properties.links and the highlight as its highlight key, and the field of the step edits that key. The editor writes the links and the highlight of a view or a step sorted by source, destination and label, without a duplicate, and highlight after links, so a file the editor saves reads the same whatever order the relationships were ticked in. Play plays the story in the player, one step at a time (see [editor-story-player]), and archicode stories generate --story order_flow draws one folder for each step, with the heavy link of each (see Draw the stories).

Rendering

ArchiCode introduces a systematic approach to rendering views, enriching architectural communication through visualization. Here’s how ArchiCode orchestrates the process of rendering views:

  • View Rendering Mechanism: Within ArchiCode, each View is rendered by a designated "Renderer." This integral component takes the architectural model and translates it into tangible artifacts that resonate with the end user, facilitating a comprehensive understanding of the design.

  • Renderer Definition: A "Renderer" within ArchiCode embodies a set of opinionated and pre-defined rules. These rules are specifically tailored to generate artifacts that eloquently present architectural insights, ensuring consistency and clarity in communication.

  • Pluggable Renderer Service: ArchiCode harnesses a pluggable Renderer Service architecture, enabling seamless integration and utilization of diverse renderers. This extensibility empowers architects to choose the most suitable renderer for the intended audience or purpose.

By introducing a robust rendering mechanism, employing opinionated rules for artifact generation, and facilitating the pluggable Renderer Service, ArchiCode enhances architectural communication by transforming abstract architectural concepts into tangible representations.

Stereotypes and styles

Every box of a diagram is drawn with a list of stereotypes. The stereotypes of an element are, in this order, its qualifiers (each one whole: Spring Boot is one stereotype) and the words of its rendering-stereotypes tag. The tag is a whitespace-separated list, so that external changed gives the two stereotypes external and changed, and a value of one word gives the one stereotype it always gave. A stereotype named twice counts once. The renderer then adds the kind of the element and the way the box is drawn. The command line and the editor read the stereotypes the same way: they come from the same kernel.

The styles of the workspace give a stereotype its look. styles.by-tags and styles.by-qualifiers map a stereotype to a style, and a box has the style of every stereotype it is drawn with. The key is the stereotype itself, not the name of a tag: the keys of by-tags are the words of rendering-stereotypes, the keys of by-qualifiers are the qualifiers. The two maps are equivalent for the renderer, which only sees stereotypes; use the one that says where the stereotype comes from. A style sets any of background-color, line-color, line-style, line-gap, line-thickness, round-corner, font-color, font-style and shadowing. line-style is the length of a dash (0 is a solid line) and line-gap the gap between two dashes: without line-gap the gap is as long as the dash, so that line-style: 5 is a dash of 5 and a gap of 5 and line-style: 16 with line-gap: 3 is long dashes with narrow gaps (PlantUML’s LineStyle: 16-3). A line-gap means nothing without a line-style; when a style overrides the line-style of another one, the gap of the other style goes with the dash it replaces.

Styling the boxes tagged external and changed
styles:
  by-tags:
    external:
      background-color: "lightgray"
    changed:
      line-color: "orangered"
      line-thickness: 3
  by-qualifiers:
    PostgreSQL:
      round-corner: 20
An element drawn with the external and the changed styles
content:
  id: "mailer"
  name: "Mail Provider"
  tags:
    rendering-stereotypes: "external changed"

In the editor. The editor draws a box with the style of the stereotypes it is drawn with: background-color is the fill, line-color, line-thickness, line-style and line-gap the outline (derived from the fill when only the fill is given, see below), and round-corner, font-color, font-style and shadowing the rest. A styled stereotype is also written on its box as a text badge with its name, so that a colour is never the only carrier. The same badge follows the name of the element in the Elements tree and in the tree of the Map, and in the header of an element (after its kind, on Details and on the diagram tabs; when the header is narrow it shows the badges that fit and the name keeps its room), and the key under the diagram shows the badge of each styled stereotype drawn. The badges are part of the SVG and PNG exported from a diagram, while the key is not; a badge is not the dot that marks an element as modified and not yet saved.

For the same stereotype, by-tags wins over by-qualifiers. When a box has several styled stereotypes, a later one overrides an earlier one property by property: with rendering-stereotypes: "external changed", the fill of changed wins, and the properties it does not set come from external. The order is that of the stereotypes of the box, its qualifiers first and then the words of the tag. The command line follows the same rule: it writes the stereotypes of a box last first, since PlantUML lets the first stereotype of a box win, property by property, whatever the order of the styles. The .puml of a box carrying external changed therefore says <<changed>> <<external>>, and the same workspace always gives the same file, byte for byte. The one difference: shadowing is a flag that is only ever switched on, so a style cannot switch off the shadow another style of the same box switched on (the editor lets shadowing: false of a later stereotype do it).

Colours are read as #rgb, #rrggbb, rgb(r, g, b), transparent or a CSS colour name such as lightgray; a value the editor cannot read is ignored. Numbers are whole numbers.

The fill is the colour written in styles, in both themes, as in the diagrams of the command line; styles has no separate dark colour. A box with a background-color and no line-color is outlined with a line derived from its fill: the same colour, darker (each of its channels at 60 %, so the hue is kept), or lighter when the fill is so dark that darkening it would show nothing, as for black. Without it the outline would be the grey of the theme, lost on the fill, and a box inside a box of the same fill, such as the systems of a solution that are both external, would vanish into it. The derived line is as firm on a box that contains others as on one that does not. The line-color of the style always wins, whatever the fill, and the fill itself is never changed; a box with no fill keeps the outline of the theme, and the outline of the box in focus or selected stays the accent colour. The same line is drawn on the badge of the stereotype, and in the SVG and PNG exported from a diagram. In the diagrams of the command line, PlantUML draws a box with the fill of its style and its own thin dark outline, so a box inside a box of the same fill is already told apart there: nothing is derived. The editor chooses the colour of the text so that it reads on that fill, black or white with a contrast ratio of at least 4.5: the font-color of the style is kept when it reads well, and replaced otherwise. Without a background-color, the box and its text keep the colours of the theme, and font-color is not used. Except that a box that contains others, with no background-color of its own, drawn inside one that has one, lets that fill show: its title is then black or white against that fill, in either theme, so that it never lands on a fill it cannot be read on.

Compared with the command line, line-style and round-corner are an approximation of what PlantUML draws. The editor styles only the stereotypes of an element, its qualifiers and the words of rendering-stereotypes: it does not use the kind of the element, nor atomic and composite, as keys, and styles.atomic and styles.composite are not drawn.

Technical Configuration

ArchiCode embraces configurability as a cornerstone of its design, affording you the ability to tailor the tool to align seamlessly with your architectural needs. Here’s a comprehensive look at ArchiCode’s configuration capabilities:

  • Opinionated Default Configuration: Out of the box, ArchiCode ships with an opinionated and carefully crafted default configuration. This configuration sets the tone for a seamless and intuitive architectural experience.

  • Workspace-Specific Customization: ArchiCode extends the flexibility of configuration to your workspace. The default configuration can be effortlessly customized, allowing you to fine-tune settings and preferences to match the unique demands of your architectural endeavor.

  • Manifest Localization: ArchiCode acknowledges the importance of manifest localization. This localization can be dynamically modified, enabling you to adapt and align the tool’s behavior with your project’s localization requirements.

  • Tailored View Generation: ArchiCode’s configurability extends to the very essence of view generation. You have the ability to redefine the output folder for generated views, as well as customize the labels utilized in the built-in viewpoints. This level of customization ensures that the generated views align harmoniously with your architectural narrative.

The settings also accept a name for the workspace (see Workspace) and the plateau that is current (see [plateau-choose]). They have no default, so the snippet below does not show them. There is no settings.plateaus: the plateaus are written in the manifests (see Plateaus).

The settings.views.properties key has one entry for each viewpoint (overview, detailed, deep and map), a mapping of properties for the views of that viewpoint. The editor reads one key of them, layout, the number of boxes per row its diagrams aim at (see The boxes per row). Settings edits the entries of overview, detailed and deep; the entry of map is written in the file, in Files. The command line reads none of it.

The default settings of a workspace
settings:
  manifests:
    paths:
    - "manifests"
  relationships:
    default-synthetic-label: "uses"
  views:
    path: "views"
  facets:
    global-enabled: true
    directory-name-template: "%s-%s"
The default configuration for styling
styles:
  composite:
    shadowing: false
    background-color: "transparent"
    line-color: "darkgray"
    line-style: 5
    font-style: "normal"
The default configuration for labels formatting
formatters:
  atomic:
    name: "%s"
    qualifiers: "%s"
    description: "%s"
  composite:
    name: "%s"
    qualifiers: "%s"
  link:
    label: "%s"
    qualifiers: "%s"

By affording you the capability to tailor the configuration of ArchiCode, the tool seamlessly integrates with your architectural process, providing a personalized and adaptable framework.

States of an architecture (plateaus)

An architecture is never finished: it is how it runs today, how it should run next year, and what the year after may bring. ArchiCode calls each of these states a plateau and tells you what changes from one to the next. This section is the story in one place, from the idea to a review of the changes; the rules are in Plateaus and Comparing two plateaus (in the Domain section), the commands in Choosing a plateau, Compare plateaus and Generate views, and the editor in The pages, The band of plateaus, The Plateaus page, Writing in a plateau and Comparing two plateaus in the editor. What does not work yet is in Limits of today.

Important
 Breaking changes. settings.plateaus, archicode plateau add and --baseline no longer exist: a plateau is a layer (see Plateaus, and Upgrading: what breaks, and what does not to convert a workspace by hand). A path of settings.manifests.paths may no longer contain .. nor be absolute, a reference declared twice is an error, and a plateau id is now lowercase letters, digits, _ and -. A workspace that has no plateau and does neither is read and drawn exactly as before.

The idea

A plateau is a layer: the architecture in that state is the plateau it overrides, plus the elements its own manifests add, changed by its patches. You write what differs and nothing is copied, so nothing has to be kept in sync by hand: when the root changes, every plateau above it changes with it, and a patch that no longer fits tells you so. The difference between two plateaus is computed from the references of the elements and the relationships, never typed: ArchiCode tells what is new, what changed and what was removed, and the views, the table of changes and the editor all draw the same answer.

One thing cannot be computed: that two elements are the same thing. An element that moves under another parent, or is renamed, has another reference, so it reads as one removed element and one new one. ArchiCode never guesses it; you say it, on the element of the more recent plateau, with replaces, and the comparison then reads a moved, split or merged element. It proposes the pairs that look like one thing said twice (the candidates) and applies none. The editor writes the hint when you accept a candidate.

The layout

The folder of the architecture holds one .archicode.yaml, with the settings, the styles and the facets that every plateau shares, the manifests of the root, and, for each other plateau, the manifests that say plateau: <id> in their header and a manifest of kind archicode.morin.io/plateau with its patches. The folders of the example domain_plateaus of this manual (src/doc/examples/domain_plateaus in the source repository) are drawn in Plateaus; the tutorial below builds it.

The settings in short

settings.manifests.paths

The folders the manifests are read from. A layered workspace ends a path with /** to read the folders of its plateaus too (see Manifests in folders).

settings.current

The plateau that is drawn when none is asked for; the last one by default when the plateaus form one line. It must be a plateau of the tree. The editor writes it too (see The current plateau), and opens the folder on it when nothing else says which plateau to draw.

settings.plateaus

No longer exists (see the note in Plateaus): a plateau is a plateau: header and a manifest of kind archicode.morin.io/plateau.

A path of manifests.paths is relative to the folder of .archicode.yaml, below it (no ..) and not absolute. Every rule is checked before anything is read, and each problem is reported. The details, with the messages, are in Plateaus.

The states

What a plateau changes is told with four states, computed, on elements and on relationships (the fourth is for boxes only), and three more for the elements that say what they replace (see Saying what an element replaces):

State Mark Means

new

+

the reference is only in the plateau

changed

~

the reference is in both and something differs: a property, or a relationship the element declares (an element whose description is the only difference is wording only)

removed

−

the reference is only in the other plateau, the one compared with

contains-changes

±

the element did not change by itself, but something inside it did

moved

→

the element says that it replaces one element of the other plateau (elements only)

split

⋔

the element says that it replaces an element that another element replaces too (elements only)

merged

⊕

the element says that it replaces several elements (elements only)

Each state has a mark, a line style and a legend, so that nothing relies on colour: see Comparing two plateaus for the command line and Comparing two plateaus in the editor for the editor.

The states and the tags you wrote by hand. The states are stereotypes of the boxes, the same ones that a styles.by-tags entry or a rendering-stereotypes tag names (see Stereotypes and styles). If you used to mark the boxes of a target by hand with rendering-stereotypes: "new" or "changed", nothing breaks, and the command line and the editor draw such a tag the same way. One rule decides it: a box gets the default look of a state (its mark, its outline, its tint, its struck title) only when the comparison computes that state for it.

  • A word you wrote that is the state the box has (a new tag on a new element) is one stereotype: the computed state is not written twice, it is the last stereotype of the box, so its look overrides the look of the other stereotypes as a later stereotype always does (the generated .puml writes the stereotypes in reverse, so it reads first there). Your styles.by-tags entry named like the state wins, property by property, over the default look of ArchiCode: a new with only a background-color keeps the thick outline of the default.

  • A word you wrote that is not the state the box has (a new tag on an element that did not change, a new tag on an element that changed, any state word when no comparison is made) is only your stereotype (a .puml of a comparison writes it written-new, so that the default look of new cannot reach it). The box has the style you gave to that name in styles.by-tags or styles.by-qualifiers, if any, and nothing of the default look, whatever else the view shows. On an element that changed, the computed state, which is last, has the last word over it.

  • The tags themselves are not read by the comparison (a rendering-* tag is never a change), so you can drop them when you like: the computed states say the same, and say it only where it is true.

The command line tells you where a word and the comparison disagree, one line per element and word, on the error output (not in the log, which hides warnings by default), and the files it writes and the table it prints are the same whether you read the lines or not. With the tag rendering-stereotypes: "new" added to customer in the example, which did not change, archicode diff says:

warning: the element `customer` carries `new` (`rendering-stereotypes` tag), but the comparison of `to-be` with `baseline` finds that it did not change and holds no change: it is drawn with your own `styles` entry named `new` (if any) and not with the default look of the state. The comparison computes the states, so the word can be dropped.

archicode diff and archicode views generate check the plateau they compare. archicode views generate of a plateau that is not compared says that a state word is a state of a comparison and that nothing is compared here. A workspace with no plateau has no comparison to disagree with, and is never told anything: there the words are your own stereotypes, as they always were. A word that agrees with the comparison is never reported. The editor draws the same: a hand-written word is a badge and a style, and the look of a state only where the state is computed. It tells you the same, in the same sentences, when a plateau is compared (Compare with on the band): in the panel of the element, under what changed, and in the Hand-written states list of the Changes panel (see Comparing two plateaus). Nothing is told when nothing is compared, since the comparison is a choice of the page.

Saying what an element replaces

A comparison finds an element by its reference. An element that moves under another parent, or is renamed, has another reference: the comparison reads it as one removed element and one new one, with nothing that links them. ArchiCode never guesses that two elements are the same thing; you say it, on the element of the more recent plateau, with the key replaces: a list of the full references, in the plateau it is compared with (below, the other plateau: the one it overrides, or the one --against names), of what the element replaces. The key is written where the new element is declared, in its manifest. It is the same hint as the moved block of Terraform. The editor writes it when you accept a candidate (see Moved, split and merged elements, and candidates), and you can edit it in the form of an element. The editor can move an element under another parent without breaking a reference, but a move does not say that the two references are one thing, and nor does a rename (see Moving an element under another parent).

The container inventory in the plateau to-be (plateaus/to-be/inventory.yaml of the example domain_plateaus)
header:
  kind: "archicode.morin.io/container"
  version: "1"
  plateau: "to-be"
  parent: "warehouse"
content:
  id: "inventory"
  name: "Inventory"
  description: "Counts the books on the shelves."
  replaces:
    - "shop.inventory" # the inventory moved from the shop to the warehouse
  elements:
    - kind: "component"
      id: "levels"
      name: "Levels"
      description: "The count of each book."
      replaces:
        - "shop.inventory.levels" # what is inside a moved element says it too

The inventory used to be a container of the system shop and is now one of the system warehouse, so its reference went from shop.inventory to warehouse.inventory. It says what it replaces, and so does the component levels inside it.

The key is optional, and a tool that does not know it ignores it. The comparison turns the elements that say what they replace into three states, and the replaced elements are no longer removed:

  • moved: the element replaces one element, which no other element replaces. It is in another place or has another name, and it is the same thing. Whatever else it changes, it keeps it: a name, a description and the relationships it declares are compared with the element it replaces, and the table says which (the natures), next to moved. A moved element is counted as moved, not also as changed.

  • split: several elements replace the same element (shop.orders became shop.cart and shop.checkout). Each of them is split, and the table names the other ones.

  • merged: the element replaces two elements or more (shop.billing replaces shop.invoices and shop.receipts), or an element that is in the other plateau and in the plateau at the same time. When an element is both split and merged, it is merged.

What the replaced elements held is not carried by itself: a container that moved takes none of its children with it, so each child needs its own replaces (levels above), or it is read as removed (the box of the replaced container is then drawn as the place it was removed from, with the words "replaced by"). What points at a replaced element follows it when exactly one element replaces it: a relationship to shop.till that now points at stock.till is not a change of the element that declares it, and nor is a node that deployed shop.till and now deploys stock.till. What points at a split element is not rewritten, because which half would own it is a guess. The relationships of the halves, and those that end on a half, are therefore not compared with the other plateau and read as new, whether the element had them before it was split or not; archicode diff, gap.md and gap.json say so on each of those rows (of the split shop.report-a) and count them in the header (see Compare plateaus).

Candidates. When an element is only in the other plateau and another one of the same kind and the same layer is only in the plateau, and nothing replaces or is replaced by either of them, the comparison may find that they look like one thing that was said twice: the same name (ignoring the case), a description with most of its words in common, or the only two elements of that kind under the same parent (only when one of the two plateaus is the past of the other, that is, the plateau it overrides or one further down the same line, baseline being the past of all: two plateaus that build on the same one are alternatives, and sharing a parent says nothing between them). A name counts only when no other removed element and no other new element of that kind has it: a Cache in every service, or a Message Broker in every environment, would pair every removed one with every new one and say nothing about which is which, so such a name proposes nothing (the description and the parent can still). It lists them as candidates and it never applies one. To accept one, write replaces on the new element; to refuse it, do nothing. archicode diff, gap.md and gap.json list the candidates once the plateau uses replaces (a workspace that does not is written as it always was), each with its reasons. The candidates are not drawn in the views.

Warnings. The comparison does not fail on a replaces that it cannot use: it ignores it, and says so on the error output of archicode diff and of archicode views generate (not in the log, which hides warnings by default), one line per entry (the example has none; the sample below is from the invented warehouse that Compare plateaus uses):

  • a reference that is not an element of the other plateau (you may be comparing with another plateau than the one you wrote it for);

  • a reference that is still an element of the plateau (what is there is not replaced, and an element cannot replace itself);

  • a reference listed twice (it counts once);

  • an entry that is not a reference (- 12, or an empty entry: it is ignored, and it is not read as the reference 12);

  • a replaces that is not a list (replaces: "shop.mailer": it is ignored, and the element replaces nothing).

The command line gives the comparison the list as you wrote it, so these lines are the ones the comparison says (the editor refuses a replaces that is not a list with a message). A replaces that is well written says nothing, and so does an empty list.

warning: replaces of shop.report-b (application layer): shop.nowhere is not an element of the base plateau, ignored

replaces is read from the elements of the plateau that is compared; a replaces on an element of the other plateau is not read. A plateau that is not compared does not use it.

The replaces of the layers are composed. Each layer writes replaces relative to the plateau it overrides, so a move that took two steps (a plateau moves shop.app.search to commerce.search, the next one moves it again to discovery.search) names, in the second layer, commerce.search, which a plateau two layers up does not have. Between two plateaus several layers apart the comparison reads the entries through the layers in between, down to the plateau it is compared with, so the move is a move: discovery.search replaces shop.app.search. Only what the layers wrote is read, nothing is guessed. A move then a split is a split, a move then a merge is a merge; an element that a layer in between created and the next one moved replaces nothing (it is new) and its entry is no mistake; an element that a layer in between removed ends its own chain. The comparison with the plateau a plateau overrides is as it was: one layer, nothing to compose. On the layered Brightcart of the repository (src/test/resources/brightcart/layered), archicode diff --plateau marketplace --against baseline counts +38 ~3 -6 elements, 11 moved, 2 split, 1 merged and +35 ~1 -9 relationships, lists discovery.search as moved from shop.app.search, and has no warning. Between two plateaus that do not stand on each other (marketplace against saas) only the layers of the plateau drawn since the plateau they have in common are read, and an entry that the plateau compared with still lacks is ignored with the usual warning, which names the reference the layers composed (shop.app.search, where the entry says commerce.search). Write replaces in the editor writes the entry you choose, one step. When the comparison is several layers apart and a layer in between removed the replaced element, the entry would be a mistake of its own layer: the editor refuses it and prints the sentence quoted in Moved, split and merged elements, and candidates.

What the command line shows. archicode diff has the three states in its table, with the counts of moved, split and merged on a line of their own (replacing), and says in the details of each row what the element replaces (see Compare plateaus). The views of the comparison draw them with the marks above (see Comparing two plateaus). The editor draws them too, lists the candidates, and writes replaces for you (see Moved, split and merged elements, and candidates).

A tutorial: from one workspace to a review of the changes

The tutorial builds the example domain_plateaus of this manual, an invented bookshop, from the architecture it has today to a review of the changes in the editor, including the things that change shape. Its commands run in the folder of .archicode.yaml; the output quoted is the one the commands print.

Step 1. Start from the architecture you have. An existing workspace is already your first plateau: its manifests are the root, baseline, and nothing in them has to change, not even a header. To keep the next plateaus in folders beside them, move the manifests to a folder of their own and read it with a path that ends in /** (see Manifests in folders); the folder does not have to be called baseline, the name is for the reader.

mkdir -p plateaus/baseline
mv manifests/* plateaus/baseline/
.archicode.yaml
# the plateaus are layers. The manifests in `plateaus/baseline` say no `plateau:`: they are the root, `baseline`, the architecture as it
# is. The manifests in `plateaus/to-be` say `plateau: "to-be"`: they are the layer that builds on it, with the patches of its
# `plateau.to-be.yaml`. The folders are only a habit; the tools read the `plateau:` of the header, not the folder.
settings:
  name: "Bookshop"
  manifests:
    paths: ["plateaus/**"]

A manifest of kind archicode.morin.io/plateau is optional for the root. This one only gives it a name and a horizon, and the tools show them (baseline is its id, and cannot be another):

plateaus/baseline/plateau.baseline.yaml
header:
  kind: "archicode.morin.io/plateau"
  version: "1"
content:
  # the root of the plateaus: its manifests say no `plateau:`. This manifest only gives it a name and a horizon
  id: "baseline"
  name: "As is"
  horizon: "2026-Q3"

archicode plateau list shows the tree, which is a root for now:

archicode plateau list
PLATEAU   PARENT  SOURCE                                            NAME   HORIZON
baseline  -       manifest plateaus/baseline/plateau.baseline.yaml  As is  2026-Q3

A workspace that names no plateau (no manifest of that kind, no plateau: in a header) is not a workspace of plateaus: it lists baseline alone, implicit, and generates its views in views, as ever. Once a plateau is named, even the root, the views of a plateau go to a folder named after it, here views-baseline (and views-baseline-<facet> for the facets, as the directory-name-template of the facets says). Move or update what reads the old folder.

plateaus/baseline/shop.yaml
header:
  kind: "archicode.morin.io/system"
  version: "1"
content:
  id: "shop"
  name: "Shop"
  elements:
    - kind: "container"
      id: "web"
      name: "Web site"
      description: "The front of the shop."
      relationships:
        - destination: "shop.catalog"
          label: "reads"
        - destination: "shop.mailer"
          label: "sends mails with"
    - kind: "container"
      id: "catalog"
      name: "Catalog"
      description: "The books on sale."
    - kind: "container"
      id: "mailer"
      name: "Mailer"
    - kind: "container"
      id: "orders"
      name: "Orders"
      description: "Takes the basket and the order of a customer."
    - kind: "container"
      id: "invoices"
      name: "Invoices"
      description: "Writes an invoice for each order."
    - kind: "container"
      id: "receipts"
      name: "Receipts"
      description: "Sends a receipt for each payment."
    - kind: "container"
      id: "reviews"
      name: "Reviews"
      description: "Readers rate the books they bought."

Step 2. Add the plateau of the future. A plateau is two things: a manifest of kind archicode.morin.io/plateau, which says what it overrides and what it changes, and the manifests of the elements it adds, which say plateau: "to-be" in their header. Create the folder plateaus/to-be, and in it plateau.to-be.yaml, which for now only names the plateau (its patches come in step 3), and, for instance, the container ratings:

plateaus/to-be/plateau.to-be.yaml, to begin with
header:
  kind: "archicode.morin.io/plateau"
  version: "1"
content:
  id: "to-be"
  name: "To be"
  horizon: "2027-Q1"
  override: "baseline"

The other container manifests of the layer (payments.yaml, cart.yaml, checkout.yaml, billing.yaml) and the manifests of the root that this page does not show (customer.yaml, warehouse.yaml) are in src/doc/examples/domain_plateaus: copy them from there to follow the steps.

plateaus/to-be/ratings.yaml
header:
  kind: "archicode.morin.io/container"
  version: "1"
  plateau: "to-be"
  parent: "shop"
content:
  id: "ratings"
  name: "Ratings"
  description: "Readers rate the books they have bought."

Its parent is the system shop, which only the root declares: a layer can put an element under any element of the plateaus it stands on. The tree has a second plateau now:

PLATEAU   PARENT    SOURCE                                            NAME   HORIZON
baseline  -         manifest plateaus/baseline/plateau.baseline.yaml  As is  2026-Q3
to-be     baseline  manifest plateaus/to-be/plateau.to-be.yaml        To be  2027-Q1

The folder plateaus/to-be holds what the layer adds, nothing of what it inherits. The command line and the editor read it as the plateau to-be because of the headers, not because of the name of the folder.

Step 3. Change what the layer inherits. In the example, shop.payments is added, shop.mailer is removed, shop.web gets the qualifier React and its relationships change, a description is reworded, and four things change shape: the orders are split in two, the invoices and the receipts become one, the reviews are rewritten as the ratings, and the inventory moves from the shop to another system, the warehouse. What is added is manifests (payments.yaml, cart.yaml, checkout.yaml, billing.yaml, ratings.yaml, inventory.yaml); what is removed or changed is the patches of the plateau, which are applied in this order (see [plateau-patches]):

plateaus/to-be/plateau.to-be.yaml
header:
  kind: "archicode.morin.io/plateau"
  version: "1"
content:
  id: "to-be"
  name: "To be"
  horizon: "2027-Q1"
  override: "baseline"
  patches:
    # what the new containers replace, and what is no longer done
    - operator: REMOVE
      target: "shop.mailer"
    - operator: REMOVE
      target: "shop.orders"
    - operator: REMOVE
      target: "shop.invoices"
    - operator: REMOVE
      target: "shop.receipts"
    - operator: REMOVE
      target: "shop.reviews"
    - operator: REMOVE
      target: "shop.inventory" # it moved to the warehouse: `inventory.yaml` says so
    # the web site
    - operator: SET
      target: "shop.web"
      field: qualifiers
      value: ["React"]
    - operator: SET
      target: "shop.web"
      field: relationships
      where: {destination: "shop.catalog", label: "reads"} # the one relationship that has this destination and this label
      value: {label: "browses"}
    - operator: ADD
      target: "shop.web"
      field: relationships
      value: {destination: "shop.payments", label: "pays with"}
    - operator: SET
      target: "shop.catalog"
      field: description
      value: "The books that are on sale."
    # what pointed at the inventory follows it
    - operator: ADD
      target: "warehouse.picking"
      field: relationships
      value: {destination: "warehouse.inventory", label: "counts with"}

  • Six REMOVE: what the new containers replace, and what is no longer done. A REMOVE takes the element with what is in it and what points at it, and archicode diff lists what it took besides the element (step 5).

  • A SET of the qualifiers of shop.web; a SET with where that relabels the one relationship of shop.web towards shop.catalog (the same patch that the editor writes when you change a relationship); an ADD of the relationship towards shop.payments.

  • A SET of the description of shop.catalog: the wording of the description is the only change of that element.

  • An ADD of a relationship to warehouse.picking: what pointed at the inventory follows it. The REMOVE of shop.inventory took the link that picking had, and the inventory is a new container of the warehouse in inventory.yaml.

shop.payments still carries the tag rendering-stereotypes: "new" that its author wrote by hand: it works as before, and the comparison does not need it. In the comparison its word agrees with the state new, so nothing is said; when a hand-written word and the comparison disagree, the command says so on its error output (see [plateau-state-tags]).

A patch that no longer fits is an error that names the plateau, the rank of the patch and its selector. With a typo in the target of the description’s SET (shop.catalogue):

error: plateau to-be: patch 10 (SET target shop.catalogue): selects nothing, there is no element shop.catalogue in the application layer

Step 4. Say what moved, was split or merged. Compare the two plateaus. Read by their references alone, shop.orders, shop.invoices, shop.receipts, shop.reviews and shop.inventory (with its child) are gone, and cart, checkout, billing, ratings and warehouse.inventory (with its child) are new, with nothing that links them. Some of them are the same things, and only you know which, so you say it with replaces on the elements of the more recent plateau (see Saying what an element replaces):

  • shop.orders was split: cart and checkout both say replaces: ["shop.orders"].

  • shop.invoices and shop.receipts were merged: billing lists both in its replaces.

  • shop.inventory moved to the warehouse. It is a manifest of its own, with the parent of its header, and plateaus/to-be/inventory.yaml says parent: "warehouse" and replaces on the container and on its child levels: a container that moves takes none of its children by itself.

  • shop.ratings says nothing, on purpose: it will show a candidate in step 6.

plateaus/to-be/inventory.yaml
header:
  kind: "archicode.morin.io/container"
  version: "1"
  plateau: "to-be"
  parent: "warehouse"
content:
  id: "inventory"
  name: "Inventory"
  description: "Counts the books on the shelves."
  replaces:
    - "shop.inventory" # the inventory moved from the shop to the warehouse
  elements:
    - kind: "component"
      id: "levels"
      name: "Levels"
      description: "The count of each book."
      replaces:
        - "shop.inventory.levels" # what is inside a moved element says it too

In plateaus/baseline, inventory.yaml has the same content with parent: "shop" and no replaces, and warehouse.yaml holds the system warehouse with its container picking. What points at a moved element follows it, but only if you say it: picking counts with shop.inventory in the root, and the layer’s last patch adds the link to warehouse.inventory, so that the comparison does not read it as a change of picking.

Step 5. Ask what changed. archicode diff compares the plateau that is drawn by default, the last one, with the plateau it overrides:

archicode diff
comparing to-be (To be) with baseline (As is)
elements      +2 ~2 -2
replacing     2 moved, 2 split, 1 merged
relationships +1 ~1 -1
wording only  1 of the 2 changed elements

CHANGE   THING         LAYER        REFERENCE                                   NATURES                                                                     DETAILS
merged   element       application  shop.billing                                -                                                                           from shop.invoices, shop.receipts; name: "Billing"
split    element       application  shop.cart                                   -                                                                           from shop.orders; name: "Cart"; split with shop.checkout
changed  element       application  shop.catalog                                description (wording only)                                                  description: "The books on sale." -> "The books that are on sale."
split    element       application  shop.checkout                               -                                                                           from shop.orders; name: "Checkout"; split with shop.cart
removed  element       application  shop.mailer                                 -                                                                           name: "Mailer"
new      element       application  shop.payments                               -                                                                           name: "Payments"
new      element       application  shop.ratings                                -                                                                           name: "Ratings"
removed  element       application  shop.reviews                                -                                                                           name: "Reviews"
changed  element       application  shop.web                                    qualifiers,relationships-added,relationships-removed,relationships-changed  qualifiers: +React
moved    element       application  warehouse.inventory                         -                                                                           from shop.inventory; name: "Inventory"
moved    element       application  warehouse.inventory.levels                  -                                                                           from shop.inventory.levels; name: "Levels"
changed  relationship  application  shop.web -> shop.catalog (browses)          label                                                                       label: "reads" -> "browses"
removed  relationship  application  shop.web -> shop.mailer (sends mails with)  -
new      relationship  application  shop.web -> shop.payments (pays with)       -

candidates, not applied (each pair looks like one thing said twice; to accept one, write `replaces: [FROM]` on the element TO)
LAYER        FROM          TO            REASONS
application  shop.reviews  shop.ratings  description

cascade of the removals (what a REMOVE took besides the elements it selects, in the plateaus the target adds to the one it is compared with)
PLATEAU  PATCH                           EFFECT   LAYER        ELEMENT                DETAIL
to-be    1 REMOVE target shop.mailer     dropped  application  shop.web               relationships: shop.mailer (sends mails with)
to-be    6 REMOVE target shop.inventory  removed  application  shop.inventory.levels
to-be    6 REMOVE target shop.inventory  dropped  application  warehouse.picking      relationships: shop.inventory (counts with)

The NATURES column says what changed in an element that changed (here qualifiers and the relationships of shop.web, description (wording only) for the catalog), and the DETAILS column says from what and to what. Two elements changed, one only by its wording, and two moved, two were split and one merged: the table says in each row what changed or where the element came from (see Compare plateaus for the options and the other formats). The replaced elements have no row and are not counted as removed, and shop.mailer and shop.reviews are, because nothing replaces them. The last section is the cascade of the REMOVE patches (see [plateau-cascade]): the removal of the mailer took the link of the web site towards it (patch 1), and the removal of the inventory took its component levels and the link of picking (patch 6). Nothing is written, and a difference is not an error, unless you ask for it to be: archicode diff --fail-on removed,moved prints the same table and exits with the code 3, because the example has removed and moved elements (a state the plateau does not hold gives 0; see Compare plateaus). That is the line to put in a build that must not let a removal through.

Step 6. Decide about the candidates. The candidate section is a pair: shop.reviews is removed, shop.ratings is new, they are of the same kind in the same layer and their descriptions share most of their words, so the comparison thinks they may be one thing said twice (see [plateau-candidates]). It never applies a candidate. If it is the same thing, write replaces: ["shop.reviews"] on ratings: ratings becomes a moved element (moved, natures name,description, from shop.reviews), the counts read elements +1 ~2 -1 and replacing 3 moved, 2 split, 1 merged, and the table lists a new, weaker candidate, shop.mailer to shop.payments with the reason parent: with the reviews and the ratings settled they are the only removed and the only new container of the shop. They are not the same thing, and the answer is to do nothing. If the reviews and the ratings are not the same thing either, do nothing, as this example does, and the output stays as it is. The editor writes the line for you (step 8).

Step 7. Draw the comparison. views generate writes the views of a plateau, and the views of the comparison next to them (with the plateau it overrides, or the one --against names):

archicode views generate
$ ls
plateaus
views-to-be
views-to-be-vs-baseline
.archicode.yaml
$ ls views-to-be-vs-baseline
gap.json
gap.md
plantuml

(The ls shows what a clean folder shows; a folder that already had manifests or views-baseline still has them: they are not removed.) views-to-be holds the views of the plateau, as for any workspace. views-to-be-vs-baseline holds the views of the comparison, and gap.md and gap.json, the table of changes of step 5 in a file that a pull request can carry (with the cascade, the key cascade of the JSON). archicode views generate --only-changes draws, in views-to-be-vs-baseline only, what changed and the context faded: the views of views-to-be and the table stay as they are. Every box and every link of these views that changed says its state with a mark and a line style, and the views that have a change have a legend. The overview of the container shop.web:

The overview of shop.web in views-to-be-vs-baseline

shop.web and shop.catalog are changed (dotted outline, ~), shop.payments is new (thick outline, `), `shop.mailer` is removed (dashed, struck title, `−`), and the system `shop`, which did not change by itself, _contains changes_ (`±`). The links say it the same way: ` pays with is new, − sends mails with is removed, and the label of shop.web to shop.catalog changed, from reads to browses.

The detailed view of the system shop draws what was split and merged. The titles say where the boxes came from, (from shop.orders) and (from shop.invoices, shop.receipts), and the legend has one line for each of the two states, with dashes that tell them from the others. shop.ratings is new and shop.reviews removed: the candidate is not drawn.

Step 8. Look at it in the editor. archicode editor serve opens the folder in your browser (see Serve the editor).

The editor opens the plateau of the address (plateau=), else the one this browser drew last in this folder, else the one settings.current names, else the root, baseline: here the root. The command line, without a name, draws the last plateau.

  1. The top bar has four buttons, Timeline, Stories, Files and Settings. Timeline opens Applications and has four pages, in a bar of their own: Plateaus, Applications, Environments and Elements. On those four pages, and on those only, the band under the top bar has two lists: Plateau, which draws a plateau in the whole editor, and Compare with, which shows the same tree of plateaus under Nothing and Past. Choose To be in Plateau (see The band of plateaus). The page Plateaus says the same as a tree: one entry for each plateau, with its name, its horizon and what it brings over the plateau it overrides (chips such as + 2 new, ~ 2 changed and − 2 removed), and it is where a plateau is written (see The Plateaus page).

  2. In Compare with, choose Past (follows the plateau drawn): Applications shows the states, the new, changed, removed, moved, split and merged boxes, and the key under the drawing is the legend (see Comparing two plateaus in the editor). Past is the plateau To be overrides, here As is; choosing As is in the tree below it is read the same way. The tree lists every other plateau (the plateau drawn is greyed in it), so that a plateau that overrides the one drawn, or any other, is compared with by choosing it there.

  3. Plateaus, Applications, Environments and Elements, and Stories while a comparison is on, have the Changes panel on the right: the counts (+ 2 new, ~ 2 changed, − 2 removed, → 2 moved, ⋔ 2 split, ⊕ 1 merged for the elements), the candidates, the Cascade of the removals with the three rows of step 5, then every element and relationship that changed. They are the rows of step 5, in the same words. Next change and Previous change walk through them, one by one, and the lists State and Layer narrow what is read; a click on a row shows it (see The Changes panel).

  4. Select a changed element in Elements: the block What changed gives each property Before and After and its relationships that changed; for a moved, split or merged element it says what it replaces (see What changed). The page of the element says where each value comes from: for Web site, the name and the description are inherited from baseline and the qualifiers are set by patch 7 of to-be (SET target shop.web).

  5. Changes only, among the tools of a diagram, keeps what changed and the boxes needed to place it, and Hide wording-only changes sets the rewording aside (see Changes only and wording-only changes).

  6. Under the counts, the Candidates section proposes the pair of step 6, Reviews and Ratings, with its reason, close description. Write replaces adds replaces: [shop.reviews] to ratings in the manifest that declares it, plateaus/to-be/ratings.yaml, and changes nothing else in the file; ratings is now moved, the counts read → 3 moved, and Save all changes writes the file. With the clear pair resolved, the comparison proposes a weaker one: Mailer and Payments are now the only removed and the only new container under shop, which is the reason it gives (the only two of their kind under the same parent). They are not the same thing, so Not related is the answer (see Moved, split and merged elements, and candidates).

  7. A change is written in the plateau that is drawn. In To be, select Web site, Edit, change its description and Save all changes: the description comes from baseline, so the editor adds one SET patch at the end of the patches of plateaus/to-be/plateau.to-be.yaml and leaves plateaus/baseline/shop.yaml as it was. The same edit made in As is, on a field that to-be inherits (the description of Picking), writes the root and, because to-be sees the change, asks first (Writing in baseline changes other plateaus, with the list of the plateaus) (see Writing in a plateau).

  8. Move puts an element under another parent (or, for a kind that may be a root, at the top level), with what is inside it, and rewrites the references to it (see Moving an element under another parent); it moves an element that the plateau drawn declares, such as Ratings of to-be, and refuses one it inherits. Move writes no replaces.

  9. Stories lists the stories and the views of the workspace, whatever plateau is drawn. The story From the shop to the new shop (plateaus/story.migration.yaml, the file shown in [story-step-plateaus]) tells it in four steps: Play opens the story in a window of its own that covers the editor and plays it like slides, and each step that says at and against is drawn at that plateau and with that comparison, so that the first step shows the shop of As is and the second the shop of To be with what changed marked; the slide says Plateau To be · compared with As is, and closing it leaves the editor as it was (see Stories: stories and views and [editor-story-player]). Show on diagram, on a step, draws that step on the editing diagram instead, and the bar over it says told at To be, compared with As is.

  10. Files lists every file, in a group for each plateau: As is with its five manifests, To be with its seven, and, last, No plateau with the story, which belongs to none (see Files).

A change is written in the plateau that is drawn; the comparison itself writes nothing (see Writing in a plateau).

Step 9. Go on. To prepare the state after to-be, write another manifest of kind archicode.morin.io/plateau with override: "to-be" and the manifests that say plateau: "later":

plateaus/later/plateau.later.yaml
header:
  kind: "archicode.morin.io/plateau"
  version: "1"
content:
  id: "later"
  name: "Later"
  horizon: "2028-Q1"
  override: "to-be"
  patches:
    - operator: REMOVE
      target: "shop.checkout"
plateaus/later/search.yaml
header:
  kind: "archicode.morin.io/container"
  version: "1"
  plateau: "later"
  parent: "shop"
content:
  id: "search"
  name: "Search"
  description: "Finds a book by its title."

The plateaus still form one line, so later is now the plateau that is drawn by default, and it is compared with the plateau it overrides:

archicode diff
comparing later (Later) with to-be (To be)
elements      +1 ~0 -1
relationships +0 ~0 -0
wording only  0 of the 0 changed elements

CHANGE   THING    LAYER        REFERENCE      NATURES  DETAILS
removed  element  application  shop.checkout  -        name: "Checkout"
new      element  application  shop.search    -        name: "Search"
