(The command also prints, on the error output, the warning of step 3 about the hand-written new of shop.payments: against to-be that element did not change, so the word is only the author’s.) archicode diff --plateau later --against baseline compares the last plateau with the first one, and archicode plateau list shows the three. When two plateaus override the same one, the tree forks and the commands ask for the plateau with --plateau (see [plateau-choose]); the editor opens the plateau of the address, else the one you drew last, else the one settings.current names (which Settings can write, see The current plateau), else the root.

Step 10. Put it away. A plateau can be deleted in the editor, from the page Plateaus (see The Plateaus page): Delete opens a dialog that lists the files that go, here plateaus/later/plateau.later.yaml and plateaus/later/search.yaml, and nothing else. Had another plateau overridden later, the dialog would also have said which plateaus go under its parent and what they lose. The files leave the disk at the next Save all changes. The command line deletes nothing: remove the files by hand, and the folders views-later and views-later-vs-to-be that views generate wrote, if you made them.

Upgrading: what breaks, and what does not

From settings.plateaus to layers. The key no longer exists, and no command converts a workspace: a .archicode.yaml that still has it is refused with the one line quoted in Plateaus. To convert one by hand:

  1. Keep the manifests of your first plateau: they are the root, baseline, and need no header. Its id changes: --plateau as-is, settings.current: as-is and scripts that name it must say baseline (a Kind: Plateau manifest with id: baseline and name: "As is" keeps the name for people).

  2. For each later plateau, from the oldest, write a manifest of kind archicode.morin.io/plateau (its id, name, horizon and override: the previous plateau, or baseline for the first). The manifests of elements that are new in that plateau get plateau: "<id>" in their header. What the plateau removes or changes among the elements it inherits becomes a patch (REMOVE, SET, ADD or MERGE, see [plateau-patches]). The copies of the unchanged manifests in the old complete folders are deleted: a manifest that declares again an element of a plateau below it is the error of [plateau-header].

  3. Delete settings.plateaus, and write manifests.paths (a workspace with plateaus could not have one; the default is the folder manifests) with an entry that ends in /** for the folders that hold the plateaus (see Manifests in folders).

  4. Run archicode plateau list and archicode diff --plateau <id> for each plateau: the counts should be the ones you had.

Four differences to know: the views of a plateau are written in views-<plateau> and the views of its comparison in views-<plateau>-vs-<against>; every plateau but the root is now compared with the plateau it overrides when its views are generated; a plateau id is lowercase letters, digits, _ and - (a capital, or a dot as in v1.2, is refused: error: the plateau id `v1.2 in …​ is not valid`); and --baseline is --against. The from key of a plateau, which was reserved, and archicode plateau add, are gone.

Earlier changes. Two things that used to pass stop with an error that says what to change. Everything else about a workspace that has no plateau is unchanged: it is read, and generates its views in the same folders, byte for byte as before (checked on the views of this repository’s workspaces and on a copy of a real workspace of 3,411 views).

  1. A path of settings.manifests.paths with .. or an absolute path. ../shared, /srv/manifests and C:\data are refused: one folder holds a whole workspace, so that the editor, which only reaches the folder it opened, reads what the command line reads. Move the folder below the folder of .archicode.yaml, or move .archicode.yaml up to a folder that holds both, and change the path to match. A path that stays below the folder (manifests, node_modules/<module>/manifests) is not affected.

  2. A reference declared twice. Two manifests, a manifest and an element nested in another one, or two elements of one file that share a reference used to pass silently, and which of them was drawn depended on chance. The command line now stops, names the reference and the two files, and writes nothing, and the editor reports the same in the same words (see [duplicate-references]). Remove one of the declarations, or give one of them another id. Two identical copies count too. This check applies to workspaces without plateaus as well; with plateaus, the same reference in a plateau and in its ancestor is the same error, and in two plateaus that do not stand on each other it is no duplicate, since they are alternatives.

Nothing is to be changed in the manifests themselves: their format is the one it always was, with two optional keys more, plateau in the header and replaces (see Saying what an element replaces), that an older version of ArchiCode ignores.

Later changes. Layers came with the editor reshaped; a few things moved after them, and what a workspace written for the first version of layers may meet is this:

  1. A view or a story that says plateau:. It is accepted and means nothing, with no message at all (see [plateau-untied]; the warning that earlier versions printed is gone). A workspace whose only plateau: is on a view or a story is no longer a workspace of plateaus, so --plateau and settings.current are errors for it, as for any workspace without plateaus. Two plateaus that each declared a view of one id, or a story of one id, are now one error: a view and a story are declared once.

  2. Kinds. An element declared with header.parent under a parent that cannot hold its kind (a system under a container) is an error, with or without patches, where it used to load in a plateau that had none (see [nesting]); an id with a dot is an error. The group kinds were refused here at the time; since 0.17 there is no group at all (see below).

  3. Empty values and spellings. A key with nothing after it and an item with no value in a list are absent, in both tools (global-enabled: with nothing is true; an empty .archicode.yaml is the default workspace; an item of relationships with no value draws no relationship in the editor either). The keys of settings.facets are global-enabled, directory-name-template, hide-unlinked and json-path; the camel-case spelling was still read then; since 0.17 it is refused (see below). The text of header.parent, header.plateau and header.version is the text as written: parent: yes is the reference yes.

  4. The maps. archicode views generate --view <id> draws a view of the map viewpoint, which it used to skip with a warning (see [map-viewpoint]).

  5. The order of the warnings. The facets are read in the order of the file, so the warnings that name them, and the creation of their folders, come in that order.

  6. Folders of a comparison. A folder that holds both gap.json and gap.md is left out of a /** read, whichever command wrote it (see Manifests in folders).

  7. The editor. The pages are Plateaus, Applications, Environments, Elements, Stories, Files and Settings (see The perspectives and their pages); the band has two lists; Files has no choice of what it lists.

What changes for a reader who knows 0.21.0. These are the changes after the release 0.21.0; they are in the release 0.22.0. Nothing breaks: the editor lays a wide diagram out in rows of a size you choose, a link copied from the address bar draws what the sender sees, and the buttons that walk the changes stay in reach. A workspace that loaded in 0.21.0 loads in 0.22.0 and the command line writes the same files for it (the key layout is one it ignores, see The boxes per row).

  1. A diagram can aim at a number of boxes per row (New, see The boxes per row). layout: {columns: N}, a whole number from 1 to 20, in a saved view or under settings.views.properties.<viewpoint>, and a Columns list in the toolbar of a diagram; the editor and the story player lay the diagram out with it. A workspace that sets none is drawn as before, and the command line ignores the key.

  2. A link copied from the address bar draws the diagram the sender saw (Changed, see What a link carries). The address carries what changes the boxes and the links drawn: the layout, Changes only, and which boxes of a Map are open or hidden, on the page of an element, on both Maps and on the page of a saved view. The wording toggle, the key and the zoom stay out of it.

  3. The buttons that walk the changes stay in view (Fixed, see The editor on a small screen). With many changes, each press of Next change scrolled the panel and carried the buttons away; the row now stays under the tabs, and the selected row is brought into view without moving one that is already visible.

  4. The Workspace menu and the top bar of a browser workspace fit a narrow window (Fixed, see The editor on a small screen). In a window 360 pixels wide the note of Export as a file ran out of its item and the buttons of the bar went past the window, so the page scrolled sideways; the buttons now wrap, the notes stay in their items and the menu stays inside the window.

  5. You can read the plan of a fold before you make it (New, see Fold a plateau into the one above). archicode plateau fold <id> folds a plateau into the one above it: it rewrites the manifests, deletes the manifest of the folded plateau, composes the replaces, checks on a temporary copy that the plateaus that stay are the same before and after, and writes all its files or none. --dry-run prints the plan and the check and writes nothing; the plain form writes, so read the plan first and fold in a clean git tree. A fold it cannot make is one error: line (exit 1) and nothing is written. The name and the horizon of the folded plateau, and the comments, quotes and layout of the files rewritten, are not kept.

What changes for a reader who knows 0.20.0. These are the changes after the release 0.20.0; they are in the release 0.21.0. Nothing breaks: the editor opens in a browser that has no folder picker, a Map can be drawn from the relationships it lists, a story can light up one relationship at a time, and a few mistakes are fixed or told in the same words by both tools. A workspace that loaded in 0.20.0 loads in 0.21.0 and the command line writes the same files for it (checked by running every command on every example and fixture of the repository against the jar of 0.19.0: the only differences are the sentence of 0.20.0 for a reference that names no element, see [upgrade-0-19], and the files of the new story order_flow); the new keys links, highlight and layout mean nothing until you write them. As for the blocks below, there is no release notes file in the repository: these blocks are the notes.

  1. The editor works in Chrome, Firefox and Safari with nothing installed (New, see A workspace kept in the browser). Where the browser has no folder picker, or you do not want one, the editor keeps a workspace in the browser; one button on the presentation page opens the Brightcart sample; a workspace is exported to, and imported from, one YAML file with the same bytes. Nothing on your disk is read or written unless you pick a folder, and the editor served by archicode editor serve never touches the served folder while a browser workspace is open.

  2. A Map can list the relationships it draws (New, see [map-links]). links in the properties of a Map of the application layer is a list of source, destination and an optional label: the boxes drawn are the ends of those relationships with the boxes around them. archicode views generate draws it, and an entry that names no relationship is one error: line. A Map with no links is drawn as before.

  3. A link can be highlighted, in a view and in a story step (New, see [map-highlight] and [story-of-a-flow]). highlight draws the named links heavy, with a mark, and the others calm; a story step can say its own, so that a story lights up a flow one link after the other. archicode stories generate draws it, and the editor and the story player draw it too. The shipped example is the story order_flow of the Brightcart fixture.

  4. The editor lists the relationships of a Map (New, see [editor-map-links]). Under the tree of the page Applications, tick the relationships to draw, or click them on the diagram; the choice is saved as the links of the view, kept in the address, and followed by a rename or a move of an element.

  5. The plateau tree is indented by depth for a chain of any length (Fixed). The indentation stopped growing at the sixth level, so a long chain of plateaus read as siblings on the page Plateaus and in the two lists of the band.

  6. The name field of a new browser workspace is one line high (Fixed).

  7. A view with no element, or one that names an element that does not exist, is said in the same words by the command line and the editor (Changed message, see [upgrade-0-19] for the first of the two sentences).

  8. The step 3 of the story migration of the example domain_plateaus draws something (Fixed). It played an empty slide. The editor no longer logs an error on load when the browser asks for its icon.

  9. The manual’s recipe for folding a plateau by hand is repaired (see [editor-plateau-delete]). It now says to compose the replaces of the plateau above, and that two columns of the cascade of the removals change, the plateau and the number of the patch.

  10. The editor page is built from source files (Changed, no effect on a reader). The one file that is distributed is the same page; it is assembled, formatted and linted from about eighty parts.

What changes for a reader who knows 0.19.0. These are the changes after the release 0.19.0; they are in the release 0.20.0. Nothing breaks: three things are new, one thing is fixed, five mistakes are told better (a sentence that names the file and the key, or one error: line where there was a stack trace), and every other command prints and writes what 0.19.0 printed and wrote, byte for byte (the views, the tables of diff, query graph, query views, the generated stories and the output of query schemas workspace; this was checked by running every command on every example and fixture the repository ships against the jar of 0.19.0). A script that reads the old text of the sentence of the second item below has to read the new one. As for the blocks below, there is no release notes file in the repository: these blocks are the notes.

  1. archicode query schemas manifest describes the content of every kind (New, see [manifest-schema]). It printed a schema of the envelope only, with the content as any value; it now carries the schema of the ten kinds side by side under kinds (keys, required keys, enums, shapes, what each element kind holds, whether it can be the top of a layer, the id rule and the reference form), the nested elements under nested, and it is derived from the classes that read the manifests. The output is still sorted. A tool that read the old output and ignored what it did not know is not affected; one that took the content for anything will find an object. The schema cannot say a dozen things the loader checks (a reference that resolves, a name declared twice, the tree of plateaus, a patch that selects nothing); the section lists them.

  2. archicode guide is new (see Print the guide for an agent). It prints a guide for an agent, generated from the code, that needs no workspace and no network, and starts with archicode guide <version>.

  3. archicode init is new (see Start a workspace). It writes a small workspace that loads and is drawn, adds /views*/ to .gitignore, and overwrites nothing.

  4. The editor’s save keeps two layouts it used to write again (see Elements and Limits of today). A file that has a block scalar as an item of a list (- |, then its text) no longer makes any save, even of another key, write the whole file again in the editor’s layout with every comment lost; and a block scalar on the second key of a map that is a list item (note: |) stays a block scalar when the first key of the item goes or another comes before it, where it was written as a quoted text. The file read the same before; what changes is the comments and the layout that are kept. Two shapes still fall back to the emitter’s text: - - | on one line, and a folded block (- >) that reads differently once written again; they, and the two nesting cases listed with them, are in the limits.

  5. A saved view or a story step with no properties is one error: line (Fixed). It ended in a NullPointerException and a stack trace; it now says the view v has no element: write the reference of one in properties.element , exit 1, the same mistake as properties: {}. A map needs no element and is drawn.

  6. A reference that names no element says where it is (Changed message). A relationship destination, a uses entry or a hosted application that names an element which does not exist said unable to find the element nowhere; it now says which element holds it, in which file and at which key, for example error: shop.web has a relationship towards nowhere, which does not exist, in manifests/shop.yaml at relationships[0].destination , and the editor says the same sentence word for word (a workspace with plateaus adds in the plateau p, ). It stays an error where it was one, so no command that succeeded fails.

  7. archicode views generate -e <engine> with an engine that does not exist, and -p <file> with a file that does not exist, are one error: line each (Fixed). They ended in a stack trace; they now say unknown rendering engine nope (known: plantuml) and that the file does not exist or cannot be accessed.

  8. archicode init leaves nothing behind when it is stopped (Fixed). A folder it could not write to (a manifests folder without the right to write) left .archicode.yaml behind, and the next init refused the folder as already a workspace; it now removes every file it created.

  9. A mistake in one key of a manifest never ends in a stack trace (Checked). A test copies every example and fixture, removes one key, empties one list or sets one key to null, and runs the commands: each result is a success or an error: line. The sweep found no other stack trace in the files the repository ships.

What changes for a reader who knows 0.18.0. These are the changes after the release 0.18.0; the release that carries them has no number in this manual yet. One of them can stop a workspace that loaded before, a facet whose name cannot be the name of a folder, and it comes first; the others change a sentence, a saved file or a screen, or add something. As for the blocks below, there is no release notes file in the repository: these blocks are the notes. The error: lines are the ones the tools print.

  1. A facet whose name is not a folder name is refused (Breaking, for such a name). The name of a facet goes as it is written into the folders of its views, so a name that is empty, is . or .., holds /, \, a NUL or another control character or one of < > : " | ? *, or ends with a dot or a space is now refused by every command that loads the workspace and by the editor, with one error: line for each facet, exit code 1, before anything is written. A facet called a/b loaded in 0.18.0 and wrote its views in views-a/b, a folder inside a folder; it now stops with:

    error: the facet name `a/b` cannot be the name of a folder: it holds `/`, which separates the folders of a path (write a plain word, such as `journey`)

    Rename the facet in .archicode.yaml, and rename or delete the folder it wrote if you keep it. Every facet of the examples of this manual and of the repository’s tests uses plain words, so their views are the ones they were, byte for byte. The rule and the names it does not look at are in Tackling view clutter.

  2. A workspace file that cannot be read is one line, not a stack trace (see Errors, warnings and exit codes). A .archicode.yaml that is there and that the file system refuses to read (a file without the right to read it) printed error: unexpected java.io.FileNotFoundException: …​ (Permission denied) and a stack trace in 0.18.0; it is now error: the workspace file `...` cannot be read: permission denied, exit code 1. The other ways to have no workspace file, and the sentence for workspace.yaml, are word for word what they were.

  3. The editor’s card says what is wrong with the file (see Opening the editor). For a .archicode.yaml that is read and found wrong (a key that does not exist, YAML that is not valid, a file that is not a mapping, settings.plateaus, a path or a facet name that is refused) the card Can’t open this workspace shows the sentence of the command line, where it said that the file could not be read and added a hint to check that it is valid YAML. It keeps those words for a file that cannot be had, and the list of the remembered folders notes only those.

  4. The editor keeps the brackets (see Elements). A list or a map that you wrote in brackets (qualifiers: [a, b], tags: {zone: north}) and that a change reaches is saved in brackets, on its line, when it fits, where it was saved as a block. Nothing is lost either way: the file reads the same, and the cases that still become a block are listed there.

  5. A story can be played (see Stories: stories and views and [editor-story-player]). Play, on the page of a story, opens the story in a window of its own that covers the editor and plays it like slides: the diagram of each step, drawn at the plateau and with the comparison the step names, its words, a list of the steps, Previous and Next, full screen, and the arrow keys, Home, End and Esc. The editor behind it does not change: when the window closes, the plateau, the comparison, the selection, the page and the address are what they were, and nothing is written. play= in the address keeps the window in a link. The bar over the editing diagram, which Show on diagram raises for one step, no longer has Previous, Next or a list of the steps: they are in the player. Nothing the command line writes changes: archicode stories generate and views generate draw what they drew.

  6. The save keeps the quotes and the comments it can (see Elements and Limits of today). When you save a file, a value that comes back on a line that is written again (a map in brackets that becomes a list of maps, an item that moves, a list that gives back its item) keeps the quotes it had, and so does a key; a comment stays on the line of its own key or item; and removing the first of two items that read the same removes the first, with its comment, where the comment could stay on the second. A comment that a removed line left right under a block scalar (key: |) no longer makes the editor write the whole file again in its own layout, which lost every comment of the file. The file reads the same either way; what changes is that quotes and comments that were lost or put on the wrong item are kept.

  7. archicode --version is new (see Print the version): one line, archicode <version>, on the top command only. Nothing else changes on the command line.

  8. The release page carries native programs (see Install). The releases after 0.18.0 attach archicode-<version>-linux-amd64, archicode-<version>-linux-arm64 and archicode-<version>-macos-arm64 with a SHA256SUMS file, each run on its own kind of machine before the release is public. The Maven Artifact and the OCI Image are as they were. There is no file for Windows or for an Intel Mac yet.

What changes for a reader who knows 0.17.0. This release has one change that stops every workspace, the name of the workspace file, so it comes first. There is no release notes file in the repository: this block and the ones that follow are the notes. The error: lines below are the ones the tools print.

  1. The workspace file is .archicode.yaml (Breaking). It was workspace.yaml. The new name is the default of -w for every command and the name the editor looks for, and there is no alias and no command that converts: rename the file once, with git mv workspace.yaml .archicode.yaml, or keep the old name and say so with -w workspace.yaml. A folder that has workspace.yaml and no .archicode.yaml is refused, by every command and by the editor, with this one line, exit code 1, before anything is loaded or written:

    error: `workspace.yaml` is no longer the name of the workspace file: rename it to `.archicode.yaml` (`git mv workspace.yaml .archicode.yaml`), or tell the command line where it is with `-w workspace.yaml`

    archicode -w workspace.yaml views generate works as it did, since the path is explicit. archicode editor serve is the exception: it opens a folder that has a .archicode.yaml, so -w workspace.yaml is refused with a usage error (exit code 2), the editor opens a folder with a .archicode.yaml: /work/workspace.yaml is not named so., followed by the usage of the command; rename the file for the editor. The name begins with a dot, so the file is hidden: ls -a shows it, and a file dialog shows it when you ask for hidden files. It is never read as a manifest, in a folder read with /** or without it (see Manifests in folders); a workspace.yaml left behind in a folder that a path ending in /** reads is read as a manifest and refused (error: `workspace.yaml` is not valid: unknown key `settings` (known: content, header) at `settings` (line 5, column 3)), so remove it. Change the scripts, the CI files and the Docker commands that name the old file; the views, the tables and everything else the commands write are byte for byte what they were (checked on the twenty workspaces of this repository).

  2. archicode stories generate writes more (see Draw the stories). The folder of a step that compares two plateaus gets the table of changes, gap.md and gap.json, and the facets of the workspace are drawn beside each step, in <NN>-<view>-<facet> folders. The files that were written are the same, byte for byte, with one exception: global-enabled: false is now honoured, so a workspace with that setting and stories no longer gets the folders of the global views of its steps, only those of its facets. 0.17.0 drew them. A workspace that does not set it loses nothing.

  3. More links in the technology views (see Using other environments and [map-technology]). A relationship whose destination is inside a deployed application (a component of a container that a node lists in applications) is now a dependency towards the node that hosts it, as a relationship whose source is inside already was; and the links between the nodes of one environment that a facet that keeps a flow draws read the relationships of everything inside the deployed applications. The overview, detailed and deep views of the technology layer and the Map of the technology layer gain the links, in the command line and in the editor. The views of a workspace that deploys no application with such relationships are the same, byte for byte, and so are the views of all the examples of this manual. In the layered Brightcart of the repository’s tests, the technology views of the facets journey, no-externals and without-cart and of their comparisons gain links, all inside one environment.

  4. The editor reads more like the command line (see Elements). A tagged number is read as the command line reads it: !!int 0x1F is 31, !!int 1:30 stays the text 1:30, !!binary aGVsbG8= stays the text aGVsbG8= (the page used to refuse them); foo: .inf under a key the header does not have is the unknown key, as in the command line; and a style written line-gap: 0x1F, round-corner: 8.0 or 1.5 is drawn with the number the command line reads. The numbers of 0.17.0 are unchanged otherwise.

  5. The save keeps more as written (see Elements). An anchor, an alias or a tag inside square or curly brackets, a text that goes over several lines and a list item whose map starts under its dash are kept as written when you save a file, where the editor wrote the whole file again in its own layout and lost its comments. A few layouts still fall back (see Limits of today).

  6. The editor checks before it writes (see Moved, split and merged elements, and candidates and [editor-plateau-delete]). Add replaces is checked as Write replaces is; a merge that the layer cannot hold is refused as a whole, where its first pair used to be written alone; and the delete of a plateau that others override is also refused when a step of a story is compared against a plateau above that would lose what it draws and the plateau the step is told at does not keep it either, or when a saved view is drawn in such a plateau. A delete that was allowed and loses nothing is written as before. How to fold a plateau into the one above by hand is told in [fold-by-hand].

  7. Three rough edges, fixed (see Comparing two plateaus, [editor-rename] and Tackling view clutter): the open key under the diagram keeps its 30 % cap when the window is resized, a write that fails after you said yes to the dialog of the plateaus gives the form back as typed, and a facet the kernel refuses shows its sentence as the error of the view instead of "facet removes this element".

  8. The editor is quicker on a large pair of plateaus: finding what has nothing to draw and resolving the patches take a quarter to a fifth of the time they took on the 591 elements of the test pair, and grow with the elements instead of faster (see Limits of today). No output changes.

What changes for a reader who knows 0.16.0. This release removes things that were deprecated, so the first four items can stop a workspace that loaded before. Each prints one error: line, exit code 1, and writes nothing; the sentences below are the ones the tools print.

  1. The group is gone (Breaking). Write the children of a former group in the elements of its parent, which shortens each reference by one segment (shop.web.edge.gateway with a group edge becomes shop.web.gateway). The sentences, for kind: group and for the three old header kinds, are in [manifest-kinds]; the editor says the same words.

  2. The camel-case keys of a facet are gone (Breaking). hideUnlinked, globalEnabled, directoryNameTemplate and jsonPath must be written hide-unlinked, global-enabled, directory-name-template and json-path. The sentence names the key to write: +unknown key `hideUnlinked, write hide-unlinked (known: actions, hide-unlinked, name) at settings.facets.customs[0].hideUnlinked +. `json_path of a patch is still read.

  3. The Jayway dialect of the facets is gone (Breaking). anyof, noneof, == null and != null are not JSONPath. The command line and the editor refuse the path with +facet f: the facet path `…​ uses @.qualifiers anyof ['a'], which is not JSONPath (RFC 9535) and is no longer read: write @.qualifiers[?(@ == 'a')] +`; the table of the four rewrites is in Tackling view clutter. The warning that used to rewrite them is gone with them.

  4. The old addresses of the editor are gone (Breaking). /map, /elements, /library, /diagram, /applications, /plateaus, /environments and /timeline alone open the default page, Applications, like any unknown address. Write /timeline/applications, /timeline/elements, /stories, /stories/views, /timeline/plateaus and /timeline/environments (see The perspectives and their pages). Update your bookmarks.

  5. archicode stories generate draws the steps of the stories (see Draw the stories). views generate still does not.

  6. More dependencies between environments. A relationship declared by what is inside a deployed application (a component of a container that a node lists in applications) is now a dependency of the node, in the overview, the detailed and the deep viewpoints of the technology layer and in the Map of the technology layer (see Using other environments). A workspace that deploys containers whose components declare the relationships gains links there; the layered Brightcart gains the dependencies of app-vm and staging.vm towards partners and sandbox. The views of every other example are written byte for byte as before.

  7. Deleting a plateau in the middle. A plateau that other plateaus override can be deleted in the editor, in one step; they go under its parent and the dialog lists what each loses (see [editor-plateau-delete]).

  8. Write replaces refuses an entry its layer cannot hold, when the comparison is several layers apart and a layer in between removed the replaced element: the button is off, with a sentence (see Moved, split and merged elements, and candidates).

  9. The key of a comparison is folded under the diagram, with a Key button (see Comparing two plateaus), and a write the editor refuses, or an error while it reads the form, no longer empties the form: what you typed stays (see [editor-rename]).

  10. The numbers. The editor reads a plain scalar that YAML 1.1 reads as a number as the command line does, and saves it back as written: id: 010 is the reference 8 in both tools, parent: 0 and version: 1 in a header are the text as written, line-gap: 0x1F is accepted for a whole number and "0x1F", quoted, is refused, and .inf is the one line +Malformed numeric value '.inf' at `content +`. Two differences are left and pinned (see Limits of today), and 0.18 reads the others like the command line (above). The command line is unchanged.

What changes for a reader who knows 0.15.0. Since that release, these things moved; the first can change a file that a workspace had, the second an error:

  1. A comparison several layers apart reads the replaces of the layers in between. archicode diff --plateau <a> --against <b> and the editor give a move that took two steps as a move, where they gave one element new and one removed with a warning; the table of the two plateaus, the counts and the files that views generate writes for the comparison change with it. The comparison of a plateau with the plateau it overrides is as it was (see Saying what an element replaces).

  2. views generate --view on a view whose element a facet removes is one error: line that names the view and the facet, with nothing written, where it was a bare dangling reference after some folders were written (see [plateau-views-lacking]).

  3. A patch can take a field out (operator: REMOVE, target, field), and the editor writes it when you clear an inherited text, where it refused (see [plateau-patches] and Writing in a plateau). The patches that were written before are read as they were.

  4. The editor: the Remove buttons of a qualifier, an application and a use remove the item they sit on; cancelling the dialog of a rename or of a write that changes other plateaus gives the form back as typed; Move asks which views would break in the plateaus beside the one drawn, as a rename does; a saved file keeps its byte-order mark, and a comment after the last line of a moved block goes with it (see [editor-rename], Moving an element under another parent); and the forms of the centre no longer go past a window 360 pixels wide (see The editor on a small screen).

What changes for a reader who knows 0.14.0. Since that release, these things moved; the first two can change a file or an error that a workspace had:

  1. A saved Map of the technology layer draws more. It draws the applications deployed on the open nodes and the dependencies between environments that they give, unless the view says applications: false or relationships: false (see [map-technology]). The views of a workspace that has no such map are written as they were, byte for byte, and so are the other viewpoints.

  2. A root container, component or node in a workspace that has plateaus is an error, with the same line in both tools (see [nesting]). A workspace with no plateau is read as it was.

  3. The band: Compare with shows the tree of the plateaus and has no entries Future any more (see The band of plateaus).

  4. The editor has the field Current plateau in Settings, J and K for the walk through the changes, the box of Add to story…​ that writes at and against, and a dialog before a rename that leaves a view broken in a plateau beside the one drawn (see The current plateau, Keyboard, Stories: stories and views and [editor-rename]).

  5. The command line: archicode diff --width <columns> wraps the text table (see Compare plateaus), and a plateau: in the header of a view or a story says nothing (see [plateau-untied]).

One more thing changed for a workspace that has plateaus and that marks its boxes by hand with a state word (rendering-stereotypes: "new" or "changed"). A word that agrees with the comparison is drawn as before. A word on an element that does not have that state used to give the box the default look of the state in the views of the command line, and the editor did not: it now gives it nothing in both, and the command says so on its error output (see [plateau-state-tags]). The files that a workspace without a state word writes are the same, byte for byte.

Limits of today

These are things that do not work yet, or not the way you might expect. Each is known, and the tools say so when they meet one. Those marked not planned will not be built; the others are known and not scheduled.

Plateaus.

  • The editor does not fold a plateau into the one above (not planned). Deleting a plateau that others override is one step and tells what the plateaus above lose, but no operation moves the patches and the elements of the deleted plateau into the plateau above, and the delete is refused when a step or a view would lose what it draws. archicode plateau fold does it from the command line (see Fold a plateau into the one above); [fold-by-hand] says how to do it by hand, for a plateau the command refuses.

  • The editor does not move an element a plateau inherits (a patch never changes an id or a parent): draw the plateau that declares it.

  • A comparison with the parent cannot stay on the parent. On the band, the parent in the tree of Compare with is read as Past (follows the plateau drawn), so the comparison moves to the new parent when another plateau is drawn. There is no fixed comparison with the parent.

  • Which plateau the editor and the command line draw first. The editor draws the plateau of the address, then the one this browser drew last, then settings.current (which Settings writes, see The current plateau), then the root; the command line draws the last plateau when the plateaus form one line and no settings.current says otherwise.

  • The editor and a large pair of plateaus. Finding what has nothing to draw and resolving the patches grow with the number of elements, not faster: for the 591 elements and 839 changes of a test pair (replaces-large-pair), in the page, the first call takes about 0.08 second, where the 82 elements of the layered Brightcart take 10 milliseconds. What still grows faster is the unfolded Map, which builds the view again for each box it could open. No shipped workspace is that large.

Reading and writing files.

  • The save of the editor writes a file again in its own layout in a few cases, with the comments lost and the aliases written as their values: an anchor or a tag on a key inside brackets ({&k zone: north}); a single pair in a list in brackets that also holds an anchor, an alias or a tag ([&a one: two]); a tagged text over several lines inside brackets; a list item whose map starts under its dash, nested inside another such item; and two block scalars that a list item holds in a form the save does not follow: - - | on one line (a block scalar that is the item of a list that is itself an item), and a folded block (- >) that the emitter would write with other line breaks, which read differently once written again. A list item that is a block scalar (- |, then its text) and a block scalar on the second key of a list item whose first key goes or comes are kept as written. The file is read and the model is what it says. The other layouts that were in this list in 0.17.0 are kept as written (see Elements).

  • A few comments are lost or move, and a list is wrapped in brackets, when the lists around them are nested again. Two cases, found by a generated test of the save, are left: a comment on an item that is itself a list (- then its items, or - [c, d] # second row) is lost, or taken by the item above, when the list around it is wrapped in another list or unwrapped; and a list that holds a list in brackets, wrapped in another list, is written in brackets as a whole. The value read is the same in each case; the layout, or a comment, is not what you wrote. (Up to 0.19.0 a block scalar that is a list item, and a block scalar on the second key of a map that is a list item, were in this list; they are kept now, see [upgrade-0-19].) In every other layout the generated test of the save covers, it finds no quote or comment lost on a line that an edit did not touch.

  • A collection in brackets that a change reaches is sometimes saved as a block. It stays in brackets, on its line, when it fits (see Elements); it becomes a block when it would be wider than the widest line of the file (100 to 120 columns), when a comment stands inside its brackets, when it is an empty [] or {} that the change fills, and when it has an anchor, an alias or a tag in it. A block in the file is never turned into brackets. The file reads the same either way; only its layout differs from what you wrote.

  • An anchor or a tag on a mapping key (&k name: x) is read by the command line and refused by the editor, which does not open the file and says an anchor or a tag on a key is not supported (not planned: a splice on a key could lose text, for a form nobody writes). The merge key << is no merge in either tool: it is read as a key named <<, which a manifest does not have, so both refuse it (not planned).

  • Differences in how the two tools read a number or a tag are left, and pinned (a plain scalar that YAML 1.1 reads as a number, a tagged number and a style number are read alike, see Upgrading: what breaks, and what does not). The editor does not tell the line and the column of a mistake in a file: it names the place by its path in the file, as the command line does (see Errors, warnings and exit codes), because it reads a file into a tree that has no positions. A float that JavaScript prints another way than Java (5.0) is the text in a field of any shape: a value of a patch is the text "5.0" in the editor, a number for the command line (the qualifiers it sets are texts in both). A !!binary text in a field that is a whole number, a boolean or an enum is the wrong shape (found a string) for the editor, and Cannot deserialize value of type …​ for the command line, which names a Java class. A few spellings that only Java defines differ in the sentence of the mistake (the digits of other scripts, a character that is not a base64 code point).

The command line.

  • archicode diff cannot compare with another version of the workspace in git (a --baseline-ref; not planned). --width, --fail-on and --only-changes are built (see Compare plateaus and Generate views). views generate writes no text table, so it has no --width.

The editor on a phone (not planned). Nobody has used the editor on a real phone: what the editor does in a window 360 pixels wide was measured in a desktop browser, and is told in The editor on a small screen. That includes the player of a story, which was measured at such a window but not tried on a phone: the finger that moves its diagram, the picker of its list of the steps, full screen on a phone and the on-screen keyboard are not checked.

Editor

ArchiCode comes with an editor to browse, edit and draw a workspace without leaving the browser. It is a single page, standalone.html, that reads and writes the files of a workspace folder: nothing is uploaded anywhere, the page and the files stay on your machine.

Opening the editor

There are two ways to open it:

  • Served by the command line. archicode editor serve serves the editor and opens the folder of the .archicode.yaml in it, with nothing to pick. It works in any browser. See Serve the editor.

  • From the published page. The page published next to this documentation, at /editor/ of the same site, works in Chrome, Edge, Brave, Opera, Firefox and Safari. With no folder to choose it keeps a workspace in the browser, and it still lets you pick a folder where the browser has the File System Access API (Chrome, Edge, Brave and Opera). To try it without a workspace of your own, open the editor on the Brightcart sample. See A workspace kept in the browser.

The folder to open is the one that holds the workspace file, .archicode.yaml. You pick the folder, not the file, so the hidden name is no obstacle in the dialog of the browser. A folder that has a workspace.yaml and no .archicode.yaml is not opened: the editor gives the reason beside the folder, with the sentence the command line prints (see [upgrade-0-17]).

When a folder cannot be opened, the editor says so on a card, Can’t open this workspace, and what it says depends on what went wrong with .archicode.yaml. If the file cannot be had at all (there is none, a folder stands in its place, or the browser or the server refuses to read it), the card says .archicode.yaml could not be read in “folder”, with the reason the browser gave and the hint to choose the root of the workspace. If the file is read and found wrong (a key that does not exist, YAML that is not valid, a file that is not a mapping, settings.plateaus, a path of manifests.paths or a facet name that is refused), the card shows the sentence the command line prints for it, as it is, without could not be read and without the hint: the file was read, and the sentence says what to change. The sentence for workspace.yaml is shown as it is as well. In the list of the remembered folders, the note Its .archicode.yaml could not be read the last time it was opened is given to a folder whose file could not be had, or is still named workspace.yaml, and not to one that failed for another reason.

The published page remembers, in the browser, every folder it opened, with the name its workspace gives itself (see Name of the workspace). When the page opens again, it opens the last folder directly if the browser still lets it read it; otherwise it asks for a click (Reopen), because a browser only gives that permission back on a click. Close folder, at the top right, closes the folder and shows the list of the remembered folders: the closed one stays in it, and the next visit does not open it by itself. When no folder is open, the list shows each folder by the name of its workspace, with the name of the folder below it when they differ, most recent first: a click on one opens it, Remove from the list makes the browser forget it, and nothing is deleted on disk. Under each folder, the list says what a click will meet, as the browser answers without asking anything: Opens without asking, Will ask for permission (the browser shows its prompt), Blocked by the browser, or Could not be reached / Could not be checked (the folder may have been moved or deleted). A folder picked again is not added twice, even by two tabs at the same moment: the browser then holds two records for it for an instant, and the editor merges them into one, keeping the name of its workspace and its place in the list. A folder that cannot be opened (the permission refused, the folder moved or deleted) stays in the list, which says why, until it is removed. Served by archicode editor serve, the editor has one folder, the one of the server: there is no list and no Close folder.

The editor loads elkjs (the diagram layout) and CodeMirror (the file editor) from a CDN when it opens. Without network access, it still works, with plainer diagrams and file editor.

In Settings, the paths of the manifests are one per line, and a path that ends in /** is read with the folders below it (see Manifests in folders); a new element, view or story is written in the folder of the first path. The editor served by archicode editor serve does not list the hidden entries of a folder at all, .archicode.yaml of the folder itself excepted (it is served at the root only, not in a sub-folder), so a path that names a hidden folder reads nothing there, and does not list a link to a file that points out of the folder, which the command line reads. A browser that lets the page read a folder gives no way to tell a link from a folder: the page stops at a folder that is one it is inside of, but reads a link to another folder, which the command line does not.

The editor reads and writes YAML manifests (.yaml and .yml) only. The command line reads JSON and TOML manifests too (see Manifest formats and kinds): the editor reads neither, so what such a file declares is missing from its trees and diagrams, and relationships that point at it show as dangling. It says so, in Files (which names the files) and above the trees of Elements (which counts them).

A reference declared more than once is reported, not chosen: a red note above the trees of Elements and of Files gives the same message as the command line, each file that takes part is marked duplicate in Files and says so when opened, and no diagram is drawn until each reference is declared once (a view or a story with a duplicated id is reported too, and does not stop the diagrams). Nothing else is blocked: the editor still edits and saves, so that the duplicate can be fixed in it.

A workspace kept in the browser

Where the browser has no folder picker, or when you prefer not to give the page a folder, the editor keeps a workspace in the browser. The card Open a workspace then offers Open the Brightcart sample, New workspace in this browser and Import a workspace file, next to Open workspace folder when the browser can pick one. The workspaces kept in the browser are listed under In this browser, each with Rename, Export and Delete; a name is 1 to 80 characters and is not used by another workspace.

A browser workspace lives in the data of the site, on this device only: nothing is uploaded. Clearing the data of the site, a private window or a browser that frees space can remove it, so the editor asks the browser to keep it (once, at the first save) and the top bar says In this browser and when the workspace was last exported. Nothing is stored until you click Save all changes. Keep a copy with Export as a file.

The sample. Open the Brightcart sample opens a copy of the layered Brightcart workspace, as the same workspace every time: the second click opens the one you already have, with your changes. The address /timeline/applications?plateau=marketplace&compare=past&sample=brightcart does the same, and it is what the button of the presentation page uses (the editor on the Brightcart sample). A workspace made from the sample has Reset to the sample…​ in its Workspace menu: it names the workspace and what is lost (your changes), and replaces the files with the sample’s, in one step.

Export and import. The Workspace menu of the top bar has Export as a file, which downloads one YAML file named after the workspace (brightcart-bundle.yaml for the sample): every YAML file of the workspace, each opened by a line --- # file: <path>. It waits while changes are not saved. A file the bundle cannot carry (a path that is absolute, hidden other than .archicode.yaml, leaves the folder or is not YAML, a file that is not UTF-8) is left out and listed. Import a workspace file on the card reads such a file and creates a new workspace from it, named after the file’s settings.name or else the file; it never changes an existing workspace. It refuses a file over 10 MB, one that is not UTF-8 text, and a path with .., an absolute path, a hidden file other than .archicode.yaml, or a path given twice, and then creates nothing.

Served by archicode editor serve, the page has the same Workspace menu, with Open the Brightcart sample and, once a browser workspace is open, Back to the served folder. Nothing in that menu writes to the served folder.

What is tested. The flow above is tested end to end in Chromium, Firefox and WebKit as Playwright builds it. That WebKit is not Safari: the real Safari, Windows and macOS are not tested.

The perspectives and their pages

The top bar has four buttons: Timeline, Stories, Files and Settings. Timeline has four pages, in a bar of their own under the top bar: Plateaus, Applications, Environments and Elements. A click on Timeline opens Applications, a click on one of the others opens its first page, and the page that is on screen is the pressed one. On the four pages of Timeline, and on those only, the band shows the plateau that is drawn and the one it is compared with (see The band of plateaus).

Page What it is Address

Plateaus

the plateaus as a tree, each with what it changes over the plateau it overrides; the page where a plateau is made, edited and deleted (see The Plateaus page)

/timeline/plateaus (and /plateaus)

Applications

the Map of the application layer, with no element selected (see Applications and Environments: the Map)

#/timeline/applications

Environments

the Map of the technology layer, with no element selected (see Applications and Environments: the Map)

#/timeline/environments

Elements

the trees of both layers, and the page and the diagrams of the element selected in them (see Elements); where an element is edited, created and deleted

#/timeline/elements

Stories

the stories and the views of the workspace, the same whatever plateau is drawn (see Stories: stories and views)

/stories and /stories/views

Files

every manifest of the workspace, in a group for each plateau (see Files)

#/files

Settings

the settings of the workspace (see Technical Configuration)

#/settings

Since 0.17 the pages answer to the addresses of the table above and to no other: /map, /elements, /library, /library/stories, /library/views, /diagram, /applications, /plateaus, /environments and /timeline alone are no routes any more, and open the default page, Applications, like any address the editor does not know. Replace them with /timeline/applications, /timeline/elements, /stories, /stories/views, /timeline/plateaus and /timeline/environments. The page writes its own address in place of the one that was typed, without adding an entry to the history of the browser. The parameters of an address (ref, tab, pin, facet, story, view, plateau…​) are the ones they were.

The address says the plateau. A link with plateau=<id> draws that plateau, and compare= the comparison it names (see The address and the trees); an address with no plateau= is the root, baseline, which the page never writes. This is also true once the editor is open: an address that is typed in the tab, followed, or reached by Back and Forward draws the plateau it names (an unknown plateau leaves the one that is drawn). A plateau that you choose, on the band or on the page Plateaus, is an entry of the history, so Back walks back through the plateaus you drew. A comparison chosen on the band is an entry of the history too, and the address says it (compare=, see What a link carries), so Back walks back through the comparisons of the same plateau.

Environments is a Map like Applications, on the technology layer: the tree of environments and nodes on the left, the pins, the eye, the chips, the Changes panel and Name view…​ are the ones of Applications. Unlike Applications, it draws the applications deployed on the nodes and the relationships between environments, and its tools can leave each of them out (see Applications and Environments: the Map). The page Elements keeps the tree of both layers, and is where an environment or a node is edited and created, as an application element is. Show on map, on an element of either layer, opens the Map of that layer with the element pinned, and a row of the Changes panel about the other layer leaves the Map on screen for the Map of that layer.

Settings has no section for plateaus: a workspace of layers writes its plateaus in its manifests (see The Plateaus page), and has the paths of its manifests, as a workspace with no plateau does. It has one field about them, Current plateau, which is the key settings.current (see The current plateau). The rest of this chapter says the Map for what Applications and Environments draw, one for each layer. A workspace that writes no layer has the same perspectives and the same pages, and its band has one plateau. Its files are read, drawn and saved as they were.

Elements

The Elements page is the tree of both layers of the workspace on the left, and the selected element in the center. The center has four tabs:

  • Details: the fields, qualifiers, tags and relationships of the element, with Edit to change them (change its id and every reference to it follows; Move, in the edit form, puts it under another parent, see Moving an element under another parent), and Delete (the relationships of other elements that pointed at it are listed first, so that none is left dangling by surprise).

  • Overview, Detailed and Deep: the viewpoints of the same names, drawn live from the model, as the command line generates them. Detailed is for an element that can contain others. Clicking a box in a diagram selects that element.

Renaming an element in a workspace of plateaus. Saving the form of an element with another id rewrites every reference to it. In a workspace that has plateaus the rewrite is made in the files of the plateau that is drawn and of the plateaus that stand on it, since the others do not see the element through it, and in the views and the stories of the workspace, which belong to no plateau and so follow everywhere. A plateau beside the one drawn (one that does not stand on it and does not override it) may still declare the element, or inherit it, under its old reference: a view or a step that names the new reference then cannot be drawn there any more. When that is so, saving the form first opens a dialog before anything is written. It names the plateaus that keep the old reference and the views and the steps of the stories (by story and number) that could no longer be drawn in them, and has two buttons: Cancel, which writes nothing, and Rename anyway, which writes what a rename always wrote. To keep every view whole, cancel, draw each of those plateaus and give the element the same new id there; once every plateau that has it uses the new reference, the views are drawn in all of them. The links and highlight of a Map view and of a story step name two references each, and a rename or a move rewrites them as it rewrites pinned, hidden and opened (the label never changes). Nothing is asked when no view or step is at risk, when the plateau drawn is baseline (every plateau stands on it), or in a workspace with no plateau.

Cancelling. Cancel (or Escape, or a click beside the dialog) writes nothing and gives the form back as you typed it: the new id, the name, the description, what was in the boxes that add a qualifier or a tag, the relationship rows and the replaces, and Save all changes is lit as before. The same goes for the dialog that lists the plateaus a write changes (Writing in baseline changes other plateaus, see Writing in a plateau). Saying yes writes the form that was typed. A write that the editor refuses (Not written: …​), a write that fails after you said yes to the dialog of the plateaus, and an error while it reads the form give the form back as typed too, beside the sentence that tells why: nothing you typed is lost.

Move changes a reference too, and the views and the steps follow it in every plateau, so it asks the same question when you press the button of its dialog with a parent chosen and the move is allowed: the same list of plateaus that keep the old reference and of views and steps that could no longer be drawn there, in words of a move, with the buttons Back to Move and Move anyway. Back to Move, Escape and a click beside the dialog give the Move dialog back with its parent and its search as they were, and write nothing, so that you can choose another parent. A move that no sibling plateau is concerned by (none keeps the old reference, or no view or step names it there), a move in baseline and a move in a workspace with no plateau are written at once, as they were.

The badges and colours of styled stereotypes, on the boxes and in the tree, are explained in Stereotypes and styles.

Changes stay in memory, with a mark on the tree for each modified element, until Save all changes writes them. A file that changed on disk since the editor read it is not overwritten: the save is refused and says which one. A file that is saved keeps what the changes did not touch: its comments, its blank lines, the quotes of the values, its final line break and, for a file that starts with one, its UTF-8 byte-order mark (.archicode.yaml as well as the manifests; a browser never hands the mark to the page, so the editor reads the first three bytes of the file to know it, and puts the mark back when it writes; a file the editor creates has none). Only the lines that changed are rewritten, and a value written anew (a renamed id, a new parent, a reference that follows, a replaces) keeps the quoting of the line it replaces, the way its key is written and its trailing comment; a line that is added is written like the lines around it (double quotes, single quotes or none, as the file does), and plain when the file quotes nothing. A value that comes back on a line that is new (a map in brackets that becomes a list of maps, an item that moves, a list that gives back its item) keeps the quotes it had, and so does a key; a comment after a line stays on the line of its own key or item when that line is written again, and removing the first of two items that read the same removes the first, with its comment. A comment on a line of its own between two lines of an item that is removed stays where it was. A list or a map written in square or curly brackets (replaces: [a, b], tags: {zone: north}, on one line or on several, nested, as an item of a list of maps) stays as written when the change does not reach it; when a change reaches it, it is written in brackets again, on the line where it started: qualifiers: [a, b] that gets a c is qualifiers: [a, b, c], a map in curly brackets that gets a key stays a map in curly brackets, and so does an item of a list of maps (- {destination: x}). The comment after the closing bracket, the quotes of the values and of the keys, the words of the booleans (on, yes) and the spacing inside the brackets ([ a ] or [a]) are the ones you wrote, except for a value the change sets; a collection that you had over several lines goes on one line when it fits, and one that a change empties is [] or {}. It fits when its line, the comment included, is no wider than the widest line of the file, but at least 100 and at most 120 columns, or no wider than it already was. A collection stays a block, as the editor has always written it, in four cases: it no longer fits; it has a comment inside its brackets (the comments stay as lines of the block, a comment above a value above it, a comment after a value at the end of its line); it is an empty [] or {} that the change fills (the emitter’s own spelling for nothing, which a new element or story has); or it has an anchor, an alias or a tag in it. A collection that is a block in the file is never turned into brackets. The same words (yes, no, on, off, ~) and a key with no value (description:) stay as written in the lines the change does not reach, and a comment or a blank line above a line that is rewritten stays above it. A list written at the same column as its key (tags: then - a at the same column) is followed too: its items and comments stay, and an item that is added is written at the column the list uses; the comment after [], {} or a key with no value stays on the line when a change fills the collection, and the comments inside empty brackets stay under the key. A manifest that uses a YAML anchor, an alias or a tag is read by the editor and by the command line the same way: an alias (description: *n) is the value of the anchor it points at (name: &n "Bakery"), not its name, and a tag qualifies a scalar (!!str 12 is the text 12; !!int, !!float, !!bool and !!null read it as such, any other tag as a text; a tagged number is read as the command line reads it: !!int 0x1F is 31, !!int 1:30 stays the text 1:30 and !!binary aGVsbG8= stays the text aGVsbG8=). A merge key (<<: *base) is not a merge: it is a key named <<. The anchor, the alias and the tag stay as written in the lines a change does not touch, and a line a change rewrites keeps its anchor and its tag; an alias stays an alias as long as the line still says what its anchor says, and is written as its value when a change makes them differ. An anchor, an alias or a tag inside square or curly brackets (qualifiers: [&a oven, *a]), a text that goes over several lines (tagged or not) and a list item whose map starts on the line under its dash (-, then its keys, with or without an anchor) stay as written as well: every line a change does not reach is the line of the file, byte for byte. A collection in brackets that a change reaches is written as a block that keeps its anchors, aliases and tags; an anchored value that a change edits keeps its anchor, and each alias of it is written as the value it had, since it would now say the new one. An anchor or a tag on a key (&k name: x) is read by the command line and refused by the editor, which does not open the file and says so (see Limits of today). A few layouts are still written again entirely in the editor’s own layout, with the comments lost and the aliases written as their values, and Limits of today lists them; so is a file laid out in a way the editor does not write (an indentation other than two spaces, for example).

Applications and Environments: the Map

The Applications page, which the editor called the Map until wave 014, draws the whole application layer, with no element selected, and the Environments page draws the whole technology layer the same way, with the same tools: one Map for each layer. The tree on the left of each lists the elements of its layer only (Environments in the title of the tree of the technology Map), the pins, hidden elements and open boxes of a layer are that Map’s own (a reference can exist in both layers, and a pin of one means nothing in the other), and a technology element is the pin of the Map of its layer. A Map opens folded: only the top of the layer shows (the solutions and systems, or the environments), and a link is drawn between the boxes its two ends land on, so opening a box splits the links that reached it among its children.

In the tree on the left, each row has two buttons, which appear when the pointer is over the row or the row has the focus:

  • Pin (the pin) keeps only what touches the element. Pin several to keep what touches any of them. With nothing pinned, the whole layer is drawn.

  • Hide (the eye) leaves the element, what is inside it and their relationships out of the map.

The pinned and hidden elements are also shown as chips over the map, each removable, with Clear all once there are more than two. In the tree, rows of the elements the map does not draw are greyed. Folding the tree does not change the boxes of the map: use Fold all and Unfold all in the toolbar of the map, or the buttons of the boxes. Hide unlinked leaves out the boxes that no link reaches; it is in the toolbar of the diagrams of Elements too, where the element itself always stays. It is in the URL (hide-unlinked=1) and in a saved view. A saved view of a Map keeps the layer it draws, and the command line draws it too (see [map-viewpoint]).

Drawing some relationships only. Under the tree of the page Applications, a section Relationships lists the relationships of the plateau drawn (and, in a comparison, those of the plateau compared with, so that a removed one can be ticked), once each, sorted by source, destination and label. Tick some to draw only them, with the boxes at their two ends and what encloses them, as the links of a Map do (see [map-links]); with nothing ticked the whole map is drawn. A field filters the list by source, destination, kind or text, a list limits it to a kind, Ticked only keeps the ticked rows, and Clear (or the button of the chip Relationships: n over the diagram) ticks nothing again. When a link of the diagram is selected, its panel lists the relationships it stands for, each with a check box, and, while nothing is ticked, a button Draw only this link. The choice is view state, not a change of the workspace: it is in the address (rel=, once for each relationship, a JSON list of the source, the destination and the label when there is one; a relationship that no model has is dropped), and a saved view of the Map keeps it as its links, as the other choices of the Map are kept, written as the command line reads them. The Map of the technology layer has no such section and refuses links. When an element that a ticked relationship names is renamed or moved, the entry follows it in the saved views and in the steps of the stories.

Highlighting a relationship. The panel of a selected link (see The inspection panel) has a Highlight box beside Drawn, for each relationship it lists, and the chip over the diagram says how many are highlighted (Highlight: n, with a cross that draws every relationship calmly again; Clear all does the same). A highlighted relationship is drawn heavy, with a ▶ before its caption and the caption in bold; the other links of the map are drawn calm, thinner, lighter and grey, so that the colour is not the only sign. The highlight is kept in the address as hl= (one parameter for each entry, as rel= is for the ticked relationships), and a relationship that the drawing does not show (hidden, inside a closed box, or left out by the ticked ones) marks nothing and stays in the list. The Map of the technology layer has no highlight, and Keep as named view keeps the highlight of the diagram as it keeps the ticked relationships (see Stories: stories and views).

What Environments draws besides the technology elements. The Map of the technology layer also draws the applications deployed on the nodes, a box each inside its node, and the relationships: the ones the technology elements declare, and the dependencies between environments that the deployed applications give (an application of an environment that depends on an application hosted in an environment or a node that the first uses). Both are what the command line draws for the same view (see [map-technology], which says exactly which). A node that hosts applications can be opened, with its chevron or Unfold all, and only an open node shows them, so the Map opens as folded as before. A click on an application box selects the application, in the application layer: the panel on the right is the one of that application. An application is not pinned, hidden or opened on its own: pin or hide the node. The group Applications and Relationships in the tools of the diagram turns each of the two parts off and on (a button is lit while its part is drawn): Applications off leaves the application boxes out and the links between the nodes that host them stay, Relationships off leaves every link out. The choice is in the URL, as what is left out only (applications=0, relationships=0), and it is what a saved Map view says in its properties (applications: false, relationships: false; the same two buttons, on the page of a saved view of the technology Map, edit the view like the texts do). It is a choice of the view, not of the model: it writes nothing to the workspace, and it is not kept when you go to another page. On the Map of the application layer there is no such group.

Center puts the selected box in the middle at a readable size (with nothing selected on the Map: the top of the map, centered across), never below 80%, and Fit shows the whole diagram, however small. Fit is in the URL (frame=fit) and Center, how a diagram opens, is a URL with no frame, so a link opens the diagram framed as you left it; a zoom or a move of your own is not part of the link. PNG and SVG, in the header, download the diagram.

The inspection panel

Beside a diagram, on the right, a panel shows the data of the selected element without leaving the view: its name and styled stereotypes, its kind, its layer, its id and reference, its description, qualifiers and tags, and the file that declares it. It is there on the Map (Applications and Environments), on the Overview, Detailed and Deep tabs of Elements (where a click on a box moves to that element, and the panel follows) and on the page of a view in Stories (where a click on a box selects it on the page only, see Stories: stories and views); it is not on Details and in the edit form, which show the same things themselves. Nothing is shown, and no room is kept, while nothing is selected. The panel is resized by dragging its edge (the width is remembered; a double click on the edge gives the default back), it goes away with Maximize, and on the Map its x button deselects the element. On the Map the panel says so when the selected element is not drawn (a pin, a hidden element or a folded box leaves it out), and when it is an element of the other layer, which the Map never draws (This map draws the technology layer only. on Environments, The map draws applications only. on Applications). A click does what it did before: on the Map it selects, in Elements it moves to the element.

A click on a link of the diagram, or on its caption, selects it instead, and the panel lists each relationship the link stands for: its label, its qualifiers and its tags (tcp-port, doc-exchange…​), with the element that declares it (a button: it selects that element, in the layer of the drawing, as a click on its box does) and the one it points at. What is selected is the pair of boxes the link joins, so a link that groups several relationships lists them all (and says so), a link between folded boxes names the folded end and how many elements it hides (open it to see the relationships one by one), and a link to an unknown element says it points at nothing. The links of that pair are outlined. When several links join the same two boxes, the panel says how many, and marks (with the words Clicked link) the relationships of the one you clicked, unless they cannot be told from the others'. The links of a diagram are one stop of the Tab key, however many there are, and so are its boxes (Tab goes from one group to the other): once on a link, the arrow keys move to the next and the previous link, in the order the diagram draws them, and Home and End go to the first and the last. Enter or Space selects the link that has the focus. The stop is where the focus last was among the links, or else the selected link, or else the first one; for the boxes it is where the focus last was, or else the selected box, or else the one in focus of the diagram, or else the first one. x or Escape deselects: on the Map the panel goes, in Elements the panel of the element comes back. Selecting a link changes neither the perspective nor the tab, and in Elements the element stays the one being looked at (the tree, the tabs and the URL keep it). On the Map, if the panel that appears leaves the link behind its edge, the view moves by the least it takes to show it, once; a link already in view does not move the view. The selected link is in the URL, after the other parameters of the view (link-from=customer&link-to=shop: the two boxes of the drawing), so a copied URL, a reload and Back or Forward bring it back, and bring the view to it if it is not in sight. Selecting a link rewrites the current entry of the history, like a zoom, and does not add one. A link the drawing does not have when the URL is opened (it was renamed, or the boxes it joins are folded differently, which the URL does not carry) is dropped, and the URL with it. The selection ends as soon as the diagram it was made on is no longer the one on screen (another perspective, Details, another element, or a drawing that no longer has the link: a fold, a pin, a facet). A click on a box does what it did before and ends the link selection. A relationship the diagram does not draw (secondary links between deployed applications, unless a facet asks for them) cannot be selected.

Since the panel takes room from the center, the header of the diagram goes to its narrower states sooner (see The header of a diagram): resize or close the panel to give it back.

The header of a diagram

The header over a diagram, on the tabs of Elements and on the Map, adapts to the width of the center panel, which is the window minus the left panel, so dragging the left panel changes it. From wide to narrow:

  • Wide: every control shows its words.

  • Narrower: Show on map (or Open in Elements on the Map), Name view…​, Add to story…​ and Maximize show only their icon; the tooltip says what each one does.

  • Narrower still: Show on map or Open in Elements, the Story buttons and the Download buttons (PNG, SVG) move into the menu of a "⋯" button, which closes with Escape or a click elsewhere. The facet list and Maximize stay in the header. The menu items work exactly like the buttons they replace.

  • Very narrow: the header takes two rows.

  • The key of a comparison is one row that scrolls along under 700 pixels (see Comparing two plateaus).

The editor on a small screen

In a window narrower than 40 rem (640 pixels at the usual size) the regions of the editor are stacked one under the other, and the page scrolls from one to the next. It was measured in a desktop browser (the browser pane of the development tool), at 360 by 800 pixels, in the light and in the dark theme, on the layered Brightcart and on the example domain_application, with a script that walks every page and form and compares the right edge of every box with the width of the window, and the screenshots of the pages were looked at:

What Measured

the top bar

105 pixels high

the band of the plateaus (the pages of Plateaus, Applications, Environments and Elements)

80 pixels, two lists of one line each

the tabs of the pages

39 pixels

the tree / the center / the inspection

up to 240 / 640 / up to 400 pixels (30, 80 and 50 percent of the height of an 800 pixel window)

the width of the document, on Plateaus, Applications, Environments, Elements, Stories, Files and Settings

360 pixels on all seven

the widest control of Settings, the list Current plateau included

ends at 332 pixels

the story player (below), in a window 360 pixels wide, dark scheme only

the page is 360 pixels wide, no horizontal scroll

The top bar row of the table is the measure it had before #182 and is left as it is. The bar of a browser workspace, with the Workspace menu of its name, was measured again after the fix, on the sample, in a window of 360 pixels and of 1280 pixels, in the light and the dark scheme, with the menu open: at 360 pixels the bar is 141 pixels high, its buttons wrap onto as many rows as they need and every one lies inside the window, the document is as wide as the window, and the menu is 328 pixels wide, inside the window; at 1280 pixels the bar is 48 pixels high and the menu is 336 pixels wide. In the menu each item holds its label and its note: Export as a file with its note is 48 pixels high and Reset to the sample is 29 pixels, and the items do not overlap. Before the fix the note of Export as a file ran out of its item and the bar ran past the window, so the page scrolled sideways (see [upgrade-0-21]).
