The section Relationships of the page Applications (see [editor-map-links]) wraps its filter, its kind list and Ticked only in the width of the tree, which is stacked above the diagram in this layout, and its long references wrap inside the rows. The controls of the highlight (the Highlight boxes, their chip, and the Highlight fields of a saved view and of a story step, see [editor-map-links] and Stories: stories and views) came with run 0200 and are not in the table above. The Columns list of the toolbar (see The boxes per row) came with run 0202 and is not in the table above either.

Nothing is wider than the window, on the seven pages, on 52 states of the Brightcart in each theme (the forms of ten elements in view, edit, Move and delete, three stories, seven views) and on 20 of domain_application; the diagram is drawn to scale and scrolls in its own box, and the code editor of Files scrolls inside itself, as they do on a large screen. Four places did go past the window inside the scroll of the center and are fixed: the row that adds a tag, the four buttons in the header of an element, the list of saved views of a story, and the lists of the references in Confirm delete. The list of the places not offered in the dialog of Move has text wider than its box and scrolls inside it.

The walk row of the Changes panel (see The Changes panel) is sticky at this width too: Previous change, Next change, the position and Show all stay under the tabs while the list scrolls, and a press brings the selected row into view with the nearest scroll (block: nearest) that shows it. The row’s height was measured at 360 by 640 pixels, as a share of the panel’s height, against the 35 percent limit of the design, and the figure is in the record of the change (run 0201). Safari was not tested, so whether the row sticks the same way there is not proved.

The story player (see [editor-story-player]) is not a stacked layout either: it is a window of its own, as high and as wide as the browser window, and nothing in it makes the page scroll sideways. Its bar (the title, "Step n of N", the list of the steps, Previous, Next, Full screen and Close) wraps onto as many lines as it needs (four at 360 pixels), the plateau and the comparison are one line under it, the diagram takes the room that is left and is moved with a finger or the mouse inside its own box, the status line and the folded key under a comparison are one row that scrolls along, and the words of the step scroll in their own box. It was looked at in a desktop browser (the browser pane of the development tool), on the example domain_plateaus, in a window 360 pixels wide in the dark scheme, and the page was as wide as the window (scrollWidth 360), the diagram, the status and the words readable, zoom, Fit, Center and the wheel moving the drawing, and the open key capped and scrolling inside itself; the wide window in the light scheme was looked at as well, the same way (the first build of the player, before the cap on the key went from 30 to 24 percent of the height). The player is tested with a fake layout in the automated tests, which do not see pixels.

What this does not say: whether the order of the stacked regions and their heights suit a phone, whether the buttons are easy to touch, and how the on-screen keyboard behaves. They are a judgment on a real device, which nobody has made and which is not planned (see Limits of today). The same goes for the player: nobody has tried a finger on its diagram, its list of the steps (a native list, so that the phone shows its own picker), Full screen on a phone, a screen reader on the announcement of a step, or the player in a folder picked in the browser (the File System Access path; a served folder was tried). Full screen itself was not seen working in a real browser: the pane used for the look refuses the request, so the grant is tested in the automated tests only.

The texts of a diagram

Four buttons in the toolbar of a diagram, in the group Show, choose what the boxes and the links say. A lit button is a text that is drawn:

  • Labels: the caption of each link, with its qualifiers in brackets. A long caption does not run over the boxes next to it: it is broken at the spaces into lines of at most 220 pixels (about forty characters at the size of the diagram), centred on the link, and the layout leaves room for all the lines. A word that is wider than a line is cut with an ellipsis, as a name is. The qualifiers are kept in one piece as far as possible, at the end of the last line when they fit and on a line of their own when they do not: the label gives way to them rather than the other way round. They are cut only past two lines without Wrap, or when a single qualifier is wider than a line (then its closing bracket is kept). Without Wrap a caption has at most four lines, and an ellipsis replaces the end of the label. The caption is broken whether Wrap is lit or not, and the tooltip and the accessible name of the link always have all of it. The command line breaks the captions of its PlantUML views too, in narrower lines (PlantUML’s own wrapWidth of 200).

  • Qualifiers: the qualifiers of each element, after its kind, as the command line writes them: [container: rest, public].

  • Descriptions: the description of each element that does not contain others, under its kind, up to three lines.

  • Wrap: long names, qualifiers and descriptions are broken over several lines, and the box gets taller, instead of being cut with an ellipsis. A box that contains others cuts the qualifiers of its header at any rate. The captions of the links, which are always broken, then show all their lines instead of four at most.

The diagram of an element starts with the labels and the qualifiers, as the command line draws them. The Map starts with none of them, to stay readable. Only a choice that differs from that is remembered: it is in the URL (labels=0, qualifiers=1, descriptions=1, wrap=1), and in a saved view (see Stories: stories and views), so that a link or a step of a story draws the same texts. The tooltip of a box always has the qualifiers and the whole description, and the SVG that is downloaded is the diagram as it is on screen.

The boxes per row

Columns, in the toolbar of a diagram, chooses how many boxes a row of its layout aims at. The list begins with Default, which names the value the diagram gets from the levels below it (Default (4), or Default (none) when there is none), then the numbers 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 8 and 12; a value that is none of these, such as a 7 written in a view, is listed too, in its place. On the Applications and Environments pages and on the page of an element it changes the diagram on screen and writes nothing. On the page of a saved view it changes the view, which is then modified and saved with Save all changes, as the texts are.

The choice is a layout with one key, columns. The value in force is the first one that is set, looked for in this order:

  1. the list of the toolbar, for the diagram on screen;

  2. the layout of the saved view, in its properties;

  3. the layout that the workspace gives its viewpoint, in settings.views.properties.<viewpoint>.layout, for overview, detailed, deep or map;

  4. none, which is the layout the editor has always drawn.

With nothing set, nothing moves. A diagram that aims at N boxes per row asks the layout engine for layers of at most N boxes, on the diagram and in each box that holds other boxes, so that boxes no link joins do not all fall in one long row. It is an aim and not a limit: when links join boxes, the layout can need more than N boxes in a row, and then it draws them so.

The value is a whole number from 1 to 20. A layout that is not a mapping, a key other than columns, or a columns out of that range is not guessed at: the diagram is not drawn, and its place says why, for instance the view shop_map has an invalid layout: columns must be a whole number from 1 to 20 (found 0). The page Settings refuses the same sentence at its field when you save the value of overview, detailed or deep. It has no field for the Map: a Map draws with the value of the workspace when it is drawn, and a wrong one is told there, on the Map. The first level that is set decides, valid or not: a wrong value of the view is told even when the workspace has a good one.

A story step has no toolbar: it takes the value of its saved view, else that of the workspace, as the editor does on the page of a view, so the player draws the same boxes as the editor for the same view. Keep as named view writes the value of the toolbar as the view’s layout, and nothing when the diagram takes the value of the workspace; a rename or a move leaves the layout of a view alone. The value of the toolbar is in the address as layout=N (see the table of the address), so a reload of the page or a copied URL carries it, on the page of an element and on both Maps; a saved view and the workspace keep theirs in their files.

The command line does not read layout. The views it writes are the same with or without it, and the key is one of the view properties it ignores (see [map-viewpoint] and Print the guide for an agent).

Stories: stories and views

The Stories perspective, which the editor called the Library until wave 014, keeps what the workspace says about itself, on two sides: Stories (see Stories) and Views, the named diagrams that the stories are made of. Each side has its list on the left and its page in the center, and they lead to one another: the view of a step is a link to that view, and a view lists the steps that show it. The URL says which side is open (/stories and /stories/views; the former /library/stories and /library/views still open them). The page lists the same stories and the same views whatever plateau is drawn: a story and a view belong to no plateau (see [plateau-untied]), so there is no list per plateau and no note about other plateaus, and a story or a view is edited, renamed and deleted from any plateau. A new story or view is a manifest in the first folder of manifests.paths, with no plateau: in its header, in a workspace of plateaus as in one without. A diagram on the Applications page, on the Environments page or in Elements has two buttons in its header:

  • Name view…​ keeps the diagram as a view, in a file of its own (view.<id>.yaml): the element and the viewpoint (Elements), or what is pinned, hidden and open, and the layer (Map), with the facet, Hide unlinked, the relationships ticked and highlighted of a Map of the application layer (links and highlight, see [editor-map-links]) and the texts it draws (and, for the Map of the technology layer, applications: false and relationships: false when one of them is turned off). It keeps the recipe, not a picture: the view is drawn again from the model each time. A named view can be put in several stories, and generated by the command line.

  • Add to story…​ puts the diagram in a story, as a step after the one being told, or at the end. A dialog asks which story (a list to pick from, or the name of a new one: a name that is already a story’s is that story). The step carries its own view (no extra file), unless the diagram is one that was named: then it points at it. In a workspace that has plateaus the dialog has a box that keeps the plateaus in the step: Tell the step at to-be, compared with baseline`. It is checked when a comparison is on screen, and not otherwise. Checked, the new step is written with `at (the plateau drawn) and against (the plateau compared with), as the plateau ids they are now: a comparison with the Past, which follows the plateau drawn, is written as the plateau that it is at that moment, and the dialog says so. Unchecked, the step has no plateau key and is drawn as the reader has it, as before. Whatever the box, a highlight of the diagram is written as the step’s own highlight key, when there is one, and the relationships ticked stay in the recipe’s properties.links (see [story-of-a-flow]). A comparison that has no plateau to name (the plateau drawn itself, a plateau that is gone) leaves against out, and the dialog says why; plateaus that cannot be drawn offer no box. A workspace with no plateau has no box.

Show on diagram, on a step of a story on the Stories side, draws that step on the editing diagram, on the page that shows its view, and raises a bar over the diagram that says where you are: the title of the story, "Step n of N", the plateau and the comparison the step names, and the words of the step, to read. Edit text turns the words into a box to type in (it opens by itself right after Add to story…​, so that you can write as you go), Edit story goes to the story, Back to this step draws the saved view again when you moved away from it, and Leave story closes the bar. The bar has no Previous, Next or list of the steps: press the Show on diagram of another step, or play the story (below). A step that cannot be drawn (its element was deleted) says why in the bar, which stays, over the diagram that was there. The story is in the URL (story= and step=), so a link or Back lands on the same step.

Playing a story. Play, in the header of a story on the Stories side (off for a story that has no step), opens the story at its first step in a window of its own, on top of the editor, to be read like slides. The window covers the browser window and the editor behind it cannot be reached while it is open: the focus stays inside (Tab and Shift+Tab go round its controls), and closing it gives the focus back to the Play that opened it. It is a dialog with the story as its name, and each step is said to a screen reader when it changes, through a live region that nothing redraws: "Step n of N", then the name of the view, then cannot be shown and the reason when it cannot, and, for a step that highlights a relationship, Highlighted with its two ends and its label (. Highlighted: <source> to <destination>, <label>.).

The slide holds, in a bar, the title of the story, "Step n of N" with the name of the view, the list of the steps (an entry for each, its number and a title; a story of one step has no list), Previous and Next, Full screen and Close. In a workspace that has plateaus a line under it says the plateau the step is drawn at and, when it has one, the plateau it is compared with: it is said, not chosen, and when the step names none it says (from the editor), since a step that names none is drawn at the plateau, and with the comparison, that the editor had when Play was pressed, which stay that way for as long as the window is open. Under the line comes the diagram, which can be zoomed (the buttons, or Ctrl or Cmd with the wheel, around the pointer), moved (a drag, or the wheel) and put back by Fit and Center, but not clicked: a click on a box or a link selects nothing and opens no panel. A step opens at a readable size, from the top of its drawing. Under the diagram are the status line, the key of the comparison when one is drawn (folded, Key opens it and a long one scrolls inside itself) and the words of the step. A step that cannot be drawn (its element is not in its plateau, its plateau is none, it is compared with itself) says why where the diagram would be, and the way on stays open: the list, Previous and Next still work, and the entry of that step in the list says cannot be shown and the reason. The bar of Show on diagram says the same with cannot be shown.

Each step is drawn from the model of its own plateau, on the side, with its highlight: the step’s own highlight replaces the view’s, and an empty one switches it off, so that the heavy link and the calm links are those of the step (see [story-of-a-flow]). The player reads the editor (the model, the story, the plateau and comparison it had when Play was pressed) and writes nothing to it: when the window closes, the plateau drawn, the comparison, the selection, the page, the address and the files are what they were, and nothing is marked as changed. The Changes only and wording switches of the editor are not read; a comparison is drawn with all its boxes.

Full screen asks the browser to give the whole screen to the window. A browser that has no full screen does not show the button; one that refuses leaves the window as it is and says The browser did not give full screen. under the bar; closing the window leaves full screen.

The keys are the arrow keys: Left and Right show the previous and the next step, Home and End the first and the last, and Esc closes the window, from the list of the steps too (the list owns the arrows, Home and End, and lets Esc through). Up and Down are not taken, so that nothing competes with the scroll of a long text. No other key acts while a control that types or owns the arrows has the focus (a field, a text box, a list), while a dialog, the help or the menu of the header is open (then Esc is theirs), or with Ctrl, Alt, Meta or Shift held; J and K of the changes do nothing in the window, which has no list of changes.

The window is in the address, as play=<story>&step=n after the keys of the editor, n counted from 0 as step= is. Opening it is an entry of the history; choosing another step is not (the entry is changed in place), so Back closes the window and Forward opens it again at the step it was left on; Close and Esc go Back when the history holds that entry, so that Forward brings it back. A link with play= opens it when the folder is loaded, for someone who has the folder open (archicode editor serve, or a folder picked in the browser), at the plateau the address names; it is then no entry of the history, and closing it writes the address in place. The story= and step= of the bar are not in the address while the window is open, so there is one step=. A story that is not there, a story with no step, a step that is missing, is not a number or does not exist, and a workspace that has no story leave the window closed, and the page writes the address it really is. It is not a page that exists outside the editor: the editor reads a folder, so a link opens nothing for someone who has none, and there is no export of a story as a document. The player reads; to change a story, Edit story, the page of a story and the story editor are as they were.

A step told at a plateau. A step that says at or against (see [story-step-plateaus]) is drawn at its plateau and with its comparison in the player (above), without the editor changing. With Show on diagram it draws its plateau and its comparison before it draws its view, in the whole editor: the band, the Changes panel and the diagram are the ones of that plateau, and it is the step, not the plateau, that is an entry of the history. The bar says told at To be, compared with As is. With Show on diagram, a step that names none is drawn as the reader had it when the story began; a plateau or a comparison that the reader chooses on the band in the middle of the story is the new home of the steps that name none. Back to this step is offered when the plateau or the comparison on screen is not the step’s, and Leave story closes the bar and changes nothing: the diagram stays as it is. A name that the plateau of the step lacks is no mistake of the story: the step says The element “x” is not in the plateau “p”. and cannot be drawn. A step whose plateau is none, or cannot be drawn, changes nothing, says why in the bar, and stays.

On the Stories side, a story is open as soon as there is one, and edited in place: its name, its text, the order of its steps (Up, Down, Remove, which can be undone right away) and the words of each step. Each step also has a Highlight field, which edits its own highlight key: the relationships it highlights, as pills, and a list to add one. A note under it says that the key replaces the highlight of the view and that an empty list switches it off. Removing the last one leaves highlight: [] only when the view the step names has a highlight to switch off, and otherwise deletes the key. In a workspace that has plateaus, each step has two lists, Told at (the plateau drawn, by default) and Compared with (nothing, by default): a choice that is the default takes the key out of the step, a plateau that is none is listed and flagged (not a plateau) so that it is not lost, and Keep as named view keeps the two keys with the step. The step that is showing on the diagrams is marked. Play opens the story in the player, from its first step (see [editor-story-player]), and Show on diagram draws one step on the editing diagram. A step that carries its own view says so, and Keep as named view gives it a name and a file; a step whose view was deleted offers to point at another one. Renaming an element updates the views and steps that point at it. A change that is not saved yet is marked modified.

On the Views side, the page of a view that can be drawn draws it in the center, from the model, with the canvas and the tools of the Map and Elements: zoom, Center, Fit, Hide unlinked, the four texts (see The texts of a diagram), and PNG and SVG in the header. The facet of the view is applied: it is drawn here, not picked. There is no folding on this page, since the open boxes of a Map view are part of the view. The zoom and the moves are the page’s own and are not changes to the view, and the page changes nothing the Map and Elements show (selection, pins, tab, facet, texts, address). The header keeps Open on diagram, which draws the view on the Map of its layer or in Elements to explore it with their tools, Rename and Delete, and has Maximize, which hides the list of views and the right-hand panel (M maximizes, Esc restores; the Map is not touched).

The panel on the right, resized and hidden as the one of the Map (see The inspection panel), has the view first (while a plateau is compared, it has two tabs, View and Changes, see The Changes panel): what it draws and the file that keeps it, then a field for each attribute of the view, and the steps of the stories that show it, each a link to its story. Once something is selected, the inspection of the selection comes under it; the panel stays where it is when the top of the inspection is in sight, and otherwise scrolls by the least it takes to show its first lines, so that the fields of the view stay in sight as long as there is room. A click on a box, or Enter on the box that has the focus, selects the element and shows its panel, which has a Show in Elements button: it leaves the page and selects the element there, on the tab Elements was left on. A click on a link or on its caption shows the relationships it stands for, and the elements named in that panel select on the page too. The selected box is outlined, and x or Esc deselects. A box for a relationship that points at nothing cannot be clicked here. This selection is the page’s own: it does not select anything on the Map or in Elements, it is forgotten when another view is shown, and it is not in the URL, which carries no more than #/stories/views?view=<id> (and the plateau).

A view is changed by its fields, and the diagram follows at once:

  • Viewpoint: Overview, Detailed, Deep or Map. From an element viewpoint to the Map, the element becomes the pinned element, in the layer the view has (a technology element is the pin of the Map of the technology layer); from the Map to an element viewpoint, the first pinned element that still exists becomes the element, and the hidden and open elements are dropped; the layer stays. What the view showed of the texts is kept. Cancel gives the change up as long as it has not been saved.

  • Layer (every viewpoint: a Map draws the layer it names) and Element (the element viewpoints): the element is typed, with the references of the layer as suggestions, and applied with Enter or by leaving the field. A reference that is not an element of the layer is refused, with the reason under the field, and stays there to be corrected. Changing the layer keeps the element when the other layer has it, and otherwise leaves the view without one: it then says so and cannot be drawn until one is chosen; for a Map it keeps the entries of its three lists that exist in the other layer.

  • Pinned, Hidden and Open (the Map): each is a list of elements of the layer of the view (an element of the other layer is refused), with a cross to take one out and a field to add one (Enter). The drawing of this page is not edited itself, on purpose: a click on a box selects it. To choose pins by clicking, use Open on diagram.

  • Links and Highlight (the Map of the application layer): each is a list of relationships of the layer, a pill for each entry and a select of the relationships not there yet, with a missing one marked as such. They are properties.links and properties.highlight (see [editor-map-links] and [story-of-a-flow]).

  • Columns: the boxes per row the layout aims at, with Default first (the value the view gets from the workspace) and then the numbers. It is properties.layout, and it is the same list as the one of the toolbar (see The boxes per row).

  • Facet: the facets of the workspace, or none. Hide unlinked, Labels, Qualifiers, Descriptions and Wrap are boxes to tick (a Map of the technology layer has two more, Applications and Relationships, ticked when the property is absent: unticking one writes false, see [map-technology]), and they are the same as the buttons of the tools over the diagram. A text that is what that kind of diagram draws by default is not written to the file, as for any view.

Nothing is written to the disk by an edit. The view gets the mark modified, in the header and in the list, and loses it when what you did is put back, as soon as the file of the view would be the one on disk (the last one written); Save all changes writes the file of the view and no other (the comments and the layout of the file stay, as for any manifest; a property that an edit takes out and puts back again is written where the editor writes it, element, facet, Hide unlinked, then the texts), and Cancel gives every unsaved change up: the view, and the diagram with it, are the saved ones again. A view that an edit left impossible to draw (no element, nothing that was pinned exists) says why in the center, with the same fields to correct it, and can still be saved. Renaming an element in Elements still rewrites the views that point at it; the steps of the stories point at the view, so they show the edited view, saved or not.

A view that cannot be drawn (its element was deleted, nothing that was pinned exists any more, the viewpoint is unknown) keeps its message, what it draws, its fields and its steps in the center of the page, with no diagram and no panel.

A story is a file (story.<id>.yaml) in the first manifests folder, and so is each named view. They wait in memory, like the other changes, with a mark on the file, for Save all changes, and Cancel gives them up: only the files that changed are written. When an older workspace still has views or stories in .archicode.yaml, Stories says so, on both sides, with Move to manifests, which works whatever plateau is drawn.

Facets

The facets of the workspace (see Tackling view clutter) are in a list in the header of the diagrams: the diagram is then drawn as the command line generates it for that facet, with the relationships the facet removes left out, and the elements it removes, with the relationships that pointed at them. In the tree, the elements the facet leaves with nothing to draw are greyed. A facet that the kernel refuses (an old path of the previous engine, an invalid JSONPath) shows its sentence as the error of the view. The facets themselves are edited in the Settings perspective, along with the other settings of the workspace (see Technical Configuration). Add facet there refuses a name that cannot be the name of a folder (a/b, .., a name that ends with a dot), with the reason under the field, as the command line refuses it when it loads the workspace (see Tackling view clutter).

Files

Files lists every manifest of the workspace on the left, and the text of the one that is open in the center, which can be edited (see [editor-reads] for what the editor reads). Whatever plateau is drawn, the list holds every file: there is no choice of what it shows. A workspace that has layers lists them in groups.

A group for each plateau. The heading of a group says the plateau, its name and the number of its files. The groups come in the order of the tree, the root first and then each plateau under the one it overrides. The plateau of a manifest is the one its header names (plateau: saas), baseline when the header names none, and, for a manifest of kind archicode.morin.io/plateau, the plateau it declares. A group that holds no file has no heading. A workspace with no layer lists its files as it always did, with no group and nothing to fold.

Fold and unfold. A click on the heading of a group folds and unfolds it (Enter and Space do the same), and Fold all and Unfold all do every group. A folded group still tells, in its heading, that one of its files has an error or is modified. Opening a file, by its row, by an address or by a link of a message, unfolds its group; a fold that you make afterwards is kept. The folds are the page’s own: they last as long as the editor stays open on the same folder, they are not written in the workspace, and they do not make a file modified.

Files that belong to no plateau. The last group, No plateau, holds the files that belong to no plateau: the views and the stories (they are declared in no plateau, see [plateau-untied]) and a file the editor could not read, which is always listed, with its error. .archicode.yaml is not in the list: it is the page Settings.

A plateau that disagrees with the folder. The folder of a manifest does not matter to the tools: the plateau is what the header says. The folders of a project are often named after its plateaus, though, and a file moved from one to the other with its header left alone is read as the plateau of its header, not of its folder. When a manifest sits in a folder that is named like another plateau of the workspace (the deepest such folder counts), its row has a folder mark, a note above the groups counts such files (N files sit in the folder of another plateau), and its page has a warning: a/x.yaml says plateau: b in its header but sits in the folder a; b/y.yaml has no plateau key, so it belongs to baseline, but sits in the folder b. It is only a warning: nothing is refused, the command line says nothing, and the file is where you put it. A folder that is named like no plateau says nothing.

Name of the workspace

A workspace can have a name, the optional name key of its settings (see Workspace). Settings has a section, Workspace, with a Name field. Unlike the theme below, the name is a setting of the workspace: it is written to .archicode.yaml, so it counts as a change to save and lights Save all changes.

When the workspace has a name, the editor shows it at the top left of the window, in place of the name of the folder, in the title of the page, in the list of remembered folders and in the "Opening" messages, which use the name the workspace had when its folder was last opened; the name of the folder is then the tooltip of the name. Without a name, or with a blank one, the editor shows the name of the folder, as before, and an emptied Name is left out of the file.

The current plateau

Settings, in its section Workspace, has a field Current plateau when the workspace has at least two plateaus (or when .archicode.yaml already says current): a list of the plateaus, in the order of the band, and None. It writes settings.current into .archicode.yaml, the plateau the command line draws when --plateau is not given, and the plateau the editor opens the folder on when nothing else says which: no address with plateau=, and no plateau that this browser drew last in this folder. A file that has no settings gets one with that line alone; None takes the line out (and leaves settings: {} when it was the only one). A name that is no plateau is refused with the sentence of the load. Like the other fields of Settings it is a change to save with Save all changes, and it is disabled while the plateaus have mistakes (only what the file says and None can be chosen then).

It does not redraw the plateau on screen: what you chose on the band is not undone by a setting for the next time. With None, the command line draws the last plateau when the plateaus form one line, and asks for --plateau otherwise, while the editor opens the root.

The band of plateaus

Under the top bar, on the four pages of Timeline and nowhere else, the band chooses the plateau that is drawn and the one it is compared with. It is lean and read-only: two lists and, when something is wrong, one button. It writes nothing, and it has no tree, no counts and no sentence of its own (the tree and the counts are on the page Plateaus, see The Plateaus page). The plateau drawn and the comparison are the state of the editor, not of the band: Stories, Files and Settings have no band, and draw the plateau that was chosen. There is no plateau selector, no Compare to list and no section of the Settings for plateaus. A workspace that writes no layer has one plateau, baseline. A workspace of layers (a plateau key in the header of a manifest, or a manifest of kind archicode.morin.io/plateau) has one for each plateau.

  • Plateau draws a plateau in the whole editor. It lists every plateau in the order of the tree, baseline first and then what overrides it, each followed by what overrides it and indented by its depth, with its name and its horizon. A plateau that the command line refuses has a ! in front of its name.

  • Compare with says what the plateau drawn is compared with. It starts with Nothing and Past (follows the plateau drawn) (the plateau it overrides, whichever that is, so it changes when another plateau is drawn; the root has no past, so no such entry), then shows the same tree as Plateau, every plateau in the order of the tree and indented by its depth, with the plateau that is drawn greyed in its place so that it cannot be chosen and the hierarchy reads in both lists. A plateau of the tree is a fixed comparison, as archicode diff --plateau <drawn> --against <other> reads it: the parent, a child, a sibling or a cousin alike. There are no entries Future any more: a plateau above the one drawn is chosen in the tree, and is read as the future of the plateau drawn. A list chooses and does not toggle: choosing again the plateau that is compared already does nothing. Choosing the parent in the tree is read as Past (follows the plateau drawn), as the editor has always read a comparison with the parent: the comparison then moves with the plateau drawn. There is no way, on the band, to compare with the parent and have it stay when another plateau is drawn (see Limits of today). An address with compare=future and several plateaus above the plateau drawn shows a disabled entry, Choose a future plateau, selected, with nothing selected in the tree, until one is chosen.

  • ! N mistakes, when there are some, opens the dialog Mistakes in the plateaus: why only baseline is drawn, the sentences of the plateau drawn and of the plateau compared with that the command line refuses (the sentences of the command line, see Plateaus), the views and the stories that name elements no plateau has, the steps of the stories that name plateaus that are none (see [plateau-untied] and [story-step-plateaus]), and the sentence that nothing is written in a plateau that cannot be drawn.

Everything | Changes only is not on the band: it is among the tools of a diagram, and is shown while a plateau is compared (see Changes only and wording-only changes).

The order of the siblings. Plateaus that override the same plateau are placed by their horizon, when it reads as a date, and by their id otherwise. A horizon reads as a date when it is a year (2027), a month (2027-03), a day (2027-03-18), a quarter (2027-Q4) or a half (2027-H2); the date of a quarter or a half is the first day of the period. The plateaus whose horizon reads as a date come first, from the nearest to the farthest, those with the same date by id; the others follow, by id. The rule is the same every time the folder is opened. (archicode plateau list orders the siblings by id only, and the editor by horizon: the tree is the same.) (The tree does not need a horizon: it is the override of each plateau that places it, the horizon only orders the siblings. Any other text in a horizon is a label that no tool interprets.)

Drawing a plateau. A choice in Plateau draws that plateau, in the whole editor: the trees, Applications, Environments, the diagrams, Stories and the panel on the right show the plateau of the band. The plateau is its chain: the manifests that say plateau: <id> are added to the elements of the plateau it overrides, then its patches change them (REMOVE, SET, ADD, MERGE, applied by the same kernel as the command line), and every plateau of the chain is checked, with the sentences and in the order of the command line: a patch that selects nothing names the plateau, the rank of the patch and what it selected; a relationship, an entry of uses or an application hosted on a node that leads nowhere, a manifest whose parent is not there or cannot hold its kind, name the plateau. The views and the stories belong to no plateau: a name that they have and the plateau lacks is told in the view (see Stories: stories and views), and a name that no plateau has is in the dialog of the mistakes. The choice reads nothing from the disk and loses nothing: the manifests stay as they are, and each plateau is resolved once and kept until a manifest changes. When the kernel refuses the patches of a plateau, that plateau cannot be drawn (the dialog says why, and baseline is one choice away); when only the checks fail, the plateau is drawn all the same, flagged !, with the sentences that the command line would stop on, so that the mistake can be repaired in it. The plateau that opens is the one the address names (plateau=), else the one this browser drew last in this folder, else the one settings.current names (a name that is no plateau is an error with the sentence of the command line; the field Current plateau of Settings writes it, see The current plateau), else baseline. When the plateaus form one line and no settings.current says otherwise, the editor therefore opens the root while the command line, with no --plateau, draws the last plateau.

Comparing. Compare with compares the plateau that is drawn with another one. The comparison is the one of Comparing two plateaus: the Map, the diagrams, Stories and the Changes panel tell what is new, changed, removed, moved, split or merged, with the same counts and the same table as archicode diff for the same two plateaus; Everything and Changes only choose what the diagrams draw. With the past the plateau drawn is the more recent one, with the future it is the older one, and what only the other plateau has is read in the panel on the right, as a removed element is. The choice is remembered by this browser for this folder.

Cascade. Below the lists, the Changes panel has Cascade of the removals when a REMOVE of the more recent plateau took more than what it selects: the elements that went with the selected one, and the entries taken out of elements that stay (a relationship towards it, an entry of uses or of applications), among what the other plateau has, as archicode diff lists them after the table. Write replaces and Not related work on the pairs of any two plateaus; Write replaces writes replaces in the manifest that declares the new element, and refuses an element the plateau only inherits (it is declared in a plateau above, which the key would change).

Where a value comes from. In the panel of an element, each value (name, description, qualifiers, tags, relationships, replaces, uses, applications) says where it comes from: declared in the plateau drawn, inherited from another plateau (the one whose manifest declares the element), or set by patch N of a plateau (with the operator and the selector of the patch, and "inherited" when the patch is one of a plateau above), or taken out by patch N when a REMOVE with a field took the value out (see [plateau-patches]). A block Origin lists the plateau that declares the element and the patches that selected it.

Writing. The plateau that is drawn is the one a write goes to, and the page Plateaus says so (writing in: Buy the commerce platform). What an edit writes, and what is refused, is in Writing in a plateau.

Writing in a plateau

A write goes to the plateau that is drawn, in the files of that plateau, and nowhere else. The page Plateaus says writing in: <plateau> when the workspace has layers. A workspace that writes no layer saves as it always did.

In the plateau drawn What is written

a field of an element the plateau declares (its manifest says plateau: <the plateau>)

the field, in the manifest that declares the element, as before

a field of an element the plateau inherits (name, description, qualifiers, tags, relationships, applications, uses, replaces)

a patch in the manifest of kind archicode.morin.io/plateau of the plateau, at the end of its patches: SET for a value (a list set whole too), ADD for items added at the end of a list, MERGE for tags that only gain or change keys, SET with where for one relationship changed (found by its destination and its label), REMOVE with a field for a text or a number that is cleared (below)

the same field again

the patch the plateau already has on that element and that field is changed (a REMOVE becomes a SET when you write a new text, a SET becomes a REMOVE when you clear it), and taken out when the field is back to what the plateau above gives it; one patch for the field, not one more for each edit. When the plateau has several patches on the field (a relationship added, another relabeled), none is rewritten: the last one takes an edit of its kind, and any other edit is a patch after it

a value a patch of the plateau set

that patch

a new element (top level, or a child of an element the plateau inherits)

a manifest whose header says plateau: <the plateau> (and parent for a child), in the folder of the manifests the plateau already has. A child of an element the plateau declares is written in the file of its parent, as before

a new view or story (they belong to no plateau, whatever plateau is drawn)

a manifest with no plateau: in its header, in the first folder of manifests.paths

removing an element the plateau inherits

a REMOVE at the end of the patches (with layer: technology for a technology element). The kernel takes what is in the element and what points at it; the manifests of the plateau above are not touched

removing an element the plateau declares

its manifests are deleted, as before, and the patches of the plateau that name it (their target, the relationship they find with where or add) are taken out with them

a new plateau (New plateau, on the page Plateaus)

a manifest of kind archicode.morin.io/plateau with its id, name, horizon and override (none when it overrides baseline)

the name, horizon or parent of a plateau (Edit plateau)

the same manifest; a plateau that exists through headers only gets one

deleting a plateau (Delete)

its manifest of kind archicode.morin.io/plateau and the manifests whose header says its id are deleted, after a dialog that lists them (see The Plateaus page)

Asking first. A plateau that other plateaus stand on is not written without a word: when an edit changes the resolution of a plateau that overrides the one written, the editor lists those plateaus (the ones that inherit what was written and do not set it themselves) and writes only when you say yes. baseline is the plateau most often in that case. A plateau at the end of the chain, or an edit that no other plateau sees, is written at once.

Only what was meant. Every write is made on the manifests in memory by the same line-preserving save as ever (the lines the edit does not touch, their comments and quotes, stay as they were written; a list or map in brackets that an edit changes stays in brackets on its line when it fits, see Elements), and it is kept only if the plateaus are resolved again and all of this holds: the plateau written gives back the value that was edited; no plateau that resolved before is refused now, and no new mistake appears in the tree of the plateaus or in a plateau (a rename, a delete or a new parent that would make a patch of another plateau select nothing is refused, and says which plateau and which patch). A write that is refused is one sentence in the top bar, and nothing is changed: not a file, not a mark of what is modified. A new patch is always written at the end of the patches, never between two patches of yours, so that a comment above a patch stays above it.

Clearing what the plateau inherits. When you clear a name, a description or another text that the plateau only inherits, the editor writes a REMOVE patch with the field, at the end of the patches of the plateau drawn, after the question above if other plateaus stand on it. The Inspector then says where the value went (taken out by patch 3 of saas (REMOVE target shop.web)), and writing a text again changes that patch instead of adding one. A list or tags are emptied with a SET of an empty list or object, as before, and clearing a field that the plateau declares itself removes its line from the manifest, as before. If the plateau written does not give the field back gone, nothing is written.

Refused, with one sentence.

  • Renaming (id) or moving an element the plateau inherits: a patch never changes an id or a parent; draw the plateau that declares the element. Renaming or moving an element the plateau declares works as it does in baseline; the target of patches, the relationships they add, and the replaces entries that name it follow the new reference.

  • A field that a patch written by hand with a free json-path sets (for instance a MERGE of tags on every element that has a qualifier): the editor shows it, with its origin, and does not edit it field by field. Edit the patch in Files.

  • A plateau cannot be drawn when the kernel refuses its patches: nothing is written in it until they are repaired in Files.

Not written by the editor: patches with a free json-path or an expect (they are written by hand in Files). A new plateau manifest is written next to the other manifests (plateau.<id>.yaml in the first folder of manifests.paths; for a plateau that exists through headers only, in the folder of its files). The text editor of Files writes the text of any file whatever plateau is drawn, and is the way to repair or write what the forms do not.

The Plateaus page

Plateaus, a page of the perspective Timeline (its address is #/timeline/plateaus), lists the plateaus as a tree: one entry for each, in the order of the tree, each indented by its depth. An entry says the name of the plateau, its id, its horizon, whether it is the one drawn or the one compared, and, in a line below, the plateau it overrides, where it is written (described in inhouse/plateau.yaml, or exists only through the plateau key of a header for a plateau that has no manifest of its own), how many patches it has and how many files belong to it. Below that, what it changes over the plateau it overrides, as the chips of the Changes panel, for the elements and for the relationships: new, changed, removed, and moved, split and merged when there are some. They are the counts of archicode diff --plateau <id>; a plateau that the command line refuses, or that overrides one it refuses, says it is not counted (a ! marks it). The root overrides nothing, and says so. A workspace that writes no layer has the page too: one entry, baseline, and a note on what a plateau is. This page is the one place that lists the plateaus as a tree with their counts and the one place that writes a plateau: the band chooses and writes nothing. The indentation is not capped: each level of the tree is one step further in, for a chain of any length, and in a window narrower than 40 rem (see The editor on a small screen) each level is half as far in, half a rem instead of a full step, so that a deep chain takes less of the width; the two lists of the band keep one em space per level.

The name of a plateau draws it, as a choice in the list Plateau of the band does. Changes draws the plateau and compares it with the plateau it overrides in one step, and the Changes panel opens on the right with the same lists as on the other pages; a click on one of its rows opens the element in Elements, since this page draws none (Next change and Previous change show the change they are at under the buttons, see The Changes panel).

Writing a plateau. New plateau after this (on an entry) and New plateau (above the list, for the plateau drawn, with writing in: <plateau>) make a plateau that overrides it; Edit (Edit plateau beside New plateau) changes its name, horizon or parent. The manifest is written as Writing in a plateau says.

Deleting a plateau. Delete is on the entry of every plateau but the root, and Delete plateau beside New plateau for the plateau drawn. It opens a dialog that says what goes before anything does.

  • What can go. Any plateau but the root. The dialog refuses, with one sentence and without a Delete button, the root (every other plateau stands on it), a workspace that has no plateau but baseline, plateaus that cannot be drawn, and a save in flight.

  • A plateau that others override. It is deleted in the same one step. The plateaus that override it go under its parent: their override is rewritten, line by line, in their manifest of kind archicode.morin.io/plateau (the key is taken out when the new parent is the root). What the deleted plateau declared and patched is lost to every plateau that stands on it, and the dialog says it before anything is written: for each plateau above, its new parent, the elements it loses, the ones it gets back (the deleted plateau had removed them) and the ones that read differently. Delete plateau is the yes to those plateaus; a change to any other plateau is still asked. A comparison with the past of a plateau that overrode it follows it to the new parent.

  • What goes, and only that. Its manifest of kind archicode.morin.io/plateau and every manifest whose header says its id, listed one by one (a file not saved yet is marked new, not saved yet, and a modified one says its changes go with it). A view or a story that says plateau: <id> belongs to no plateau: it stays, and the dialog says so. The files leave the disk at the next Save all changes, like any change, and Cancel gives the delete up with the others.

  • Files that the editor does not read. A .toml or .json manifest, or a YAML file that the editor cannot read, may say plateau: <id>, or override: <id>, for the command line: the delete touches and rewrites none of them, the dialog lists them, and says that the command line still reads the plateau if one of them names it.

  • settings.current. When it names the plateau (or baseline, when the last plateau goes), the key is taken out of .archicode.yaml, and the dialog says that the command line then draws the last plateau when the plateaus form one line, and asks for --plateau otherwise. A current that names another plateau, gone already, is left as it is.

  • What blocks it. A step of a story that names the plateau in at or against, a view or a story step that names an element that only this plateau declares (it would exist in no plateau), a step told at a plateau above that would lose the element it draws, a step compared against a plateau above that would lose it when the plateau the step is told at does not keep it either, a saved view that is drawn in a plateau above and would lose its element (or, for a map, every element it is pinned on), and a plateau above that would no longer resolve (a patch that selects nothing, a relationship or a parent that is gone): the dialog lists the sentences, offers no Delete, and says to change them first, in Stories for the steps and the views, or to repair the plateau above or give it another parent with Edit. They are not cleared for you: a step told at a plateau is your statement of which state it shows. A step compared against a plateau above is not refused when the plateau it is told at (the plateau drawn when it says none) keeps what it draws: the comparison is drawn from that plateau, and an element the plateau compared with loses is then shown as new. The sentences are these (tour is a story, beta and marketplace stand above the plateau deleted, and loyalty is an element only the deleted plateau declares):

    the story `tour` (`stories/story.tour.yaml`) step 1 is told at `beta`, which would no longer have `loyalty`
the story `tour` (`stories/story.tour.yaml`) step 1 is compared with `marketplace`, which would no longer have `loyalty`
the view `loyalty_overview` (`entries/view.loyalty.yaml`) is drawn in `beta`, `marketplace`, which would no longer have `loyalty`

    A plateau that nothing of this kind concerns is deleted as before, byte for byte. On the layered Brightcart, inhouse cannot be deleted for that reason (marketplace patches what inhouse declares).

  • Folding a plateau into the one above, by hand. The editor does not move the patches and the elements of a plateau into the plateau above: that is a choice about your history, and no button makes it. archicode plateau fold <id> makes it from the command line and checks the result (see Fold a plateau into the one above); this recipe is what it does, and what to do for a plateau it refuses. When the refusal tells you the plateau above would lose what the plateau deleted gave it, and you want that plateau to keep it, fold by hand, in the files, with the plateau to-be and the plateau later above it (later overrides to-be; archicode plateau fold to-be does these steps and --dry-run prints them first, see Fold a plateau into the one above):

    1. In every manifest whose header says plateau: "to-be", write plateau: "later". These are the elements to-be declares.

    2. In the manifest of kind archicode.morin.io/plateau of later, change override: "to-be" to the plateau to-be overrode ("baseline", or take the line out when that is the root), and put the patches of to-be in front of those of later, in their order: later was written to be read over to-be, so its patches come last. A patch of later that refers to what to-be declared (a REMOVE of an element of to-be) still finds it, since it is now declared by later itself.

    3. Compose the replaces of later. Look in the manifests of later and in the value of its patches that SET a replaces for an entry that names an element to-be declared (say shop.search). Once to-be is gone, later is read over the plateau to-be overrode, and that entry names nothing. Replace it by what that element itself replaced (its replaces, in its manifest or set by a patch of to-be), or take it out when the element replaced nothing. An entry that names an element of the plateau below stays as it is. If you forget, archicode diff prints warning: replaces of search (application layer): shop.search is not an element of the base plateau, ignored, exits with 0, and reads the move as one new element and one removed element.

    4. Delete the manifest of kind archicode.morin.io/plateau of to-be, and any manifest that still names it, with your file manager or git rm.

    5. Change what still names to-be; archicode lists the ones you forgot, one error: line each (the story `migration (plateaus/story.migration.yaml) step 2 says at: to-be, which is not a plateau of this workspace (the plateaus are baseline, later)). Each replacement is a choice. A step told `at: "to-be" becomes at: "later", but then it draws later, which is to-be and the layer above it: it can draw more, and a step that names an element later removes is skipped with a warning (warning: skip migration/4: it names `shop, which the plateau later does not have`). An against: "to-be" becomes the plateau to-be overrode, or the key is taken out: do not write later there, a step told at later and compared with later is one error: line (…​ step 5 says `at: later and against: later: a plateau is never compared with itself`). The plateau of a view or a story means nothing and may stay. If settings.current in .archicode.yaml is to-be, write later: otherwise the commands that read it stop with error: `settings.current names the plateau to-be, which is not a plateau of this workspace (the plateaus are baseline, later)`.

      Check it with archicode plateau list (to-be is gone, later has the plateau to-be overrode as its parent), with archicode diff --plateau later --against baseline and with archicode views generate --plateau later --against baseline, before and after. Name --against both times: the default of later is to-be before and its new parent after, so two defaults compare two different pairs. When the steps are followed, the text above the table of diff and every .puml that views generate writes are the same, the table has the same rows, and two columns of the cascade of the removals differ: PLATEAU, where the rows of to-be now say later, and PATCH, since the patches of later are numbered after those of to-be; gap.md and gap.json differ in those two columns only. On a copy of the example domain_plateaus with the plateau later of the tutorial, only the column PLATEAU changes, because the one patch of later has no row in the cascade. A replaces …​ ignored warning after the fold means that the third step was missed. archicode query graph is not a byte for byte check: the order of siblings that two files of the folded plateau declare can change. This is for a plateau with one plateau above it; with two, keep it: folding it into one would leave the other without what it declared. The folding loses what the plateau to-be was on its own: a comparison --plateau to-be, its views folder views-to-be, and the against: "to-be" of any step, which you changed above.

  • After it. The plateau it overrode is drawn, with no comparison, when the plateau drawn was the one deleted; the plateau drawn stays, compared with nothing, when the one deleted was the one compared with. The folders of views that the command line generated (views-<id> and the folders of its comparisons) are not manifests: they stay where they are.

Comparing two plateaus

Compare with, on the band, compares the plateau that is drawn with another one (see The band of plateaus): Past is the plateau it overrides, and the tree lists every other plateau, the one that overrides the plateau drawn (the future of the comparison), a sibling or a cousin included. With nothing compared, which is the default, the plateau is drawn alone, exactly as the editor always did. A comparison draws the plateau together with the other one: Applications, the diagrams of Elements and the page of a view in Stories tell what is new, what changed and what was removed (and, for the elements that say what they replace, what moved, was split or merged, see Moved, split and merged elements, and candidates), with the same states as archicode views generate (see Comparing two plateaus). Like the plateau, the choice is remembered by this browser for this folder (not in the workspace), and it is ignored when the workspace no longer has that plateau. A plateau is never compared with itself. The address carries the plateau (plateau=<id>, when it is not baseline), and, in a workspace that has plateaus, the comparison on every page that draws a diagram (compare=past, compare=future:<id> or compare=other:<id>, empty for none; see What a link carries); a link that has compare= draws that comparison, and an older link that has none leaves the comparison as it is.

The plateau compared with is read, not written: the editor resolves it from the manifests it has already read (there is no second folder to pick), keeps it apart from the plateau that is drawn, and an edit goes to the plateau drawn (see Writing in a plateau). Its elements are not in the trees of the plateau drawn, and what only it has is listed in the Changes panel and read in the panel on the right. A plateau that cannot be resolved cannot be compared, and the dialog of the mistakes (the button of the band) says why. The one write that is made for the comparison is Write replaces (see Moved, split and merged elements, and candidates).

The diagram is drawn from the union of the two plateaus, and the boxes are laid out on that union: what was removed is drawn where it was, so a box does not move when the comparison is turned on from the other plateau or when you choose the other plateau of the pair (when nothing is compared, the plateau is laid out alone, as before). The editor tells the states without relying on colour: each one has a mark in front of the title of the box and of the caption of the link, a line style, and a pill with its name on the box.

State Mark Box Link

new

+

thick solid outline, a pill new, a green tint

thick solid line

changed

~

dotted outline, a pill changed, an orange tint

dotted line

removed

−

long dashes with narrow gaps, grey outline, grey struck title, a pill removed, a grey tint

dashed grey line

contains changes

±

thin dashed outline, a pill contains-changes

(none)

moved

→

long dashes with wide gaps, a pill moved, a blue tint, (from shop.till) after the title

(none)

split

⋔

medium dashes, a pill split, a purple tint, (from shop.report) after the title

(none)

merged

⊕

short dashes far apart, a pill merged, a light blue tint, (from stock.bin, stock.shelf) after the title

(none)

The last three states are for the elements that say what they replace, and exist only for a box: a relationship is never moved, split or merged. They are in the table above and in the key only when the drawing has a box in that state.

A box that did not change is drawn as it always was. A removed element is not in the plateau that is drawn, so it is not in the tree and a click on its box does not open it: it shows, in the panel on the right, what the other plateau had (name, kind, properties, the file it is in, the relationships it declared), read-only. A link that changed shows its mark even when the captions are off, as on the Map. The marks and lines are the ones of the command line (a dash and a gap of its own for removed 16-3, moved 12-12, split 8-8 and merged 3-9 in both, so that no two of them are alike); the colours are only a help, chosen to read in the light and the dark theme. Red dashed keeps its meaning of a dangling reference (an unknown element), and is never used for a removed one.

The look is a default, given to the stereotype of the same name as the state (new, changed, removed, contains-changes). A style of your own for that name in styles (see Stereotypes and styles) wins property by property: your line-color replaces the colour of the line and keeps its style and thickness, your background-color fills the box and replaces the tint, your font-color replaces the grey of a removed title. The state is the last stereotype of a box, so it overrides the style of the others, as a later stereotype always does. A new or changed tag that you wrote by hand on an element that does not have that state is only your stereotype, with your own style if you gave it one, and the command line draws it the same way (see [plateau-state-tags]).

The key is folded. Under the diagram, the key starts as one row: a sample of the box and of the link, the mark, the name and the count of each state the drawing uses, and a Key button. The row wraps on a wide window and stays one row that scrolls along under 700 pixels. Key (aria-expanded) opens the rest, which says how each line is drawn and the note about references that point nowhere; the open key takes at most 30 % of the height of the diagram area and scrolls inside, and the cap follows a resize of the window (it is worked out again when the window changes size, so a key left open keeps within 30 % of a smaller window). Open or folded is not saved: it is not in the address or the workspace, and a new folder folds it again. Open, the key adds an entry for each state the drawing uses, with a sample of its box and its link, its mark, its name, how its line is drawn, and how many boxes and links of that state the drawing shows. The SVG and PNG downloads draw the same marks, lines, struck titles, pills and tints; like the other keys, the key itself is not part of the image.

The Changes panel

While a comparison is on, the panel on the right of Plateaus, Applications, Environments and Elements is never empty. With nothing selected it is the Changes panel; with something selected it has two tabs, Selected (what is selected, as before) and Changes (with the number of rows). On Stories the panel is the one of the view, and while a comparison is on and can be drawn it has two tabs, View (what was there) and Changes; a story, or a view that cannot be drawn, had no panel and has the Changes panel alone.

The panel starts with the counts: elements and relationships, each new, changed and removed, as chips that carry a mark and a word (+ 38 new, ~ 26 changed, − 5 removed), so that they read without colour; the elements add → 1 moved, ⋔ 2 split and ⊕ 1 merged when there are some. The same chip is the state on every row below. Two lists follow, Elements and Relationships, in the order a review reads them: what is new, then what changed, then what moved, was split or merged, then what was removed, and by reference inside each. A row has the chip, the name and the reference of the element (a relationship reads source → destination “label”) and, for something that changed, what changed in it in words: name, description (wording only), qualifiers, tags, deployment, relationships added, relationships removed, relationships changed, and for a relationship label, qualifiers, tags. A relationship whose label changed is one row that says the label before and after. A row of a moved, split or merged element says where it came from (from shop.till, from shop.report; split with shop.report-b), as archicode diff does.

Reading a long list. A comparison of a large workspace has hundreds of changes (the pair before and after-declared of the fixture replaces-large-pair has 360). Under the counts, the panel says how many there are (167 changes) and has what it takes to read them without losing the place:

  • Previous change and Next change walk through the list, in the order of the list (the elements, then the relationships). They stop at the ends and do not wrap, and the panel says where you are (3 of 12). With no change selected, Next change goes to the first and Previous change to the last. The walk selects the change as a click on its row does, and leaves the list on screen (the Changes tab stays). Under the buttons the panel shows the What changed block of the change the walk is at (see What changed), so that the page Plateaus, and a story with no view drawn, where no box can be shown, still say what the change is; a click on a row there opens the element in Elements, as before.

  • The walk row stays in view while the list scrolls. Previous change, Next change, the position (3 of 12) and Show all are one row under the tabs, sticky at the top of the panel, so a reader who walks with the mouse does not scroll back to the buttons after each press. A press brings the selected row into view with the nearest scroll (block: nearest) that shows it, below the sticky row: a row already in view does not move the list, and one that is below it moves the list just enough. At a window 360 pixels wide the row is measured as well, its height as a share of the panel’s height at 360 by 640 pixels, against the 35 percent limit of the design; above that limit the rows would have a box of their own and the buttons would stay outside it. The figure of that measure is in the record of the change (run 0201), not in this manual. The row was tested in Chromium and Firefox. Safari was not tested, so whether it keeps the row in view in the same way is not proved.

  • The lists State (all, then the states that some row has, each with its count) and Layer (both, application, technology) narrow what is listed: the line then reads 12 of 167 changes, each group says n of N, and Show all clears both. contains-changes is not a choice: it is the state of a box that holds a change, never of a row. A filter that leaves out the change you are at moves you to the nearest one that is shown.

  • The filters change the list only, never what the diagrams draw (Changes only does that, see Changes only and wording-only changes), nor the counts above them. They are the editor’s, not the workspace’s: they start again when the pair of plateaus compared changes. The walk has a keyboard shortcut, J and K (see Keyboard).

A click on a row selects the thing as a click on its box or its link does, and shows it. On the Map the boxes around it are opened so that it is drawn, and the view is centred on it (what the Center button does); a pin or a hidden element of your own is not undone, and the panel says when the element is not on the map. A removed element opens the read-only panel described above, a relationship selects its link (in Elements, the element that declares it, and its link when that element’s diagram is shown). The row of the selection is marked in the list, and the Changes tab brings the list back with that row in view.

What changed

The panel of a selected element, link or removed box adds a What changed block, first, so that a long description does not push it out of sight; it is the same on the page of a view in Stories.

  • For a changed element, the natures in words, then each property that differs with its value Before and After (name, kind, description, qualifiers, tags, and the applications or uses of a node), then its relationships that are new, changed or removed, each a line with its chip. The Before and After lines carry a sign and a word, and the old value is struck, so nothing relies on colour.

  • For a new element, "Whole element is new": its properties are the ones listed below the block, and the relationships it declares are listed as new.

  • For a removed element, the read-only panel above, with "Whole element is removed"; it does not repeat what the panel already lists. A removed element that is only drawn as the place of what was inside it, and that other elements replace, adds Replaced by and lists them.

  • For a moved, split or merged element, one sentence that says which, then Replaces with the name and the reference of each element it replaces (one that the other plateau does not have is flagged), then, if the element also changed, Also changed and the properties Before and After as above.

  • For a link, its state (all of the relationships it groups new, or removed, or changed, or a mixture, which reads changed) and, on each relationship it groups, its chip and, for one that changed, what it was and what it is.

Changes only and wording-only changes

Everything | Changes only, among the tools of a diagram and shown while a plateau is compared, sets what the diagrams draw: with Changes only a diagram keeps what is new, changed or removed, the boxes that enclose them and the ends of the changed links; the links that did not change are left out. The boxes kept only to place the changes, the context, are drawn faded, say no state and are told in the key ("context, faded: shown to place the changes", with their number). The focal element of a diagram always stays and is never faded. It works the same on the Map, on the diagrams of Elements and on the page of a view in Stories, and together with the pins, the hidden elements, a facet and Hide unlinked.

A change of the description alone is a change of wording, not of structure. The Changes panel counts them apart ("10 of the changed elements are changes of wording only") and keeps them visible by default; the checkbox Hide wording-only changes sets them aside in one click: those elements are drawn as unchanged (and a box that only they changed is no longer marked contains-changes, nor kept by Changes only), their rows leave the list, the counts of changed elements drop by their number and the panel says "10 wording-only changes hidden". Relationships are never set aside: a change of a label, a qualifier or a tag is structure.

Both choices are yours and not the workspace’s: they are remembered per folder in this browser, like the plateau and the comparison, never written to .archicode.yaml, and they do nothing while nothing is compared. Changes only is also in the address while a comparison is on (changes-only=1), so that a link shows what its sender saw; opening such a link does not change what this browser remembers. Hide wording-only changes is not in the address.

The address and the trees

The address of the page carries a selection, so that a reload or a shared link reopens it: ref= for an element, link-from= and link-to= for a link, as before, and removed= for a removed element (removed-layer=technology for one of the technology layer). A removed element exists only next to its comparison, so the address then also carries compare= (past, the past that follows the plateau drawn; future:<id>, a plateau that overrides the plateau drawn; or other:<id>, any other plateau of the tree: a choice in the tree of Compare with is written as one of the three; compare=future with no id is read as the future to choose, and shown as the disabled entry Choose a future plateau when several plateaus override the plateau drawn): opening such an address chooses that comparison, as if you had chosen it, even if this browser remembered another one. The page writes compare= on every diagram page of a workspace that has plateaus, with Changes only beside it (see What a link carries); the wording-only toggle is not in the address.

While a comparison is on, a row of the tree of Elements and of the tree of the Map has a small mark after its name: + for a new element, ~ for a changed one, →, ⋔ or ⊕ for a moved, split or merged one, and ± for one that did not change but has a change below it. The word is in its tooltip and is read by a screen reader. A removed element is not in these trees (it is not an element of the plateau drawn, so it can be neither pinned nor hidden nor edited): the Changes panel lists it and opens its panel.

What a link carries

A link copied from the address bar draws the diagram its sender saw: the same boxes, the same links, in the same places. The rule is that an option is in the address when it changes which boxes and links are drawn, or where, and is never there when it changes only the surroundings.

Parameter What it carries

layout=N

the columns chosen in the toolbar (1 to 20), on the page of an element and on both Maps; never the value of a view or of the workspace, which are files

compare=

the comparison (past, future:<id>, other:<id>, empty for none), on every diagram page of a workspace that has plateaus

changes-only=1

Changes only, while a comparison is on; with compare= and without it, the flag is off

open=

the unfolded boxes of a Map, a list of references

hide=

the hidden elements of a Map, a list of references

pin=, rel=, hl=, facet=, hide-unlinked, applications=0, relationships=0, the names shown, frame=fit

as before

Three things are never in the address: the wording-only toggle, the open or folded key, and zoom and pan; nor is the selection of a box. A reference the workspace does not have, or a layout that is not a whole number from 1 to 20, is dropped and the page writes the address back without it. An address the page wrote earlier opens as before.

Moved, split and merged elements, and candidates

An element of the plateau that is drawn can say which elements of the plateau it is compared with it replaces: the key replaces (see Saying what an element replaces). The editor draws the result with the three states above, counts them and lists them in the Changes panel, and the What changed block of a moved, split or merged element says what it replaces. A replaced element is not listed as removed. The Changes only filter keeps these elements like the others. The hand-written tags still follow the rule of [plateau-state-tags]: the look of moved, split or merged is only for a box the comparison says is in that state.

Candidates. Under the counts, the Candidates section lists the pairs the comparison takes for one thing said twice: an element only the other plateau has, and an element only this one has, of the same kind and the same layer, with the same name (when no other removed and no other new element of that kind has it), a close description, or as the only two of their kind under the same parent (not between two plateaus that are not one the past of the other, two alternatives), and each with its reasons. Unlike archicode diff, which lists them only for a plateau that already uses replaces, the editor proposes them for any comparison: it is how an author starts. A candidate is never applied by itself. Each pair has two buttons:

  • Write replaces adds the reference of the removed element to the replaces of the new one, in the manifest that declares the new element, and nothing else. It is an edit like the others: the file is marked as modified and written by Save all changes, the lines you did not touch (comments, blank lines, quotes) stay as they are, a list that was there gets the reference appended once, and a list written on one line (replaces: [shop.till]) becomes a list on several lines. The comparison is made again at once, and the new element is now moved, split or merged.

  • Not related sets the pair aside. It is remembered by the page only, until you close it: nothing is written and the comparison is not changed. The section says how many pairs are set aside, and Show again brings them back.

Write replaces writes the key on the element to of the more recent plateau of the two compared, in the manifest that declares it, and nowhere else; when the plateau compared with is the past of the one drawn (or any plateau the plateau drawn stands on), the more recent plateau is the one drawn, and when it is a plateau above the one drawn, the more recent plateau is the one compared with, which the button writes all the same, since it is the one thing the Changes panel offers. It is refused, with the reason in the panel and nothing written, when the element belongs to the older plateau of the two (replaces goes on the more recent plateau: compare the other way round), when the more recent plateau only inherits the element (it is declared in a plateau above, and the key would change that plateau and the ones that override it: compare that plateau with its past instead), when the replaced element is still in the more recent plateau, when it is not an element of the older one, or when the replaces of the element is not a list.

One more refusal belongs to comparisons several layers apart. replaces is read against the plateau a manifest builds on, so when a layer in between removed the replaced element, the entry would name nothing in the layer it is written in. The button is then off, and the sentence is its title and is written under the pair; a click that gets through anyway writes nothing and shows the same sentence, which names the element, the plateau the manifest builds on and what to do:

“a” is not an element of “p1”, the plateau “p2” builds on (a layer in between removed it): replaces is read against that plateau, so the entry would name nothing there.
