Compare “p2” with “p1”, or write the entry in the layer that still has “a” (a plateau that overrides one that holds it); nothing was written.

A split has one replaced element for all its pairs, so they are all refused or all allowed. A merge is written one pair at a time, so every pair is checked before the first is written, and the merge is refused as a whole: if the layer cannot hold one of the replaced elements, the buttons of all its pairs are off (the first pair keeps its own sentence above), and none is written, where the pair the layer could hold used to be written alone. The other pairs say why:

“n” would merge “a” and “b”, which is written one pair at a time, so every pair is checked before the first is written: “a” is not an element of “p1”, the plateau “p2” builds on (a layer in between removed it): replaces is read against that plateau, so the entry would name nothing there. Compare “p2” with “p1”, or write the entry in the layer that still has “a” (a plateau that overrides one that holds it). If a pair is no part of the merge, set it aside with “Not related”; nothing was written.

A pair set aside with Not related is no part of the merge, so the others are written as a lone pair is. A merge whose every replaced element the layer holds is written as it was, and a comparison one layer apart is not concerned.

By hand. The edit form of an element has a Replaces section as soon as the workspace has several plateaus: the list, each reference with a button to remove it, and a field to add one (it completes with the references of the plateau compared with). A reference the plateau compared with does not have is accepted and flagged ("not in “Base”", with the name of that plateau), because you may have written it for another baseline; one that the plateau drawn still has is flagged "still in this plateau: ignored". An element cannot replace itself, and a reference is listed once. Add replaces is checked as Write replaces is, when a comparison is on and the plateau drawn is the more recent of the two: if the comparison is several layers apart and a layer in between removed the reference, the field shows the sentence above under it, keeps what you typed, and nothing is written. With no comparison, or one layer apart, a reference is written as before. The details of an element that replaces something list it too.

Warnings. What the comparison ignored in the replaces it was given (see [plateau-replaces-warnings]) is a short list in the Changes panel, under the candidates, with the sentences of the command line; the editor does not write them to the console. A second list, Hand-written states, has one line per element and word for the state words you wrote by hand that the comparison does not give to the element (see [plateau-state-tags]), with the sentences of the command line, and the panel of such an element says it too, so that a box with a new badge that is not drawn as new tells why.

Moving an element. An element that you put under another parent in the editor has another reference, so it reads as a removed element and a new one when it is compared with the plateau it came from. Move does not write replaces (see Moving an element under another parent): write it from Write replaces in the Changes panel, or in the form of the element.

Moving an element under another parent

In the edit form of an element, Move (after Delete, before Add child) puts the element, and everything inside it, under another parent, or at the top level, without breaking a reference. It is offered when the dialog has at least one place to offer, the top level included: a solution, a person and an environment are at the top already and have nowhere else to go, so they have no Move; a system can go under a solution, and goes to the top level from there. It works on the plateau that is drawn, and never on the one it is compared with, which is read-only. In a plateau of layers it moves an element that plateau declares; an element it inherits is refused (a patch never changes a parent), and says which plateau declares it.

The dialog has a search field (it keeps the places that have every word you type in their name, id, reference or kind) and the list of the places the element can go, in the layer of the element: the top level, when it may be there, then the valid parents, each an element that may hold the kind of this one (the rules that create an element under a parent, see [nesting]). It says how many elements follow, how many references will be rewritten in how many files, and how many saved views follow. Under the places, a closed list says, for every element of the layer that is not offered, why not, with the same search: N places are not offered, and why. The reasons are the sentences that refuse a move: it is the element itself, it is inside the element, it is its parent already, its kind cannot be held by that parent (the sentence says what the parent holds), the parent already holds an element with that id, the parent is declared twice. A reason that stops every move (an element inside it is declared twice, a manifest cannot be read, a reference cannot be rewritten as text) is told once, at the top, as No move can be written now.

The top level. The kinds that may be at the top level are the ones the layer holds there: a person, a solution and a system in the application layer, an environment in the technology layer; for any other kind the list says so (A container is never at the top level: the application layer holds only person, solution and system there.), and the editor neither offers it nor moves an element there. In practice the top level is offered for a system that sits in a solution.

What follows. The reference of the element becomes the reference of the new parent followed by its id (its id alone, at the top level), and so does the reference of each element inside it. Every reference to the element or to something inside it is rewritten, in every file of the workspace, by the machinery of an id change:

  • the destination of the relationships of any element of the same layer;

  • the applications of the nodes of the technology layer (for an application element) and the uses of the environments (for a technology element);

  • the header.parent of the manifests attached to the element or below it;

  • the saved views and the steps of stories of the workspace (the element of a view, the pinned, hidden and open elements of a Map view);

  • what the page remembers: the selection, the unfolded rows, the modified marks, the pins of the Map.

The facets (their JSONPath is not a reference) are not read, as when an id changes. Nothing else of a file is touched.

What is written. There are four ways, chosen by how the manifest holds the element and where the new parent is declared, and the dialog says which one a place gets. The lines that are not part of the move stay as the author wrote them (comments, blank lines, quotes), as for any other edit:

  • An element that has a manifest of its own (app.shop.till.yaml, with its header.parent) stays in its file: only the header.parent line changes (at the top level the line goes), and the manifests attached below it follow by their own header.parent. The new parent can be in any file.

  • An element that is nested in the manifest of another one (in its content.elements) and goes under a parent of the same file is moved as a block inside that manifest: its lines, with the comments just above it and those at the end of its lines, go to the elements of the new parent, one level deeper or shallower, and the elements it leaves is dropped when it was the last element.

  • A nested element that goes under a parent in another file (declared in another manifest of the plateau written) is cut out of its file and pasted in the other, with its comments and its quotes. Both files are marked as modified, and a refused write changes neither.

  • A nested element that goes to the top level, or under a parent declared in a plateau whose files the plateau drawn does not write (a plateau it overrides), gets a manifest of its own, next to the file it leaves (app.<reference>.yaml, env. for the technology layer, and -2 when the name is taken), with its kind, its version, the plateau drawn when it is not baseline and the parent, none at the top level. A group has no kind of manifest, so a group cannot go there: that is #114.

The comments on lines of their own inside the block go with it, and so do the comments after its last line when they belong to it. The rule is the indentation, nothing else (a comment cannot be asked whom it is about): a comment after the last line goes with the block when it is indented deeper than the item, as the keys of the block are, or when it is at the column of the item and nothing follows it in the list; a comment at the column of the item above a sibling is that sibling’s introduction and stays, and so does one that is shallower than the item. The first comment that stays ends the scan, and the blank lines between the block and a comment that goes with it go with it. The rule is wrong in a few cases: a comment about the block written at the column of an item that has a sibling after it stays (it reads as the sibling’s); a comment about the next element indented as the keys of the block goes with the block; a comment at the column of the item, last in its list, goes with the block even when you meant it for the next key of the parent; and a comment deeper than the item after a blank line goes with it, even as a section comment. It is the same for the block that moves to another file and for the one that becomes a manifest of its own. A file whose layout the move cannot follow (an anchor, an alias, a tag, a list written at the column of its key) is written by the ordinary save: the move works and the file says the right thing, and the comments of the block are the price. The end of line of each file is its own.

What a move tells before it writes. A move changes the reference of the element, so the views and the steps of the stories that name it follow it, in every plateau. A plateau beside the one drawn that still declares the old reference can then no longer draw those views, and Move says so as a rename does (see [editor-rename]): it opens a dialog that names those plateaus and the views and steps, and asks before it writes (Back to Move or Move anyway). The plateaus it does not name are not concerned. Moving the element there too keeps the views whole.

Replaces. Move does not write replaces: the element that a move can reach is declared by the plateau that is drawn, and the plateaus it overrides do not have its old reference, so there is nothing for the key to name. In a workspace of plateaus the dialog says so in one line. To say that an element replaces one of another plateau, use Write replaces in the Changes panel, or the Replaces section of the form of the element (see Moved, split and merged elements, and candidates).

A move that is refused changes nothing at all, and the reason is told in the dialog, which stays open with what you chose: the new parent is the element or something inside it, it is its parent already, it cannot hold that kind, it already holds an element with that id, the element or the parent is declared twice or belongs to the plateau compared with, a manifest cannot be read (a reference in it could not be rewritten), a reference is written as a number (destination: 8080) and cannot be rewritten as the reference of another element, or an id has a / or a \ in it, which cannot be the name of a file (rename the element first).

Like every edit, a move is not written until Save all changes, and Cancel gives it up with the others (the folder is read again). The files it changed are marked as modified, and so is the moved element.

Theme

The editor is drawn in a light or a dark theme. By default it follows the one of the operating system. Settings has a section, Appearance (this browser), to choose Light, Dark or System instead: the choice applies at once, to the page, the code editors and the SVG and PNG exported from a diagram.

The choice is a preference of the browser, not of the workspace. It is remembered by the browser alone (nothing is written to .archicode.yaml or to any file of the folder), it is not a change to save, and it does not light Save all changes. A browser that refuses to remember it keeps the choice until the page is closed.

Keyboard

In a tree

  • Up and Down move, Home and End jump to the first and last row.

  • Right unfolds a branch or goes to its first child, Left folds it or goes to its parent.

  • Enter and Space select the element (in the Map, they fold or unfold the branch).

  • In the Map, P pins or unpins the element, and H hides it or shows it again.

In the Changes panel

  • J goes to the next change and K to the previous one: the walk of the buttons Next change and Previous change, in the same order, with the same filters, stopping at the ends. They work on every page that has the list (Plateaus, Applications, Environments, Elements, and Stories while a comparison is on), and from the Selected or View tab they bring the Changes tab back. They do nothing while you type in a field, an editor, a list or a text box, while a dialog, the help or the menu of the header is open, with Ctrl, Alt, Meta or Shift held, or where no list of changes is on the page. The buttons tell the key in their tooltips.

In a diagram

  • The arrow keys move between boxes, and Enter opens the one that has the focus.

  • The boxes are one Tab stop, and so are the links: a diagram takes two presses of Tab to cross, however many boxes and links it has. The arrow keys move from the box that has the focus to the neighbouring box, which becomes the stop. On a link, the arrow keys move to the next and the previous link (not to the neighbouring box), Home and End jump to the first and the last, Enter or Space selects it, and Esc deselects it. Tab goes from the boxes to the links and on, and Shift+Tab comes back.

  • +, - and 0 zoom in, out and back to the actual size. F fits the whole diagram and C goes back to how it opened.

  • M maximizes the diagram, Esc brings the tree back. On the page of a view in Stories, M hides the list of views and the panel, and Esc restores them, or else deselects the box or the link (see Stories: stories and views).

In the story player

  • Left and Right show the previous and the next step, Home and End the first and the last, Esc closes the window and gives the focus back to Play; Tab and Shift+Tab go round inside it. Up and Down are not taken. Nothing else happens while a field, a text box or a list has the focus (the list of the steps has its own arrows, and lets Esc through), while a dialog, the help or the menu of the header is open, or with Ctrl, Alt, Meta or Shift held; J and K do nothing in it (see [editor-story-player]).

Commands

Within the realm of ArchiCode, an array of meticulously designed commands empowers you to wield precision and control over your architectural workspace. Let’s delve into ArchiCode’s command utilities, which enable you to seamlessly generate views and explore the intricacies of your workspace’s data model:

  • View Generation Commands: ArchiCode offers a range of commands dedicated to the seamless generation of views situated within your workspace. These commands enable you to customize the generation of views, providing a streamlined approach to shaping your architectural visualization.

  • Data Model Querying Commands: ArchiCode equips you with specialized commands designed to query the intricate data model of your workspace. These commands serve as a window into your architecture’s foundational elements, facilitating insightful exploration and analysis.

As you delve deeper into the Command section of ArchiCode’s documentation, you’ll uncover the diverse set of commands at your disposal, each contributing to a more intuitive, efficient, and informed architectural workflow. Let’s embark on this journey through the realm of ArchiCode’s commands, discovering how they enhance your ability to shape, explore, and communicate architectural concepts.

Print the version

Print the version - The command line
archicode --version

Prints one line on the standard output, archicode <version>, and exits with the code 0. -V is the short form. The version is the one the program was built from, the same in the native program, the JAR and the OCI Image (a build from a branch says -SNAPSHOT). It reads no workspace file, so it works in any folder. The option belongs to archicode itself: archicode views --version is a usage error like any option that does not exist (see Errors, warnings and exit codes), and the options of archicode --help are -h, -V and -w=<workspace file>, then the command.

Print the guide for an agent

Print the guide - The command line
archicode guide

Prints a guide, in Markdown, on the standard output, and exits with the code 0. The first line is archicode guide <version>, so that whoever reads it knows which version of the tool it describes. It is written for an agent (a program that reads text and writes files) that has nothing else: it is self-sufficient, and the tool does not need anything outside itself to print it. It loads no workspace, so it works in any folder, reads no file and uses no network, and it is the same in the JAR, the OCI Image and the native program.

What is in it, in the order of the text: how to start from nothing (archicode init, see Start a workspace); the loop of writing a manifest, checking that it loads with archicode query graph and drawing with archicode views generate, with the form of an error: line, a warning and the exit codes; the workspace file and its settings; every kind of manifest (the keys, the required ones, the enums, the nesting, the id rule and the reference form); the commands and their options; and a small workspace of eleven files (elements, a plateau that changes them, a story, a saved view and two maps) that shows the shape of each kind of file. The kinds, the nesting, the commands and the options are generated from the code that reads the files, so they cannot disagree with the tool that prints them; the conventions (the loop, what a plateau is, the defaults of --plateau and --against, what the schema cannot say) are text. The tests keep it true: the files of the small workspace are loaded by the command line and by the editor, the commands it shows are run, and the generated parts are compared with the classes. The guide is about 24 KB; the tests keep it under 30 KB, so that an agent can read it whole.

An agent that works on a workspace reads the guide once, then archicode query schemas manifest when it needs the exact content of a kind (see [manifest-schema]). There is no archicode validate command yet: a problem is reported one error: line at a time, the first one found, and the guide says so.

Start a workspace

Start a workspace - The command line
archicode init my-architecture

archicode init [FOLDER] writes a small workspace that loads and is drawn. The folder is created when it does not exist, and the default is the current folder. It writes:

  • .archicode.yaml, with a name and manifests.paths set to the folder manifests;

  • manifests/customer.yaml, a person, and manifests/shop.yaml, a system with one container and a relationship from the person to the system;

  • manifests/view.shop_overview.yaml, a saved view of the system, with the viewpoint overview;

  • the line /views*/ in .gitignore (created when the folder has none, added once to one that exists, and nothing else of it is changed), so that the folders views and views-<plateau> that views generate writes are not committed.

It prints the files it wrote and two commands, to run in the folder: archicode query graph, which checks that the workspace loads, and archicode views generate, which draws it. A saved view is drawn only when it is named, so the view of the example is drawn by archicode views generate -i shop_overview (see Generate views); without -i the command draws the views of the elements. It ends by pointing to archicode guide (see Print the guide for an agent).

init never overwrites. A folder that already holds .archicode.yaml, or one of the three manifests, is one line on the error output, for instance error: the folder my-architecture already holds .archicode.yaml: init writes nothing over a workspace, with the code 1 and nothing written. Edit the files, add manifests, and see [manifest-kinds] for the kinds you can write.

Errors, warnings and exit codes

Every command speaks on its error output in the same way, and keeps its standard output for what it produces (the files it announces, a table, a graph), so that archicode diff > changes.txt or archicode query graph | jq never mixes the two.

Errors. When a command cannot do what you asked, it prints one line for each problem on the error output, starts it with error: ` and exits with the code 1. It prints no stack trace. The exit codes are 0 (done), 1 (an error), 2 (a usage error, below) and 3, which only `archicode diff --fail-on gives, when the comparison holds a change it was asked to fail on (see Compare plateaus). The line says what is wrong and where, for instance:

error: `shop.web` has a relationship towards `payments`, which does not exist, in `manifests/shop.web.yaml` at `relationships[0].destination`
error: unknown plateau `nope`: the plateaus of this workspace are `baseline` and `to-be`
error: `manifests/shop.yaml` is not valid: the value has the wrong shape (found an array) at `header` (line 1, column 9)
error: the workspace file `/work/.archicode.yaml` does not exist (`-w` names another one)
error: the workspace file `/work/.archicode.yaml` cannot be read: permission denied

The last two lines are the two ways the command can fail to have the workspace file: there is no file under that name (a folder in its place counts), or there is one that the file system refuses to read (the reason is the one the system gives). The second used to be error: unexpected java.io.FileNotFoundException …​ with a stack trace, which is for a bug; it is a line like the others after 0.18.0. A folder that has workspace.yaml and no .archicode.yaml has its own line (see [upgrade-0-17]). A file that is there and is read, but is wrong, says what is wrong with it (error: `.archicode.yaml` is not valid: ...).

The same sentence in the editor. A manifest or a .archicode.yaml that the command line refuses, the editor refuses with the same sentence, without the file and the line (the editor shows it beside the file): a key that the element, the setting or the header does not have, a nested element whose kind is missing or none of those its parent takes, a value of the wrong shape, a header.kind that is none of the kinds, a key that is required and absent. The place is told by its path in the file, as the command line does:

unknown key `nme` (known: description, id, name, qualifiers, relationships, replaces, tags) at `nme`
unknown kind `containr` (known: container) at `elements[0]`
missing `kind` (known: container) at `elements[0]`
unrecognized `header.kind` "archicode.morin.io/persn" (known: archicode.morin.io/component, ...)
the value has the wrong shape (found a string) at `settings.views.labels`
Missing content.elements[0].id

An option that does not exist or a missing argument is a usage error: the command prints the problem and how to use the command, as it always did, and exits with the code 2. A failure that nobody expected, a bug, prints error: unexpected …​ and keeps its stack trace, because a report needs it; the exit code is 1.

Warnings. A warning says that the command went on but that something is probably not what you meant. It is one line starting with warning: ` on the error output, printed when the command has done its work (and before the error, if it fails), once for a sentence the command would say many times. The log is not where to look: the log level of the application is `ERROR, which hides the warnings of the log. A warning does not change the exit code. The command line warns of:

  • a step of a story that names a view nobody declares;

  • a step of a story that archicode stories generate skips: it names an element that the plateau of the step does not have, or it is told at a plateau and compared with that same plateau; or, for one facet only, a facet that removes the element it names (see Draw the stories);

  • a view that cannot be drawn in the plateau, because it names an element that the plateau does not have and another plateau has: skipped by views generate --view, or, for the entries of the lists of a map, left out (see [plateau-views-lacking]);

  • a viewpoint that the command line has no service for, skipped by views generate --view;

  • what a comparison ignored in the replaces of a plateau, with archicode diff and archicode views generate (see [plateau-replaces-warnings]);

  • a state word you wrote by hand on an element, where it disagrees with the comparison (see [plateau-state-tags]).

A command that has nothing to warn of prints nothing on its error output. Nothing here makes a warning fail a command: the exit code tells whether the command did its work.

Serve the editor

Serve the editor - The command line
archicode editor serve

Serves the editor (see Editor) and opens in it the folder of the workspace file, which must be named .archicode.yaml (-w can name that file in another folder; any other name is a usage error, see [upgrade-0-17]). The command prints a URL, http://127.0.0.1:48080/?token=…​;: open it in a browser (the page is refused without the token; the editor removes it from the address bar, and a session cookie, which opens the page only and never the API, lets a reload in).

  • --port (-p): the port to listen on, 48080 by default.

  • --host: the address to bind to, 0.0.0.0 by default, which a Docker -p mapping needs.

  • --allowed-host: a host name, besides localhost, 127.0.0.1 and [::1], that a request may be addressed to. Repeat it for several.

The server writes to the folder, so it does not trust the network: the page and every call carry a token that only the printed URL gives, a request must be addressed to one of the allowed host names, a page of another origin is refused, and only the .yaml, .yml and .json files below the folder of the workspace can be reached. With Docker, keep the host side of the port mapping on 127.0.0.1 (-p 127.0.0.1:48080:48080).

Generate views

Generate Views - The command line
archicode views generate --help

The "Generate Views" command within ArchiCode serves as a dynamic catalyst for creating comprehensive architectural views. This command is designed to efficiently produce visual representations of your architecture, aiding communication and understanding. Here’s a comprehensive breakdown of its functionality:

  • Triggering View Generation: The "Generate Views" command is the key that unlocks the process of generating architectural views. Upon invocation, ArchiCode springs into action, initiating the creation of visual artifacts that encapsulate your architectural design.

  • Default Behavior: By default, this command leverages the power of discovery. It automatically identifies and generates views based on the elements present within both the Application Layer and the Technology Layer of your architecture.

  • Inclusive Viewpoint Generation: The default behavior extends to encompass all available viewpoints. This means that ArchiCode generates views for a comprehensive range of architectural perspectives, enabling a holistic portrayal of your design.

  • Built-in Renderer Integration: By default, the "Generate Views" command uses the ArchiCode’s Plantuml Renderer. This integration ensures that the generated views are easily understandable and sharable.

  • Organized Output: By default, the command orchestrates the organization of generated views within a dedicated "views" folder, nested within your workspace folder. This structured arrangement enhances clarity and enables efficient access to your visual representations.

The command offers also customization Capabilities:

  • Plateau: when the workspace has plateaus (see Plateaus), --plateau <id> chooses the one that is read. Without it, it is the plateau settings.current names, else the last one when the plateaus form one line, else the command asks for a choice (see [plateau-choose]). The views of a plateau are written in a folder of its own, views-<plateau> (and one folder per facet, views-<plateau>-<facet> with the default directory-name-template); a workspace without plateaus writes views as it always did.

Generate Views - Plateau
archicode views generate --plateau to-be

  • Comparison: a plateau other than the root is compared with the plateau it overrides, or with --against <id>, any other plateau (see Comparing two plateaus). The command then writes the plain views of the plateau, as above, and in addition the views of the comparison, which draw what is new, changed and removed. They are written in views-<plateau>-vs-<against> (and one folder per facet, views-<plateau>-vs-<against>-<facet>), next to two files that tell the same in text: gap.md and gap.json, the table of changes that archicode diff prints (see Compare plateaus), with the elements that moved, split or merged, the candidates (see Saying what an element replaces) and the cascade of the REMOVE patches (see [plateau-cascade]), and the same warnings about replaces on the error output. A workspace that sets settings.facets.global-enabled: false has nothing but these two files in views-<plateau>-vs-<against>: its views are in the folders of its facets. The root overrides nothing, so it is drawn alone unless --against names a plateau to compare it with (--plateau baseline --against to-be writes views-baseline-vs-to-be). An unknown plateau given to --against, the plateau itself, or --against on a workspace that has no plateau, stops the command before anything is written. --output is, with plateaus, the beginning of the name of these folders (see Manifests in folders for what it means for the manifests that a path with /** reads).

Generate Views - Comparison
archicode views generate --plateau to-be --against baseline

A facet applies to the views of a comparison as it does to the others, and a facet can select on change (see Comparing two plateaus).

  • Only the changes: --only-changes draws, in the views of the comparison only (views-<plateau>-vs-<against> and the folders of its facets), what changed: the new, changed, removed, moved, split and merged elements and relationships, the ends of their links, the elements that hold a change and what encloses them. The rest is kept as context and drawn faded, and a view with nothing to show still draws its own element. The views of the plateau itself and the table of changes (gap.md and gap.json) are as they are without the option. The option needs a comparison: with no plateau, or with the root and no --against, it is one error: line, and nothing is written.

Generate Views - Only the changes
archicode views generate --plateau to-be --against baseline --only-changes

  • Selective View Rendering: The power of customization lies in your hands. The command can be tailored to render specific views based on their unique identifier, providing you with control over the scope of generated visualizations. A view that a manifest or .archicode.yaml names (its id), a map included (see [map-viewpoint]), is drawn when it is asked for, and the views asked for are the only ones drawn: no built-in view comes with them. A view that cannot be drawn in the plateau is skipped with a warning (see [plateau-views-lacking]).

Generate Views - Selective View Rendering
archicode views generate --view=view_a --view=view_b

  • Renderer Engine Flexibility: You have the freedom to opt for a preferred Renderer Engine. The command allows you to specify a particular engine, aligning the rendering process with your architectural preferences.

Generate Views - Renderer Engine Flexibility
archicode views generate --engine=kubernetes-network-policies

  • Fine-Tuned View Properties: Further customization extends to individual view properties. ArchiCode empowers you to specify custom properties, ensuring that the generated views align precisely with your architectural objectives.

Generate Views - Fine-Tuned View Properties
archicode views generate --properties='{ "key" : "value" }'

The same workspace gives the same files. Generating the views of a workspace twice gives the same files, byte for byte, so that a workspace kept in git only shows a change when the workspace changed. This does not depend on the folder the workspace is in, on the order a file system lists the manifests in, on the JVM that runs the command, nor on the order in which the author wrote the elements of a list, the relationships of an element or its tags. Inside a file, the boxes come by reference and then by identifier, the links by their ends, their label, their qualifiers and their tags, and the styles by the name of the stereotype. What the author writes in an order keeps it: the qualifiers of an element and the words of rendering-stereotypes are the order of the stereotypes of its box (see Stereotypes and styles), so reordering them is a change of the workspace. Only a new version of ArchiCode rewrites files that the workspace did not change: the order of the stereotypes of a box changed in 0.10.0 and in 0.11.0, and the order of two links that only differ by the node they end on changed in 0.12.0 (it used to depend on the JVM). A saved map of the technology layer draws more since 0.15.0 (see [map-technology]). A comparison of two plateaus several layers apart reads the replaces of the layers in between since 0.16.0 (see Saying what an element replaces). A test (DeterministicGenerationTest) generates a workspace twice, with the manifests, the elements, the relationships and the tags declared in the reverse order and in another folder, and compares the files.

Query views

Query Views - The command line
archicode query views --help

The "Query Views" command within ArchiCode offers a versatile toolkit for precise exploration and customization of architectural views. Here’s an in-depth look at the capabilities and features of the "Query Views" command:

  • Default Behavior: As a testament to flexibility, the "Query Views" command, by default, refrains from applying any filtering or formatting. This ensures that you have the freedom to explore your architectural views without any predetermined constraints.

  • Focused Exploration: The essence of the "Query Views" command lies in its ability to fine-tune your exploration. Designed with precision in mind, this command empowers you to filter and tailor your view output according to your architectural inquiries.

The command offers also customization Capabilities:

  • Plateau: like views generate, the command takes --plateau <id> to choose the plateau that is read (see [plateau-choose]); so does query graph.

  • Dynamic Filtering: The command harnesses the power of JSON Path, enabling you to apply intricate filters that delve deep into the View model. This dynamic filtering mechanism facilitates targeted extraction of specific architectural insights.

Query Views - Dynamic Filtering
archicode query views -q '$.[?(@.viewpoint=="deep")]'

  • Customizable Output Formats: ArchiCode’s "Query Views" command extends flexibility by supporting various output formats, including YAML, TOML, JSON, and a customizable option. This ensures that your extracted data aligns seamlessly with your preferred format.

Query Views - Customizable Output Formats
archicode query views -f toml

  • Template-Based Customization: The command’s customizable output format integrates a template-based approach. By leveraging the Velocity template engine, you can define specific output structures tailored to your architectural needs.

Query Views - Template-Based Customization
archicode query views -f custom -t '$d.e("$.[*].id")'

  • Sorted Output: the views come sorted, by identifier and then by layer (application before technology), in every output format and before the JSON Path query is applied, so that $.[0] is always the same view. Two runs, on the same machine or not, and two builds of the same version give the same output byte for byte, which lets you diff it or keep it in git. Before 0.14.0 the order of the views was not defined and changed from one build to another; the set of views did not. The order of your scripts' results changes once, when you upgrade; if a script relies on the position of a view, select it by its id instead.

  • TOML Output: a TOML document is a table, so -f toml writes the list of views under the key views, as views = [{ id = '…​', …​ }, …​]; a JSON Path query that selects one view writes that view as a table, and one that selects anything else (a list of identifiers, say) is written under views too. Before 0.14.0 the list was written as an assignment without a key, which no TOML parser reads. The TOML output changes once, when you upgrade; the YAML, JSON and custom outputs did not change.

Query graph

Query Graph - The command line
archicode query graph --help

The "Query Graph" command prints the resolved elements and relationships of the workspace as JSON, with --plateau <id> to choose the plateau that is read. A relationship whose destination does not exist is an error, as it is for the views.

Query Graph - Print the graph
archicode query graph

  • Sorted Output: the elements come by layer (application, then technology) and, in a layer, by reference; the relationships come by the reference of their source in the same order and, for one source, as the author wrote them. Two runs and two builds of the same version give the same output byte for byte, which lets you diff it or keep it in git. Before 0.14.0 the elements came in the hash order of their references: the same for one workspace and one JDK, but not a promised one. The order of the output changes once, when you upgrade; the elements and the relationships did not change.

Draw the stories

Draw the stories - The command line
archicode stories generate --help

views generate does not draw the stories. archicode stories generate [--story <id>]…​ draws the steps of the stories of the workspace, one folder for each step:

  • A step is drawn like a view of the plateau it is told at: its at, else --plateau, else settings.current (see [story-step-plateaus]). A plateau is loaded once, however many steps are told at it. When the step says against, it is drawn like the views of a comparison of that plateau with the against one (views generate --plateau <at> --against <against>); the comparison is made once for the pair, and the table of its changes, gap.md and gap.json, is written in the folder of the step, beside its plantuml/ folder, byte for byte what views generate writes in views-<at>-vs-<against> for the same pair (a step that compares nothing has none).

  • The files go to <views.path>-stories/<story>/<NN>-<view>/, with views.path read without the -<plateau> that a workspace of plateaus adds (views-stories for the default), NN the number of the step from 01, and <view> the view the step names, else the viewpoint of the view it carries (whose file is step_NN.puml). The renderer then adds its own folders as it does for any view (plantuml/application/…​).

  • The facets of the workspace (settings.facets) are drawn as views generate draws them: next to the folder of a step, one folder per facet, <NN>-<view>-<facet> (the name follows directory-name-template), with the same views in it; for a step that compares, the facet is applied to the comparison, and no table is written in a facet folder. A facet that removes the element a step names is a warning, warning: skip fork_tour/2 in the facet `no-externals`: it names `commerce-saas`, which the facet removes, and only that facet’s folder is skipped. global-enabled: false is honoured as it is by views generate: the folder of the global views is not drawn and the folders of the facets are. In 0.17.0 a workspace with that setting still got the global folders of its steps; it does not any more. When the global views are off and the workspace has no facet either, nothing is drawn, and the command says so once: warning: the global views are turned off (`global-enabled: false`) and the workspace has no facet: the steps of the stories are not drawn. --output (-o) replaces the beginning of the folder name, as in views generate, and --engine (-e) chooses the renderer.

  • A step may say highlight, a list of entries as the highlight of a map (see [map-highlight]), for a view of the map viewpoint: it replaces the highlight of the saved view the step names (the saved view is untouched), and an empty highlight: [] switches that highlight off. For a step that carries its own recipe, the highlight of the step wins over a properties.highlight of the same step. A story can thus keep one view and move the highlight from step to step. The entries are checked as in a view, and one that the plateau of the step lacks is the warning of [map-highlight].

  • --story (-s) draws the stories named, repeated for several; with none, every story is drawn, in the order of their ids. An unknown story is one error: line and exit 1, before anything is written: error: no story `nope` in the workspace (known: migration).

  • A tree of plateaus that forks needs --plateau, as every command that loads a plateau (see [plateau-choose]), even when all the steps say at.

  • A step that cannot be drawn is a warning and is skipped; the others are drawn and the exit code is 0: warning: skip fork_tour/7: it is told at the plateau `saas` and compared with it: there is nothing to compare and warning: skip <story>/<n>: it names `x`, which the plateau `p` does not have. A story with no step is a warning too.

Draw the stories of the example domain_plateaus
archicode stories generate

writes views-stories/migration/01-overview/ to 04-overview/ (the third is 03-detailed/: that step uses the detailed viewpoint), one for each step of the story migration. On the layered Brightcart, archicode stories generate --story fork_tour --plateau saas draws the seven steps of fork_tour in views-stories/fork_tour/01-shop_overview/ to 07-overview/, the last one skipped with the warning above, since it is told at saas and compared with it. Each step also has the folders of the three facets of the workspace (01-shop_overview-journey, 01-shop_overview-no-externals, 01-shop_overview-without-cart), and the steps 02 to 06, which compare two plateaus, have a gap.md and a gap.json beside their plantuml/ folder (the step 02 has no folder for no-externals: it names commerce-saas, which that facet removes, and the command warns). On domain_plateaus, whose story migration compares steps 2 and 4 with baseline, 02-overview and 04-overview have the two files and there is no facet.

List the plateaus

List the plateaus - The command line
archicode plateau list

Lists the plateaus of the workspace, one per line, in the order of the tree: baseline, then what overrides it, each plateau followed by what overrides it, and plateaus that override the same one by their ids. The columns are PLATEAU, PARENT (the plateau it overrides), SOURCE (manifest <file>, headers only (<files>) for a plateau that only headers name, or implicit for the root that nothing names), NAME and HORIZON, with - for what is absent; the output is in [plateau-choose]. The warnings of the tree (see Plateaus) go to the error output. A workspace that names no plateau lists baseline alone.

Important
 archicode plateau add no longer exists: it copied a complete folder into settings.plateaus, and the plateaus are layers now. To add a plateau, write a manifest of kind archicode.morin.io/plateau (id, name, override, patches) and the manifests that say plateau: <id> in their header (see States of an architecture (plateaus)), or use the editor (see The band of plateaus).

Compare plateaus

Compare plateaus - The command line
archicode diff --plateau to-be --against baseline

Tells what a plateau changes: the elements and the relationships that are new, changed or removed compared with the other plateau, and what changed in each. An element that says what it replaces (see Saying what an element replaces) is moved, split or merged. It prints a table to read in a terminal, to paste in a pull request or to feed to a script, and writes nothing to disk (views generate writes the same table next to the views, see Generate views).

comparing to-be (To be) with baseline (As is)
elements      +2 ~2 -2
replacing     2 moved, 2 split, 1 merged
relationships +1 ~1 -1
wording only  1 of the 2 changed elements

CHANGE   THING         LAYER        REFERENCE                                   NATURES                                                                     DETAILS
merged   element       application  shop.billing                                -                                                                           from shop.invoices, shop.receipts; name: "Billing"
split    element       application  shop.cart                                   -                                                                           from shop.orders; name: "Cart"; split with shop.checkout
changed  element       application  shop.catalog                                description (wording only)                                                  description: "The books on sale." -> "The books that are on sale."
split    element       application  shop.checkout                               -                                                                           from shop.orders; name: "Checkout"; split with shop.cart
removed  element       application  shop.mailer                                 -                                                                           name: "Mailer"
new      element       application  shop.payments                               -                                                                           name: "Payments"
new      element       application  shop.ratings                                -                                                                           name: "Ratings"
removed  element       application  shop.reviews                                -                                                                           name: "Reviews"
changed  element       application  shop.web                                    qualifiers,relationships-added,relationships-removed,relationships-changed  qualifiers: +React
moved    element       application  warehouse.inventory                         -                                                                           from shop.inventory; name: "Inventory"
moved    element       application  warehouse.inventory.levels                  -                                                                           from shop.inventory.levels; name: "Levels"
changed  relationship  application  shop.web -> shop.catalog (browses)          label                                                                       label: "reads" -> "browses"
removed  relationship  application  shop.web -> shop.mailer (sends mails with)  -
new      relationship  application  shop.web -> shop.payments (pays with)       -

candidates, not applied (each pair looks like one thing said twice; to accept one, write `replaces: [FROM]` on the element TO)
LAYER        FROM          TO            REASONS
application  shop.reviews  shop.ratings  description

cascade of the removals (what a REMOVE took besides the elements it selects, in the plateaus the target adds to the one it is compared with)
PLATEAU  PATCH                           EFFECT   LAYER        ELEMENT                DETAIL
to-be    1 REMOVE target shop.mailer     dropped  application  shop.web               relationships: shop.mailer (sends mails with)
to-be    6 REMOVE target shop.inventory  removed  application  shop.inventory.levels
to-be    6 REMOVE target shop.inventory  dropped  application  warehouse.picking      relationships: shop.inventory (counts with)

  • --plateau: the plateau that is compared, as for views generate (see [plateau-choose]): settings.current, else the last one when the plateaus form one line.

  • --against: the plateau it is compared with: any plateau of the tree, a sibling or the future included; else the plateau it overrides (see Comparing two plateaus). The root overrides nothing, so --plateau baseline needs it.

  • --format (-f): text (the default; without --width its lines are as long as the longest reference, several hundred characters on a large workspace: wrap it with --width, or take markdown), markdown (a table to paste in a pull request) or json (the counts and one object per change, with before and after for a changed element).

  • --width <columns>: wraps the text table to that many columns, so that a long reference does not push the table off the screen (--width 100; $COLUMNS is not read, give the number). Without it the table is written as before, byte for byte. A cell wraps at its spaces; a word that has none, a reference of a deep element, is cut after a dot, a slash or a comma, then after a hyphen or an underscore, and last where the line ends, so that nothing is lost and a line read back without its line breaks is the cell. The columns share the width: each keeps at least what its heading needs, and what is left goes to the narrowest columns first, so that the change, the thing and the layer stay on one line where the details wrap. The sentences of the report (the candidates, the cascade) wrap too. Under about 50 columns the headings of the main table do not fit and the lines are longer than the width; the cells still wrap at their column and nothing is cut off. A width that is not a whole number above zero is one error: line, with the exit code 1, before anything is read. The markdown and the json formats are never wrapped, and it is an option of diff alone: views generate writes its table as Markdown and JSON only, so it has no --width.

  • --no-wording: leaves the elements whose description is the only thing that changed out of the table, and says how many are left out. The counts still include them. It changes nothing to the elements that moved, split or merged, nor to the candidates.

  • --fail-on <state>[,<state>…​]: makes the command a check for a build. It exits with the code 3 when the comparison holds a change of one of the states it lists, and with 0 otherwise. The states are new, changed, removed, moved, split, merged (the six states of a row), contains-changes (an element that did not change itself and holds a change), and any, which stands for the six of a row. The option may be repeated, and the words are in lower case. With --no-wording, a description that is the only thing that changed does not count. The table is printed as it is without the option, and nothing on the error output says why the build failed: the table does. An unknown or an empty state is one error: line and the exit code 1, before anything is read.

A wrapped table. The first rows of the table above with archicode diff --plateau to-be --against baseline --width 100 (the other rows, the candidates and the cascade wrap the same way):

CHANGE   THING         LAYER        REFERENCE             NATURES               DETAILS
merged   element       application  shop.billing          -                     from shop.invoices,
                                                                                shop.receipts; name:
                                                                                "Billing"
split    element       application  shop.cart             -                     from shop.orders;
                                                                                name: "Cart"; split
                                                                                with shop.checkout
changed  element       application  shop.catalog          description (wording  description: "The
                                                          only)                 books on sale." ->
                                                                                "The books that are
                                                                                on sale."

The table lists the elements, then the relationships, sorted by layer and reference (the reference of an element that moved, split or merged is its own, not the one it replaces): the same output for the same two plateaus, byte for byte, whatever the machine.

When elements replace others. The table above, of the example of the manual, has them already (see States of an architecture (plateaus)). A second invented workspace, the warehouse of src/test/resources/plateau-comparison/replaces in the source repository, has a till that moved to the stock system and was renamed, a report that was split in two (and its chart, which nothing replaces, so it is removed), a bin and a shelf that were merged into a place, a mailer that was rewritten as a postman without a word about it, and a replaces that names an element the baseline never had. archicode diff --plateau after prints:

warning: replaces of shop.report-b (application layer): shop.nowhere is not an element of the base plateau, ignored
comparing after with before
elements      +1 ~0 -2
replacing     1 moved, 2 split, 1 merged
relationships +0 ~0 -0
wording only  0 of the 0 changed elements

CHANGE   THING    LAYER        REFERENCE          NATURES  DETAILS
removed  element  application  shop.mailer        -        name: "Mailer"
new      element  application  shop.postman       -        name: "Postman"
split    element  application  shop.report-a      -        from shop.report; name: "Sales report"; split with shop.report-b
split    element  application  shop.report-b      -        from shop.report; name: "Stock report"; split with shop.report-a
removed  element  application  shop.report.chart  -        name: "Chart"
merged   element  application  stock.place        -        from stock.bin, stock.shelf; name: "Place"
moved    element  application  stock.till         name     from shop.till; name: "Till" -> "Cash desk"

candidates, not applied (each pair looks like one thing said twice; to accept one, write `replaces: [FROM]` on the element TO)
LAYER        FROM         TO            REASONS
application  shop.mailer  shop.postman  description, parent

cascade of the removals (what a REMOVE took besides the elements it selects, in the plateaus the target adds to the one it is compared with)
PLATEAU  PATCH                        EFFECT   LAYER        ELEMENT            DETAIL
after    1 REMOVE target shop.till    dropped  application  shop.web           relationships: shop.till (takes payments with)
after    1 REMOVE target shop.till    dropped  technology   prod.cluster       applications: shop.till
after    4 REMOVE target shop.report  removed  application  shop.report.chart

  • The first line is a warning, on the error output; the rest is the table, on the standard output.

  • The replacing line, and in the Markdown form the line "Replacing elements: …​", exist only when an element replaces another. The elements that replaced others are in the replacing counts and not in the new, changed or removed ones, and the elements they replace have no row and no count: they are not removed.

  • The details of a row begin with from what the element replaces, then its name, then what differs from what it replaces (name: "Till" → "Cash desk"), and for a split the other elements that replace the same one.

  • The candidates are listed after the table, marked not applied: write replaces on the element in the TO column to accept one (see [plateau-candidates]). The mailer and the postman are still removed and new.

  • In JSON, a row of an element that replaces others has a replaces list (the references, sorted), the counts of elements have moved, split and merged when there are some, and candidates is a list of layer, from, to, reasons and "applied": false. In Markdown, the candidates are a section, ## Candidates, not applied.

  • The relationships of a split element are not compared with the other plateau (which half would own them is a guess), so those of the halves, and those that end on a half, read as new. When there are some, the header has a line of a split (in Markdown, a sentence under the counts; in JSON, counts.relationships.of_split) that says how many of the new relationships they are, and each of those rows ends with of the split and the element or elements concerned (in JSON, of_split, a list). Nothing is invented as removed, and the counts of new still include them. The editor’s Changes panel says the same under its counts and on each row.

  • A plateau that does not use replaces gets the table it always had, byte for byte. A difference is not an error: the exit code is 0, unless --fail-on asks otherwise (the exit code is then 3, see above). The command stops with an error (exit code 1) that says what to do when the workspace has no plateau, when the plateau is the root and --against names none, and when the plateau named by --against is unknown or is the plateau itself.

Fold a plateau into the one above

Fold a plateau - The command line
archicode plateau fold inhouse --dry-run   # read the plan, write nothing
archicode plateau fold inhouse             # fold it

archicode plateau fold <id> folds the plateau <id> into the plateau that overrides it: the elements the folded plateau declares become elements of the plateau above, its patches go in front of the patches of the plateau above, and its manifest is deleted. The plateau above then overrides what the folded plateau overrode. The command does what [fold-by-hand] does by hand, and then checks the result. With --dry-run it does everything but the write: it prepares the fold, checks it and prints the report, and the folder, its manifests and .archicode.yaml are the same afterwards, byte for byte. Run it first, and read the plan.

A plateau that cannot be folded is refused with one line on the error output and exit code 1, and nothing on the standard output. The first six refusals are checked in this order, and the first one found is the only line:

error: unknown plateau `saaz`: the plateaus of this workspace are `baseline`, `inhouse`, `marketplace` and `saas`
error: the plateau `baseline` is the root of the plateaus: nothing is below it to fold it into
error: the plateau `marketplace` is not overridden by any plateau, so there is no plateau above it to fold it into: to remove it, use Delete in the editor
error: the plateau `inhouse` is overridden by `inhouse-alt` and `marketplace`, which do not stack: folding it into one would change the other. Keep it
error: `settings.current` names the plateau `inhouse`: write another plateau in `.archicode.yaml` first (`marketplace` is the one above it)
error: the plateau `inhouse` is named by the story `fork_tour` (`shared/story.fork_tour.yaml`) step 3 (`at`), step 4 (`at`) and step 5 (`against`), and the story `seller_journey` (`shared/story.seller_journey.yaml`) step 3 (`against`): change those steps first, none is rewritten for you

The sentences name the plateaus of the layered Brightcart of the repository, a test fixture; on your workspace they name yours. There the two stories fork_tour and seller_journey name inhouse, so its fold is refused with the last sentence. Once those steps are changed (the test replaces each story by one step told at baseline), archicode plateau fold inhouse --dry-run prints:

plateau fold inhouse into marketplace (dry run: nothing was written)

after: `marketplace` overrides `baseline`; the plateaus that stay are `baseline` and `saas`

files rewritten (they lose their comments, quotes and layout):
  inhouse/application/accounts.yaml
  inhouse/application/commerce.cart.yaml
  inhouse/application/commerce.catalog.yaml
  inhouse/application/commerce.checkout.yaml
  inhouse/application/commerce.pricing.yaml
  inhouse/application/commerce.search.yaml
  inhouse/application/commerce.yaml
  inhouse/application/orders.yaml
  inhouse/application/payments.yaml
  inhouse/technology/prod.cloud.yaml
  inhouse/technology/staging.k8s.yaml
  marketplace/application/discovery.yaml
  marketplace/plateau.yaml

files deleted:
  inhouse/plateau.yaml (its name "Build commerce services" and its horizon "2026-Q4" are not kept)

patches:
  the 26 patches of `inhouse` go in front of the 23 of `marketplace`: 49

replaces to compose:
  discovery.search (marketplace/application/discovery.yaml): commerce.search -> shop.app.search

plateaus removed:
  inhouse: a command line or a CI file that names it will fail
  the default --against of `marketplace` changes from `inhouse` to `baseline`

checked on a temporary copy before writing: the models of the 3 plateaus that remain and the 6 comparisons between them are the same before and after, but for the PLATEAU and PATCH columns of the cascade

Without --dry-run the report is the same text in the past tense: its first line ends in (written), the title of the first block is files rewritten (they lost their comments, quotes and layout):, and two lines close it:

Read what changed with `git diff`, and run `archicode plateau list` and `archicode diff`

Read the report as follows:

  • The first line says whether anything was written. The after line names the plateau above the folded one and the plateaus that stay.

  • files rewritten lists the files the fold rewrites: the manifests whose header names the folded plateau, and the manifest of the plateau above. A rewritten manifest keeps its format (a TOML manifest stays TOML, a JSON one stays JSON) but loses its comments, its quotes and its layout, as the editor does when it writes a file again from scratch.

  • files deleted lists the manifest of the folded plateau, and says what it takes with it: the name and the horizon of the folded plateau are not kept, and the plateau above keeps its own.

  • patches counts the patches of the folded plateau that go in front of those of the plateau above.

  • replaces to compose lists the entries of the plateau above that name an element of the folded plateau, with the file that holds them; the fold composes each one, as step 3 of [fold-by-hand] does.

  • plateaus removed says what names the folded plateau and breaks: a command line or a CI file, and the default --against of the plateau above.

  • The last block says what was checked.

What is checked. Before it writes anything, the command folds on a temporary copy and compares the plateaus that stay: the model of each plateau, and the table of changes of every pair of them, are read before and after. They must be the same, but for the PLATEAU and PATCH columns of the cascade of the removals, which name the folded plateau. The check runs in the dry run too. A fold that fails it is refused with one line that names the difference, and no file is written; nothing can force it, repair the cause the line names. Three more refusals come after the first six:

error: the fold of `a` into `b` would leave the plateau `c` unreadable: <the sentence of the loader>: no file is written
error: the fold of `a` into `b` would change the plateau `b`: before the fold it has application element extra.leg {"id":"leg","kind":"container","name":"Legacy"}, after it application element extra.leg {"description":"Retired","id":"leg","kind":"container","name":"Legacy"}: no file is written
error: the fold of `a` into `b` would change how the plateau `b` compares with `baseline`: <line> becomes <line or nothing>: no file is written

The first is for a plateau that stays and does not load once the fold is made; the second is for a model that changes, here a patch of the folded plateau that selects by json-path and would, after the fold, reach an element of the plateau above too; the third is for a comparison that changes. A plateau that does not load as it is, before the fold, is refused with the sentence of the loader, as for every command:

error: in the plateau `c`, `bad` has a relationship towards `nowhere`, which does not exist, in `c/bad.yaml` at `relationships[0].destination`

What the guarantee is. A fold writes all its files or none. The command writes each new file next to its target under the name <file>.archicode-fold, moves each over its target, and deletes the manifest of the folded plateau last. If a write fails, it puts back every file already moved and stops with error: the fold stopped at `<path> (<the reason of the file system>): every file is as it was`; if putting a file back fails too, the line lists the files that may be partly written, and says to restore them with git. A file that changed between the moment the command read it and the moment it writes is refused (error: `marketplace/plateau.yaml changed while the fold was prepared: no file is written, run the command again`). The guarantee is for a failure of a write, not for a process that is killed while it moves the files: then a file may be left as it was written last, and a .archicode-fold file may remain, which is no manifest and is read by nothing. Git is the safety net: fold in a clean tree, read git diff, and git restore what you do not want.

What it does not do. It folds into the plateau directly above and no further; it does not rewrite a story or a view that names the folded plateau (it refuses, and you change those steps first); it does not keep the name and the horizon of the folded plateau; and it does not keep the comments, the quotes and the layout of the files it rewrites. The editor has no button for it (see Limits of today).

Generate JSON Schemas

JSON Schemas - The command line
archicode query schemas --help

The "Generate JSON Schema" command within ArchiCode unlocks a world of enhanced coding experiences, seamlessly integrating precision and structure into your architectural design process. Here’s a comprehensive look at its capabilities:

  • Workspace Model Schema: With the "Generate JSON Schema" command, ArchiCode empowers you to effortlessly create the JSON Schema of the Workspace metamodel.

JSON Schemas - Generate the JSON Schema for the Workspace metamodel
archicode query schemas workspace

  • Manifest Model Schema: the command further generates the JSON Schema of a manifest. A manifest is a header and a content, and the content depends on header.kind, which the dialect of the schema cannot say (it is an older JSON Schema dialect, the one the tool has always printed, with no oneOf: the schema of the workspace is in the same dialect). So the schema does not choose for you: it carries the content of every one of the ten kinds side by side, and you pick the one that header.kind names.

JSON Schemas - Generate the JSON Schema for the Manifest metamodel
archicode query schemas manifest

The output is one JSON object. properties gives the envelope: the header and a content typed as an object. Under kinds there is one entry for each kind, keyed by its header.kind (archicode.morin.io/person, solution, system, container, component, environment, node, view, story and plateau), and kinds[<header.kind>].content is the schema of its content: the keys, the ones that are required, the enums (viewpoint, layer, the operations of a patch), the shapes (a text, a list of maps, a map) and a description of the id and of a reference. A reference is the ids from the root of the layer joined by dots (shop.web.api), an id holds no dot, and a reference must exist in the plateau. An element kind also says what it holds (holds: the kinds that can be nested in its elements), whether it can sit at the top of its layer (heldAtTheTop; in a workspace that has plateaus, a container, a component or a node never does, see [nesting]) and its layer. The elements nested in elements have a schema of their own under nested, so the content of a container points at #/nested/component, and the nesting is written once. The keys that a kind does not have are refused (additionalProperties: false), as the loader refuses them, except for a view and a story, which ignore them.

The schema describes one file, and the loader checks the whole workspace. What a schema cannot say, and where the loader says it instead, is part of the output of archicode guide (see Print the guide for an agent). Ten entries: a reference that does not resolve (the loader says unable to find the element …​ or dangling reference); a reference, a plateau, a view or a story declared twice (see [duplicate-references]); a header.parent that is not found, or a parent that cannot hold the kind (see [nesting]); a root that its layer does not hold in a workspace of plateaus; the tree of the plateaus (a plateau declared twice, a missing or circular override, a typo of an id; see Plateaus); a patch that selects nothing or changes an id or a parent, and the rule that a patch has a target or a json-path but not both (see [plateau-patches]); a view or a story step that names an element or a plateau that exists nowhere; .archicode.yaml, which is not a manifest and has the schema of archicode query schemas workspace; the name of a facet, which must be one folder name (see Tackling view clutter); and what a value means (a json-path that is not RFC 9535, a value that does not fit, a horizon earlier than the one below it). A file that conforms to the schema can still be refused by the command that loads it, with one error: line (see Errors, warnings and exit codes). The tests keep this list true: each limit is tied to the cases of the negative corpus that the loader refuses for that reason and not for the shape of a file.

  • Sorted Output: the properties of every object of the schema come in alphabetical order, so that two runs, and two builds of the same version, give the same schema byte for byte and you can diff it or keep it in git. Before 0.14.0 the properties of some objects (the elements) changed order on about one run in three; the schema did not otherwise change. The order of the properties changes once, when you upgrade.

  • IDE Integration and Coding: By integrating the generated JSON Schema into your Integrated Development Environment (IDE), your coding experience is enriched. The schema acts as a guiding framework, offering autocompletion, validation, and insights that elevate your coding accuracy and efficiency.

Best Practices

Welcome to the "Best Practices" segment of ArchiCode, where we embark on a journey of refining your architectural prowess and optimizing your workflow. This section delves into a comprehensive array of practices that elevate your architectural design, foster collaboration, and streamline your processes. Let’s delve into the key areas this part covers:

  • Design Guidelines: Discover the art of shaping architectural solutions through structured design guidelines. Learn how to create cohesive, effective, and aesthetically pleasing designs that embody architectural excellence.

  • Source Code Organization: Uncover strategies for maintaining a well-organized source code that mirrors the intricacies of your architectural vision. Efficient source code organization fosters clarity, modularity, and ease of collaboration.

  • Diagrams Styling: Dive into the world of diagrams styling, where visual aesthetics meet informative clarity. Explore techniques to create diagrams that are not only visually appealing but also convey complex architectural concepts with precision.

  • Label Formatting: Master the art of label formatting, transforming textual elements into powerful conveyors of architectural information. Learn how to structure labels to enhance comprehension and create meaningful visual cues.

  • Viewpoint Utilization: Harness the full potential of viewpoints to construct insightful architectural narratives. Explore the strategic utilization of viewpoints to effectively communicate various aspects of your architecture to different stakeholders.

  • CI/CD Integration: Seamlessly integrate ArchiCode into your Continuous Integration/Continuous Deployment (CI/CD) pipeline. Learn how to make architectural design an integral part of your development lifecycle, ensuring consistent and aligned practices.

Through these best practices, you’ll unlock the tools and techniques that enable you to transform architectural concepts into tangible realities. Join us as we journey through each facet, unraveling the strategies that contribute to architectural excellence, effective communication, and the harmonious integration of ArchiCode into your architectural process.

Design Guidelines

Incremental and In-Depth Design Approach

ArchiCode advocates an architectural design philosophy characterized by incremental refinement and in-depth exploration. This approach empowers architects to craft well-structured and comprehensive designs that capture the essence of architectural vision. Let’s delve into the tenets of this methodology:

  • Iterative Refinement: In the journey of architectural creation, ArchiCode encourages an iterative process. The model evolves incrementally, starting from the highest levels of abstraction and cascading down to the minutiae of detail. This iterative refinement ensures a nuanced and coherent architectural representation.

  • Layered Depth: With each iteration, the model’s depth is progressively enriched. As elements are introduced, relationships between them are also refined, layer by layer. This gradual deepening ensures that architectural intricacies are meticulously captured, leading to a holistic and accurate depiction.

  • Relationship Refinement: Just as elements undergo iterative enhancements, relationships between them also follow the same iterative path. The relationships are fine-tuned and perfected, aligning with the evolving architectural narrative.

  • Clarity in Relationship Hierarchy: ArchiCode empowers architects to prune relationships for clarity. In instances where higher-level relationships are encompassed by those of lower levels, these higher-level relationships can be refined or omitted, ensuring that the architectural landscape remains lucid and uncluttered.

  • Synthetic Relationship Generation: A distinctive feature of ArchiCode’s built-in Viewpoints lies in their ability to generate synthetic relationships. This intentional design prevents sibling relationships from infiltrating the views, preserving the purity and coherence of each view’s focus.

By embracing ArchiCode’s incremental and in-depth design approach, architects embark on a transformative journey of iterative refinement. This methodology ensures that architectural designs are not only comprehensive but also well-structured, intelligible, and unburdened by unnecessary clutter.

Example: An ecommerce platform

Introduction

In the realm of software architecture, ArchiCode stands as a versatile tool that facilitates the systematic design and refinement of intricate systems. In this example, we embark on an iterative journey to architect an e-commerce platform, revealing how ArchiCode empowers architects to create coherent and well-defined architectural models. We commence by identifying high-level elements and relationships, progressively breaking down the system into specialized containers and interactions. Notably, while the outlined steps establish a structured foundation, ArchiCode’s true strength lies in its adaptability, enabling architects to customize the process to fit the unique demands of their projects. As we delve into this exploration, ArchiCode emerges as a dynamic instrument, empowering architects to craft intricate architectural designs that seamlessly connect vision with implementation.

Step #1: Shape the highest elements of the application layer

In this initial step, we identify the foundational elements of the application layer:

  • EcommercePlatform: At the core of the system, "EcommercePlatform" is identified as the solution itself, representing the entire e-commerce platform.

  • User: This element represents the users who interact with the e-commerce platform, browse products, and make purchases.

  • SocialIdentityProvider: External services such as Google and Microsoft are designated as "SocialIdentityProvider," responsible for authenticating users.

  • PaymentGateway: The element symbolizes external payment processing services like Stripe and Braintree that facilitate secure financial transactions.

Step #1 - The addition of the elements in .archicode.yaml
application:
  elements:
    - kind: "person"
      id: "User"
    - kind: "solution"
      id: "EcommercePlatform"
    - kind: "system"
      id: "SocialIdentityProvider"
    - kind: "system"
      id: "PaymentGateway"

Once the elements are identified, we design the relationships between them:

  • User uses EcommercePlatform: A direct relationship is established from the "User" element to the "EcommercePlatform" element. This relationship signifies that users engage with the platform to explore products, make purchases, and manage their accounts.

  • EcommercePlatform leverages SocialIdentityProvider: A relationship is created from "EcommercePlatform" to "SocialIdentityProvider" to depict the platform’s reliance on external authentication services. This connection ensures a seamless and secure login process for users through trusted identity providers.

  • EcommercePlatform leverages PaymentGateway: Another relationship is formed from "EcommercePlatform" to "PaymentGateway" to illustrate the platform’s integration with external payment processing services. Through this relationship, the platform enables customers to complete transactions using various payment methods.

Step #1 - The addition of the relationships in .archicode.yaml
application:
  elements:
    - kind: "person"
      id: "User"
    - kind: "solution"
      id: "EcommercePlatform"
    - kind: "system"
      id: "SocialIdentityProvider"
    - kind: "system"
      id: "PaymentGateway"

After establishing the relationships, the next task involves generating a first generation of views:

  • Overview Viewpoint: At this level of abstraction, the "Overview" viewpoint is the most suitable. This viewpoint provides a broad perspective of the relationships and interactions related to the e-commerce platform.

  • Ingress and Egress Relationships: The viewpoint showcases the "Ingress" relationships, highlighting how elements such as "User" enter the platform. Additionally, it depicts "Egress" relationships, illustrating how the platform interact with dependencies.

Step #1 - Generate the "Overview" views
archicode views generate --viewpoint=overview

The command generates four Plantuml files in the ./views/plantuml/application folder:

  • EcommercePlatform_overview.puml

  • PaymentGateway_overview.puml

  • SocialIdentityProvider_overview.puml

  • User_overview.puml

The view EcommercePlatform_overview.puml is built upon the "Overview" viewpoint and focus on the Ingress and Egress relationships where the EcommercePlatform element is involved.

Step #1 - The overview of the ecommerce platform

Failed to generate image: Could not load PlantUML. Either require 'asciidoctor-diagram-plantuml' or specify the location of the PlantUML JAR(s) using the 'DIAGRAM_PLANTUML_CLASSPATH' environment variable. Alternatively a PlantUML binary can be provided (plantuml-native in $PATH).
